# The World in 1914 and the Outbreak of the First World War - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It begins with the single hardest question in modern world history - not what happened in the summer of 1914, which is well recorded and not seriously disputed, but why a murder in a provincial Balkan capital produced a general European war within five weeks and a world war within months. Between those two observations it teaches the international order that was standing in 1914: a Europe that still took most of the decisions shaping the rest of the planet, with Germany the leading continental military and economic power, Britain the naval power with the global empire, France still marked by the loss of Alsace-Lorraine, Russia expanding and insecure, Austria-Hungary a multi-national empire that experienced every nationality question as a survival question, and two powers outside Europe - the United States and Japan - that had already changed the global balance without yet changing the habits of European statecraft. It teaches the political systems of those powers precisely, because the difference between a state in which a parliament has an important say and a state in which a parliament may be ignored is part of the explanation of how the July decisions were taken and by how few people. It teaches the imperial expansion after 1880 and the residue it left, and it teaches, against the popular version, why colonial rivalry is a weak direct cause and naval rivalry is a strong one. It teaches the alliance system as something that was built step by step between 1882 and 1907 and that did not function as an automatic trigger, and it enforces that finding with the recorded cases in which allies did not help each other. It teaches militarism and the arms competition as measurable things - the Navy Law of 1897, the Dreadnought of 1906, the counts at the end of 1911, the German reduction of naval spending in early 1913, the Russian rebuilding after 1908 and the Reichstag's refusal of the taxes the German army wanted. It teaches war plans as the operational layer of the crisis, including the plan attributed to Schlieffen, the railway-timetable argument built on it, and the scholarly challenge which says the plan as usually described never existed in that form. It teaches nationalism and the Balkan state system in full: the Treaty of Berlin of 1878, the Serbian dynastic change of 1903, the Greater Serbia and South Slav programme, the nationalities of the Habsburg Empire and the preventive-war current that programme produced in Vienna. It teaches the decline of the Ottoman Empire and the Eastern Question as the structural condition without which the Balkan crises are unintelligible, including the Straits and the Russian trade route that ran through them. It teaches the domestic politics and the social and economic setting of the decision-making states, because the claim that governments wanted a foreign success for internal reasons is a real historical argument and must be handled as an argument rather than as a slogan. It teaches the crises one by one - Morocco in 1905-06, the Anglo-Russian agreement of 1907, Bosnia in 1908, Agadir in 1911, the First and Second Balkan Wars of 1912-13 - and it teaches each of them twice: once as an event and once as a rehearsal that did not produce a general war, because that record is the evidence against inevitability. It teaches Sarajevo on 28 June 1914 with the individuals named and the organisations identified, and it refuses collective attribution in every direction. It teaches the July Crisis as a chain of datable decisions taken by identifiable people - the unconditional assurance given in Berlin, the ultimatum, the Serbian reply, the declaration of war on Serbia, the Russian mobilisation and the dispute about its stages, the German ultimatums, the declarations of 1 and 3 August, the demand on Belgium, the British decision of 4 August and the Austro-Hungarian declaration on Russia of 6 August. It teaches the difference between a structural cause and a proximate trigger and insists on both. It teaches the historiography with the scholars named and their claims stated accurately, including the positions that disagree with each other. It teaches agency, misperception and contingency, and the counterfactuals that the evidence permits. It teaches why the crisis became general and then global, including the colonial and non-European dimensions that made a European war a world war. And it teaches the responsibility debate as a graded question with two levels - the people who decided and the system that made their decisions dangerous - and never as collective blame laid on a nation or a people. The war's military course, its total-war consequences and the peace settlements belong to the next owner; this session ends at the moment general war begins.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is deliberately not the order of the standard narrative. A narrative account begins with the alliances, adds the arms race, lists the crises, reaches Sarajevo and concludes that the war was bound to happen - and that structure produces the commonest failure in the examination hall, which is a script that has described a system and has not explained a decision. Here the world of 1914 is described before any cause is weighed, because an answer that cannot say what kind of states these were cannot explain how few people took the decisions. The alliances are taught before the crises, because the crises are tests of the alliances. The arms race and the war plans are taught next, because they set the clock that the July diplomats were racing. Nationalism and the Balkan state system are taught before the Balkan crises, and the Ottoman decline is taught before both, because the Balkan crises are all, in the end, arguments about what should replace a retreating empire. The crises are then taught one by one, and each is scored twice - for what it changed and for the fact that it did not produce a general war. Sarajevo, the July Crisis and the escalation follow. Only after all of that does the session turn to causation, historiography, contingency and responsibility, because those are judgements, and a judgement reached before the evidence is a prejudice.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the single habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the subject's master chronology, ages-and-eras revision chart, syllabus mapping and folder README, the one core world-history book read for this session, or a verified official question ledger | Assert it. Dates, names, instruments, recorded sequences and recorded scholarly positions can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

One further habit belongs with those labels, and it is a teaching discipline rather than a caveat. Where a standard connecting name, date or instrument is needed to hold a sequence together, this session supplies it instead of retreating into vague formulations such as "an ultimatum was sent", "the powers mobilised" or "a guarantee existed". Every such item is verified, and its provenance is recorded in the final ledger rather than repeated in the teaching. Two points in this topic are genuinely disputed inside the evidence itself - the staging of the Russian mobilisation and the date of the entry into Belgium - and in both cases the disagreement is displayed rather than resolved by preference.

Six disciplines are specific to this topic, and each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where a fluent but wrong answer is unusually easy to write.

**The trigger discipline.** The assassination at Sarajevo is the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia. It is not the cause of the First World War, and it is not a sufficient explanation of anything beyond 28 July 1914. ✅ The source states the point directly: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak of the war but not why it became a world war. Every lesson in this session keeps the two questions apart, and the session refuses the formulation "the war was caused by the assassination" in every context, including revision notes.

**The anti-determinism discipline.** This session does not use the shorthand in which a diagram of two blocs is allowed to stand as an explanation. ✅ **Fact:** there had been many crises since 1904 and none of them had led to a major war, and nothing was binding about these alliances; four separate occasions are recorded on which an ally did not help its partner. ⚠️ A structure that produced containment five times and war once cannot be the whole explanation of the sixth outcome. Alliances are therefore taught as a framework that raised the cost of restraint, not as a mechanism that removed choice. The words "inevitable" and "bound to happen" are used in this session only where a named scholar's position is being reported, and they are always accompanied by the record of the position that rejects them.

**The attribution discipline.** Responsibility is stated about people, governments, cabinets, general staffs and named organisations, never about nations or peoples. This is not politeness; it is accuracy, and it is also the difference between a script that can answer "who was responsible" and a script that can only gesture at it. The men who took the July decisions were few and are named. ⚠️ Collective formulations - "the Germans wanted war", "the Serbs planned it", "Austria was aggressive by nature" - are treated in this session as errors of fact, and the remediation set includes them.

**The evidentiary discipline for quantitative evidence.** This evidence base holds very few measured quantities for this topic, and this session uses only those it can source. ✅ The counts of the new capital ships at the end of 1911 are held and are used. ✅ The approximate Serb population of Bosnia recorded by the source is held and is used. Beyond those and the handful listed in the final ledger, no army size, no mobilised strength, no casualty figure, no military-spending series, no trade or output figure and no population total appears anywhere in this session, because none is sourced here. Where the standard literature has such series, the session says so and does not supply a number. ⚠️ This discipline governs quantitative historical evidence about capacity, loss and wealth; it does not govern ordinary numbers of date, interval, sequence or examination format, which are used freely throughout.

**The two-level discipline for responsibility.** ✅ The source records the rule explicitly: a question asking who was responsible must be answered on at least two levels - the immediate decision-makers and the wider structural setting. This session teaches both levels separately so that they can be combined under time pressure, and it teaches the recorded disagreement between scholars who place the weight in different places rather than presenting one of them as settled.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics own material this one constantly touches. The Congress of Vienna, the Concert of Europe and the nineteenth-century congress system belong to the Vienna owner. The unification of Germany and Italy, the Franco-Prussian War and the transfer of Alsace-Lorraine belong to the unification owner; this session uses only the recorded consequence that the French resented the loss. New Imperialism and the partition of Africa belong to their owner; this session uses the recorded fact of the partition and the specific frictions that the partition left behind, and narrates no African campaign. The military course of the war from the Marne onward, the entry of later belligerents, total war, the collapse of four empires and the settlements of 1919-23 belong to the First World War and aftermath owner; this session stops at the point where general war begins and hands over. The Russian Revolution belongs to its own owner and is named here only as a warning recorded before the war by a Russian minister.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Dependency-led question | Stage |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What exactly is being explained - an assassination, a European war, or a world war? | Foundation |
| 2 | What kind of world was it in 1914, and what kind of states were taking the decisions? | Foundation |
| 3 | What did the imperial expansion after 1880 leave behind, and how much of it actually mattered? | Foundation |
| 4 | How did Europe acquire two alliance blocs, and what did those blocs actually oblige anyone to do? | Core |
| 5 | Militarism and the arms competition: what was being built, by whom, and was it still a live cause in 1914? | Core |
| 6 | War plans: how did an operational timetable become a political constraint - and did that plan exist as described? | Core |
| 7 | Nationalism and the Balkan state system: why was a small kingdom a survival question for a great empire? | Core |
| 8 | The Eastern Question: what happens to a state system when a large empire retreats from it? | Core |
| 9 | Did domestic politics and economic competition push governments towards war? | Core |
| 10 | Morocco twice, and the agreement in between: what did 1905-06, 1907 and 1911 actually change? | Core |
| 11 | Bosnia 1908: the crisis that set the terms of 1914 | Core |
| 12 | The Balkan Wars of 1912-13: the last rehearsal, and why it was contained | Core |
| 13 | Sarajevo, 28 June 1914: who did what, and what may be attributed to whom | Core |
| 14 | The July Crisis, first movement: the assurance from Berlin, the ultimatum and the declaration on Serbia | Core |
| 15 | The July Crisis, second movement: mobilisation, the declarations, Belgium and Britain | Core |
| 16 | Sorting the causes: structural, regional, great-power, operational and proximate | Advanced |
| 17 | Who was to blame? The historiography, stated accurately | Advanced |
| 18 | Agency, misperception and contingency: was it inevitable, and what would have had to be different? | Advanced |
| 19 | Why a Balkan quarrel became a general war, and why a European war became a world war | Advanced |
| 20 | How is all of this converted into marks? | Synthesis |

*Read the diagram downward once for sequence and then across once for argument. The horizontal pairings are where the marks are. The left-hand column alone produces a determinist essay in which nobody chooses anything; the middle column alone produces a diary of July in which nothing is explained; the right-hand column alone produces a technical account of railways. The topic is the relation between them, and the examiner's question is almost always a question about that relation: how much of the outcome belongs to the system, how much to the men, and how much to the machinery.*

**Scope of this session.** The Congress of Vienna and the Concert of Europe belong to the Vienna owner. German and Italian unification, the Franco-Prussian War and the creation of the Alsace-Lorraine grievance belong to the unification owner; only the recorded French resentment is used here. New Imperialism and the partition of Africa belong to their owner; this session uses the recorded partition and the specific frictions it left. The course of the war, its total-war character, the collapse of the German, Austro-Hungarian, Ottoman and Russian empires, and the settlements of 1919-23 belong to the First World War and aftermath owner. India's wartime politics belong to the Modern Indian History owners and appear here in one bounded sentence in Lesson 19 and nowhere else.

## Lesson 1 - What is actually being explained: an assassination, a European war, or a world war?

Progress: 1/20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the three separate questions hidden inside one topic title; trigger, cause and condition distinguished; the recorded limit of the Austro-Serb explanation; what the syllabus clause actually owns; the boundary with the war itself; the five formulations that lose marks before the first paragraph ends

### Visual: one title, three questions, three different answers

   | with Japan is inside the system before the crisis begins      |
   | THIS IS THE GLOBALISATION QUESTION                            |

   QUESTION 2 IS THE COMMONEST FAILURE IN THIS TOPIC.

*The diagram is the reason "the First World War was caused by the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand" is not a compressed answer but a wrong one. It is a correct answer to Question 1 and no answer at all to Questions 2 and 3. ✅ The source draws the same line in one sentence: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak of the war, but not why it became a world war.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Put yourself in London or Paris in the last week of June 1914. A royal heir has been murdered in a Balkan province. This is shocking, but it is not obviously your business. The Balkans had produced a crisis in 1908 and two wars in 1912 and 1913, and none of them had drawn you in. Your newspapers are more interested in domestic politics. Five weeks later your country is at war with Germany.

The historian's problem, and the examiner's, is the gap between those two sentences. Something happened in those five weeks that had not happened in the previous five crises. If you say "the alliances", you have to explain why the same alliances produced containment in 1908 and 1911. If you say "the assassination", you have to explain why an assassination in Sarajevo obliges a British cabinet to send an army to France. If you say "Germany planned it", you have to say when the plan was made and show the decision. Each of those answers is a real historical position held by serious scholars, and each of them is contested by other serious scholars. That is why this topic is examined as an argument and not as a chronology.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the single most useful move you can make at the start of any answer on this topic is to say which of the three questions you are answering. It costs one sentence and it organises everything after it.

### 2. Trigger, cause and condition: three words that are not synonyms

This distinction is the working equipment of the whole session, so it is defined once, precisely, and then used everywhere.

| Term | What it means | In this topic | The error it prevents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Background condition** | A long-standing feature of the system that makes certain outcomes possible without making any of them likely on a given day | European primacy; empire; a multi-national Habsburg state; Ottoman retreat | Treating a permanent feature as an explanation of a dated event |
| **Structural cause** | A feature of the system that raises the probability of the outcome and shapes its form | Alliance blocs; arms competition; mobilisation planning; Balkan nationalism | Confusing what made war possible with what made this war happen |
| **Precipitant or trigger** | The specific event that starts the sequence | ✅ The assassination at Sarajevo on 28 June 1914 | Treating the trigger as the cause |
| **Decision** | A choice made by identifiable people who could have chosen otherwise | ✅ The unconditional German assurance; the Austrian ultimatum; the Russian mobilisation; Moltke's insistence on the full plan; the British decision of 4 August | Writing a determinist essay in which nobody decides anything |
| **Mechanism** | The process by which a local event is transmitted into a general outcome | Mobilisation timetables; the route through Belgium; alliance signalling | Describing a cause and never explaining the transmission |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these five are not five items of the same kind, and an answer that lists them side by side as "the causes of the First World War" has not explained anything. The relationship between them is the answer: conditions made the system fragile, structures made a break dangerous, a trigger supplied the occasion, decisions turned the occasion into war, and a mechanism turned war between two states into war between six.

### 3. What the source says about the limit of the Austro-Serb explanation

✅ **Fact:** the source is unusually direct about this. It states that the point beyond dispute is that the quarrel between Austria-Hungary and Serbia sparked off the outbreak of war; that the quarrel had become increasingly explosive since 1908; and that the Austrians seized on the assassination as the excuse for a preventive war with Serbia. ✅ It then states the limit in a single sentence: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak of the war, but not why it became a world war.

✅ **Fact:** it also records that the Austrians genuinely felt that if Serb and Slav nationalist ambitions for a state of Yugoslavia were achieved, it would cause the collapse of the Habsburg Empire, and that Serbia must therefore be curbed; and, in fairness to them, that they probably hoped the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last clause is worth more marks than its length suggests. If Vienna expected a localised war, then Vienna's decision does not explain the general war, and the general war has to be explained by what happened after 28 July. This is the hinge on which the whole causation section of this session turns.

### 4. What the syllabus actually owns here

✅ **Fact:** the subject's syllabus mapping quotes the official General Studies Paper I clause verbatim: "History of the world will include events from 18th century such as industrial revolution, world wars, redrawal of national boundaries, colonization, decolonization, political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc.- their forms and effect on the society." ✅ Within that clause the sub-clause "world wars" is separately mapped, and the outbreak of the First World War is one of the sections that answers it.

✅ **Fact:** the same mapping records that no separate World History clause appears in the supplied verbatim Prelims syllabus, and that the folder's direct official ownership is Mains Paper I only.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two consequences follow, and both are practical. First, the examinable object is not the war but the outbreak and its causation - the clause says "world wars ... their forms and effect on the society", and the form of this war and its social effects belong to the next owner. Second, because the topic is owned by an essay-type paper and not by an objective paper, the preparation target is an argument that can be produced in 150 or 250 words, not a stock of retrievable dates. The dates exist in this session to support the argument, not to be recited.

### 5. Where this topic stops

✅ **Fact:** the source states the boundary in its own words: the unification owner holds national unification and the creation of the Alsace-Lorraine question, the New Imperialism owner holds the partition of Africa, and the First World War and aftermath owner holds the war's military course, total-war consequences and the settlements of 1919-23. It adds the operative sentence: **this topic ends when general war begins.**

| Material | Owner | What this session may use |
|---|---|---|
| The Concert of Europe and the congress system | The Vienna owner | Nothing narrated here; the Concert's earlier success is referred to only as a contrast in Lesson 18 |
| German and Italian unification; the Franco-Prussian War; the transfer of Alsace-Lorraine | The unification owner | ✅ Only the recorded French resentment at the loss of Alsace-Lorraine to Germany at the end of the Franco-Prussian War of 1871 |
| The partition of Africa and the Scramble | The New Imperialism owner | ✅ Only the recorded fact of the partition after 1880 and the specific frictions it left behind |
| The Western and Eastern Fronts; trench warfare; total war; the collapse of four empires; Versailles and the League | The First World War and aftermath owner | Nothing; this session stops at 4-6 August 1914 |
| The Russian Revolution of 1917 | The Russian Revolution owner | ✅ Only Durnovo's recorded pre-war warning that a long war could lead to the collapse of the tsarist regime |
| India in the war; the Home Rule agitation; the Lucknow Pact | Modern Indian History owners | One bounded sentence in Lesson 19 establishing that the war globalised a European conflict |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the boundary is not bureaucratic. A script that begins describing the Marne or the Treaty of Versailles in an outbreak question is not showing range; it is answering a different question and losing the marks allotted to this one.

### 6. The five formulations that lose marks before the first paragraph ends

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each of these is common, each sounds confident, and each is refuted by evidence recorded in this session's own sources.

| Formulation | Why it fails | What to write instead |
|---|---|---|
| "The assassination caused the First World War" | Answers Question 1 and abandons Questions 2 and 3 | "The assassination was the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration on Serbia; the general war requires a separate explanation" |
| "The alliance system made war inevitable" | ✅ Contradicted by the record that many crises since 1904 produced no major war and that nothing was binding about these alliances | "The alliances did not oblige anyone to fight; they made restraint look like weakness, which is a different and weaker claim" |
| "Germany was responsible" (stated flatly) | Reports one contested position as settled and ignores the positions that dispute it | "A substantial body of scholarship places ultimate responsibility on the German leadership; that position is contested, and the contest is part of the answer" |
| "All the powers were equally to blame" | Dissolves the question and matches no scholar's actual position | "Responsibility is graded, not equal, and it is distributed differently at the decision level and at the system level" |
| "Europe sleepwalked into war" (used as the whole answer) | Turns a metaphor into an explanation and erases the decisions that are documented | Use contingency and miscalculation as arguments with named evidence, not as a mood |

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the verified question owned by this topic is the 2024 General Studies Paper I item on whether the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of the balance of power, recorded in the routing ledger at 15 marks and 250 words. It is treated in full in Lesson 20 and in the final verified-question section; it is named here because it determines how Lesson 1 should be used.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 2024 demand is a "how far" demand, and a "how far" demand is a graded-verdict demand. The three-question separation taught in this lesson is the fastest available route into it, because balance of power is a strong answer to Question 2 and a weak answer to Question 1. That asymmetry is the argument, and it can be set up in the opening two sentences of the answer.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Account for the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war in 1914?
> ⚠️ "Sarajevo was the occasion, not the cause." Examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** In any of these, spend the first two sentences establishing which question you are answering and the trigger-cause distinction. ⚠️ Examiners reward that opening because it demonstrates that the candidate has understood the analytical shape of the topic rather than memorised its chronology.

### Traps

- Writing "the immediate cause of the First World War was the assassination". ✅ The recorded formulation is that it was the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia.
- Treating "Balkan crisis becomes world war" and "causes of the war" as the same question. They take different evidence.
- Opening with 1871 or with the Congress of Vienna in a question about 1914. Background is not an opening.
- Using the word "inevitable" in your own voice.
- Carrying the war's course or the peace settlement into an outbreak answer.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three questions inside one title: the Austro-Serb outbreak; the European escalation; the global extension.
2. ✅ The recorded limit: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak but not why it became a world war.
3. ✅ The quarrel had become increasingly explosive since 1908; the Austrians seized on the assassination as the excuse for a preventive war with Serbia.
4. ✅ Vienna probably hoped the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars - which is why 28 July does not explain August.
5. ⚠️ Five kinds of cause, not one list: background condition, structural cause, precipitant, decision, mechanism.
6. ✅ Syllabus ownership: General Studies Paper I, the "world wars" sub-clause; no separate Prelims clause.
7. ✅ Boundary: this topic ends when general war begins.
8. ⚠️ Five prohibited formulations, each refuted by the session's own sources.
9. ✅ The owned verified question is the 2024 General Studies Paper I balance-of-power item, 15 marks, 250 words.
10. ⚠️ Opening architecture: name the question, state the trigger-cause distinction, promise a graded verdict. Three sentences.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script opens: "The First World War was caused by the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand at Sarajevo on 28 June 1914, which activated the alliance system and made war inevitable." Identify the three separate errors and rewrite the sentence as an opening that would earn marks.

**Model answer:** The first error is the conflation of trigger and cause: the assassination is the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia on 28 July, and the source explicitly states that the Austro-Serb quarrel does not explain why the conflict became a world war. The second error is the word "activated": the alliances were not automatic, and the recorded evidence is that many crises since 1904 produced no general war, that nothing was binding about these alliances, that France sent no help to Russia against Japan and gave none over Bosnia, that Austria-Hungary took no interest in Germany's Moroccan setbacks, that Germany restrained Austria-Hungary during the Second Balkan War, and that Italy, a member of the Triple Alliance, entered the war against Germany in 1915. The third error is "inevitable", which converts a probability into a certainty and makes the July decisions unnecessary to explain - yet those decisions are the only thing that distinguishes 1914 from 1908 and 1911. A marks-earning opening would read: the assassination at Sarajevo was the precipitant of an Austro-Serb war that Vienna expected to localise; the conversion of that local war into a general European war was the work of specific decisions taken in Berlin, St Petersburg, Vienna and London between 5 July and 4 August, transmitted through mobilisation timetables and the Belgian route; and the global extension followed from the fact that the belligerents were empires. The system made war possible and dangerous; it did not make it happen.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the distinction between trigger and cause is a pedantic refinement to be added at the end if there is time. It is the organising principle of the answer. A script that never makes it cannot answer a "how far" or "to what extent" question at all, because every such question is asking the candidate to grade one kind of cause against another.

*The next lesson supplies what this one assumed. It has been said that five great powers and Serbia went to war while Italy, a member of one of the two blocs, stayed out; that their decisions were taken by very few people; and that two powers outside Europe had already changed the world balance. None of that has yet been described. Before any cause can be weighed, the world of 1914 itself - who was strong, who was afraid, and what kind of government each of them had - has to be put on the table.*

## Lesson 2 - The world in 1914: power, wealth and the kind of states that took the decisions

Progress: 2/20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: European primacy in world decision-making; the industrial ranking and the position of Germany, Britain, France, Belgium, Italy and Austria-Hungary; Russian industrial expansion from a backward base; the United States as a world power; Japan after 1904-05; the three-way classification of political systems; the constitutional position of the German Reichstag, Chancellor and Kaiser; the limited franchises of Italy and Japan; autocracy in Russia and Austria-Hungary; why regime type is part of the causation argument

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - European dominance in 1914; the comparative industrial position of the powers; the most spectacular industrial progress of the previous forty years outside Europe; and the varying political systems of the world powers with their exact constitutional descriptions
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 13
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two rankings that do not match

```text
   RANKING A: WHO TAKES THE WORLD'S DECISIONS IN 1914
   ---------------------------------------------------
   MOST DECISIONS SHAPING THE FATE OF THE WORLD ARE TAKEN
   IN THE CAPITALS OF EUROPE.
        Berlin | London | Paris | St Petersburg | Vienna | Rome

   RANKING B: WHO PRODUCES THE INDUSTRIAL MATERIAL OF POWER
   ---------------------------------------------------
   UNITED STATES   coal, pig-iron and steel: more than either
                   Germany or Britain -> now ranks as a world power
   GERMANY         leading power IN EUROPE, militarily and
                   economically; has overtaken Britain in pig-iron
                   and steel, though not quite in coal
   BRITAIN         still ahead in coal; the great naval power
   FRANCE / BELGIUM / ITALY / AUSTRIA-HUNGARY   well behind
   RUSSIA          expanding rapidly, but from so backward a base
                   that she cannot seriously challenge Germany
                   or Britain
   JAPAN           modernised rapidly; a power to be reckoned with
                   after defeating Russia in 1904-05

   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE MISMATCH IS THE POINT. THE STATES THAT DECIDE ARE NOT
   THE STATES THAT PRODUCE. THE ONE POWER THAT LEADS RANKING B
   OUTRIGHT SITS OUTSIDE RANKING A ALTOGETHER IN JULY 1914.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The diagram explains a fact that otherwise looks strange: a crisis that would decide the fate of the world in 1914 was managed entirely inside Europe, with no participation by the largest industrial economy on earth. ⚠️ That is not because the United States was weak. It is because the habits of the system had not yet caught up with the distribution of capacity - and the war itself is what forced them to.*

### 1. European primacy, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the source opens its survey of the world in 1914 with the statement that Europe still dominated the rest of the world, and that most of the decisions which shaped the fate of the world were taken in the capitals of Europe.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word "still" is doing analytical work. It concedes that this primacy was a residue rather than a permanent condition, and the same account closes the chapter by recording that Europe never quite regained its dominant position in the world after the war. ⚠️ For an answer, that gives you a frame with a beginning and an end: 1914 is the last year in which a European quarrel could be treated as automatically the world's quarrel, and it is the last year in which that treatment was correct.

### 2. The industrial and economic ranking

✅ **Fact:** the source records the position power by power.

| Power | ✅ Recorded position in 1914 | ⚠️ What it implies for the crisis |
|---|---|---|
| Germany | The leading power in Europe both militarily and economically; had overtaken Britain in the production of pig-iron and steel, though not quite in coal | A state confident of its present strength and anxious about its future position - the combination that makes preventive reasoning attractive |
| Britain | Still ahead in coal; separately recorded as the world's greatest sea power | Strength located in the navy and the empire rather than in a continental army, which shapes both its deterrent value and its slowness to commit |
| France, Belgium, Italy, Austria-Hungary | Well behind Germany and Britain | Austria-Hungary in particular is a great power by status and by army, not by industrial weight - which is part of why it feared a small neighbour |
| Russia | Industry expanding rapidly, but so backward to begin with that she could not seriously challenge Germany and Britain | Rapid growth from a low base is exactly the profile that frightens a neighbour: it promises future strength without delivering present security |
| United States | In 1914 produced more coal, pig-iron and steel than either Germany or Britain, and now ranked as a world power | The decisive reserve of the war, and the reason a long war could not be won by the side with the weaker access to the Atlantic |
| Japan | Had modernised rapidly and was a power to be reckoned with after her defeat of Russia in the Russo-Japanese War of 1904-05 | Already inside the European alliance structure through the 1902 agreement with Britain, which is one route by which a European war became a world war |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three of these rows are causal and not merely descriptive. Germany's combination of present superiority and expected relative decline is the material basis of the preventive-war argument taught in Lesson 9 and Lesson 17. Russia's rapid growth from a backward base is the material basis of the German fear that time was running out. And Austria-Hungary's status as a great power without commensurate industrial weight is part of the explanation of why Vienna treated a Balkan kingdom as an existential problem rather than as a nuisance.

### 3. The political systems, classified precisely

This is the part of the 1914 survey that candidates most often skip, and it is the part that does the most work in a causation answer.

✅ **Fact:** the source divides the world powers into three groups.

| Group | ✅ States | ✅ Recorded constitutional position |
|---|---|---|
| Democratic forms of government | The United States, Britain, France | Each had a parliament consisting of representatives elected by the people, and these parliaments had an important say in running the country |
| Elected bodies with limited effective power | Germany, Italy, Japan | Germany had an elected lower house of parliament, the Reichstag, but real power lay with the Chancellor and the Kaiser; Italy was a monarchy with an elected parliament, but the franchise was limited to wealthy people; Japan had an elected lower house, but the franchise was restricted and the emperor and the privy council held most of the power |
| Autocratic rule | Russia, Austria-Hungary | The Tsar of Russia and the Emperor of Austria, who was also King of Hungary, were autocratic or absolute rulers; although parliaments existed, they could only advise, and the rulers could ignore them and do exactly as they wished |

✅ **Fact:** the source records the same three-way division in its own words: Britain, France and the USA had democratic institutions; Germany, Italy and Japan had elected bodies but limited effective democracy; Russia and Austria-Hungary remained far more autocratic.

### 4. Why the regime table belongs in a causation answer

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the classification is not decoration, and there are three distinct uses for it.

1. **It explains the size of the decision-making group.** In a state where a parliament may be ignored, a decision for war can be taken by a monarch, a chancellor or a foreign minister and a chief of staff. That is why the July Crisis can be narrated as a sequence of individual choices at all, and it is why the attribution discipline of this session is possible: the decisions have names attached because the constitutions allowed very few names to matter.
2. **It supplies the mechanism for the domestic-pressure argument.** The claim that a government wanted a foreign success in order to manage an internal problem is only plausible where the government faces a legislature it cannot control but need not obey. ✅ Germany is exactly that case: the elected lower house existed, the Chancellor did not depend on its confidence, and Lesson 9 shows the recorded 1914 instance in which he survived a vote of no confidence because the Kaiser had the final say.
3. **It disciplines the language of blame.** ⚠️ If real power lay with a Chancellor and a Kaiser, then "Germany decided" is a shorthand for a decision taken by two or three men, and an answer that writes "the German people wanted war" is asserting something the constitutional evidence does not support and that no historian in this evidence base claims.

### 5. Comparison: two great powers, two different kinds of fear

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Austria-Hungary and Germany are routinely bracketed together as the Central Powers, and the bracket hides the most important difference in the topic.

| Axis | Austria-Hungary | Germany |
|---|---|---|
| Nature of the threat perceived | Internal: ✅ a multi-national empire containing Germans, Hungarians, Magyars, Czechs, Slovaks, Italians, Poles, Romanians, Ruthenians and Slovenes as well as Serbs and Croats, in which one successful secession would invite the rest | External: ✅ encirclement by Britain, France and Russia, and the expectation that Russian strength would grow |
| Time horizon | Indefinite; the nationality problem had no terminal date | Short and specific; the fear was of a closing window |
| What war was expected to achieve | ✅ Curbing Serbia so that the South Slav programme could not break up the empire | ✅ On the preventive reading, defeating enemies before they became too powerful |
| Expected scope of war | ✅ Localised, like the Balkan Wars | Continental from the outset, because the operational plan began with an attack on France |
| Constitutional decision-maker | An autocratic emperor and his ministers | A Chancellor and a Kaiser, with a general staff whose technical authority Lesson 6 examines |

⚠️ The comparison is the reply to the examiner's favourite follow-up: if the Central Powers acted together, why did they want different things? Because one was fighting for the survival of a state form and the other was fighting about the future distribution of power. They cooperated; they did not share an objective.

### 6. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ A capable candidate may object that the regime classification proves too much: France and Britain were democracies and they went to war too, so regime type cannot explain the outbreak.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The objection would be decisive if the claim were that autocracy causes war. It is not. The claim is narrower and survives the objection: regime type explains *how* the decisions were taken and *by how many people*, and it supplies the mechanism for one specific argument about motive in one specific state. ✅ It is also consistent with the recorded sequence: the two democracies entered last, after the continental decisions had already been taken, and Britain's entry turned on a specific violation rather than on a prior commitment.

**The residual.** ⚠️ What the evidence base here does not settle is how far public opinion pressed governments in any of these states. ✅ It records one clear instance - that Russian policy after 1911 began to succumb to growing jingoistic public opinion demanding action to increase Russian prestige - and it records nothing comparable for the other powers. That asymmetry is recorded as an open uncertainty rather than filled by assumption.

### 7. UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this lesson supplies the opening paragraph of a 15- or 20-mark answer, and it supplies it in a form that is difficult to write without having read something. A paragraph that names the industrial ranking, the mismatch between decision-making and production, and the three-way regime classification demonstrates command of the 1914 system in about sixty words and buys the right to make analytical claims afterwards.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Describe the international order of 1914 and explain why it was unstable.
> ⚠️ "By 1914 the European states system no longer matched the world's distribution of power." Comment.
> ⚠️ To what extent did the internal political character of the great powers contribute to the outbreak of war in 1914?

**Answer-use guidance.** Use the mismatch between the two rankings as the thesis sentence of a system paragraph; use the regime table as the bridge into the decision paragraph. ⚠️ Do not reproduce the whole table in an answer - name three or four positions and move on.

### Traps

- Writing that Germany had overtaken Britain industrially without qualification. ✅ The recorded position is pig-iron and steel, though not quite in coal.
- Calling Russia industrially backward without the second half. ✅ Russian industry was expanding rapidly; it was the starting base that was backward.
- Treating the United States as absent from the world of 1914. ✅ It already produced more coal, pig-iron and steel than either Germany or Britain and ranked as a world power.
- Treating Japan as a spectator. ✅ It had defeated Russia in 1904-05 and had been allied to Britain since 1902.
- Describing Germany as a democracy because it had a Reichstag, or as a pure autocracy because it had a Kaiser. ✅ It had an elected lower house while real power lay with the Chancellor and the Kaiser.
- Supplying production figures, army sizes or budget shares. None is sourced in this session.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Europe still dominated the world in 1914; most decisions shaping the fate of the world were taken in European capitals.
2. ✅ Germany was the leading European power militarily and economically; had overtaken Britain in pig-iron and steel, not quite in coal.
3. ✅ France, Belgium, Italy and Austria-Hungary were well behind.
4. ✅ Russian industry was expanding rapidly from so backward a base that it could not seriously challenge Germany or Britain.
5. ✅ In 1914 the USA produced more coal, pig-iron and steel than either Germany or Britain and ranked as a world power.
6. ✅ Japan had modernised rapidly and was a power to be reckoned with after defeating Russia in 1904-05.
7. ✅ Democracies with parliaments having an important say: the USA, Britain, France.
8. ✅ Elected bodies with limited effective power: Germany (Reichstag, but real power with Chancellor and Kaiser), Italy (franchise limited to the wealthy), Japan (restricted franchise; emperor and privy council).
9. ✅ Autocracies: Russia and Austria-Hungary, where parliaments could only advise and rulers could ignore them.
10. ⚠️ Three uses of the regime table: it explains the size of the decision group, supplies the domestic-pressure mechanism, and disciplines the language of blame.
11. ⚠️ Austria-Hungary feared internal dissolution on an indefinite horizon; Germany feared external encirclement on a short one. Same alliance, different objectives.
12. ⚠️ The mismatch between the decision ranking and the production ranking is the single best one-line description of the 1914 system.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks whether the political character of the great powers helps explain the outbreak of war in 1914. Build the strongest version of the argument that it does, then state precisely where that argument stops.

**Model answer:** The argument that it helps runs in three steps. First, the constitutional facts are recorded and are not the same across the powers: the Tsar and the Emperor of Austria were autocratic rulers whose parliaments could only advise and could be ignored; Germany had an elected Reichstag but real power lay with the Chancellor and the Kaiser; Italy and Japan had elected lower houses on restricted franchises with power held elsewhere; only the United States, Britain and France had parliaments with an important say. Second, that distribution determines how decisions for war could be taken - by very small groups whose members can be named, without the delay, publicity or consent that a controlling legislature imposes, which is precisely why the July Crisis is narratable as a sequence of individual choices. Third, it supplies the mechanism for the domestic-pressure argument in the one state where the evidence supports it: in Germany a Chancellor who had lost a Reichstag vote of no confidence in January 1914 remained in office because the Kaiser had the final say, so a government facing an assertive legislature it did not depend on could contemplate a foreign success as an answer to an internal problem. The argument stops at three points. It does not show that autocracy causes war, since two parliamentary states also went to war. It does not establish motive, only opportunity and method, and motive must be argued separately from the recorded evidence about preventive reasoning and prestige. And it says almost nothing about public opinion, because the evidence base records public pressure clearly only for Russia after 1911 and not for the others.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the regime classification as background scenery to be recited before the real answer begins. It is load-bearing: without it, the claim that a handful of men decided for war in 1914 is an assertion, and the domestic-pressure argument has no mechanism. With it, both become evidenced claims.

*The next lesson takes up the item that the popular version of this topic puts first and that the evidence puts surprisingly low. Empire is everywhere in the 1914 world - the Scramble for Africa, the concessions forced on China, German disappointment with what its own expansion had produced. The question is how much of that actually helped cause the war, and the answer is not the one most scripts assume.*

## Lesson 3 - Imperial expansion after 1880, and how much of it actually caused the war

Progress: 3/20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: imperialism defined; the burst of expansion after 1880; the partition of Africa and its stated motives; the forcing of concessions on China and the collapse of the Manchu dynasty; German Weltpolitik and imperial disappointment; the recorded verdict that colonial rivalry is a weak direct cause; the July 1914 Anglo-German colonial agreement; the one side effect that did matter

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the imperial argument, weighed rather than asserted

```text
   THE POPULAR CHAIN                    WHAT THE EVIDENCE SUPPORTS
   -----------------                    --------------------------
   imperial rivalry                     imperial expansion after 1880
        |                                        |
        v                                        v
   colonies contested                   MOST OF AFRICA partitioned;
        |                               CHINA forced into concessions;
        v                               GERMANY gains Pacific islands
   the powers go to war                 and African territory - small
                                        and not very rewarding
                                                 |
                              +------------------+------------------+
                              |                                     |
                    DID DISPUTES LEAD TO WAR?              ONE SIDE EFFECT
                    NO - "they had always been             NAVAL RIVALRY
                    settled without war"                   -> did cause
                    and in EARLY JULY 1914                 dangerous friction
                    Anglo-German relations were            -> taught in
                    GOOD, with an agreement                Lesson 5
                    favourable to Germany just
                    reached on a possible partition
                    of Portuguese colonies in Africa
                              |
                              v
                    VERDICT: COLONIAL RIVALRY IS A BACKGROUND
                    CONDITION AND A SOURCE OF MOOD, NOT A
                    DIRECT CAUSE OF THE 1914 WAR
```

*The diagram is the reason imperialism must be handled carefully rather than enthusiastically. ⚠️ It is true that the powers of 1914 were empires, true that they competed, and true that this competition shaped how they saw each other. It is false that a colonial dispute produced the war, and the decisive piece of evidence is a date: in early July 1914, while the July Crisis was already beginning, Britain and Germany were on good terms over colonies.*

### 1. Why the imperial answer feels right and still fails

Most scripts want imperialism to be the deep cause, because it feels like the deep cause. The powers had spent thirty years grabbing territory; surely the grabbing is what set them against each other.

The difficulty is that the record does not cooperate. Every colonial dispute between the powers in those thirty years was settled without a general war - including two crises over Morocco that are usually presented as near-misses and that ended, in both cases, in a negotiated outcome. And the state of colonial relations between the two powers whose enmity mattered most, Britain and Germany, was better in July 1914 than it had been for years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest conclusion is a graded one. Imperialism is where the great powers learned the habit of measuring each other; it supplied the vocabulary of prestige, the sense of relative decline and the constituency for expansion. It also produced, as one specific side effect, the naval competition that did matter. What it did not do was supply the quarrel of 1914.

### 2. Imperialism defined, and the burst after 1880

✅ **Fact:** the source defines the term and dates the surge: the European powers had taken part in a great burst of imperialist expansion in the years after 1880, and imperialism is the building up of an empire by seizing territory overseas.

✅ **Fact:** it records the two principal theatres.

| Theatre | ✅ What happened | ✅ Recorded motive or result |
|---|---|---|
| Africa | Most of Africa was taken over by the European states in what became known as "the Scramble for Africa" | The idea behind it was mainly to get control of new markets and new sources of raw materials |
| China | There was intervention in the crumbling Chinese Empire; the European powers, the USA and Japan all, at different times, forced the helpless Chinese to grant trading concessions | Exasperation with the incompetence of their government caused the Chinese to overthrow the ancient Manchu dynasty and set up a republic in 1911 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the China row is worth keeping, and most scripts drop it. It is the evidence that the pre-war order was not simply a European order with colonies attached but a global system in which a large non-European state could be coerced by a coalition that included two non-European powers. ⚠️ It also links directly to the globalisation question of Lesson 19: a system in which the United States and Japan already act alongside European powers outside Europe is a system in which a European war will not stay European.

**Boundary note.** The mechanics of the partition of Africa, the conference that regulated it, the ideologies that justified it and its consequences for African societies belong to the New Imperialism owner and are not narrated here. ✅ This session uses only the recorded fact of the partition, its recorded motive and the specific frictions it left behind.

### 3. German Weltpolitik and imperial disappointment

✅ **Fact:** the source records this as one of the named causes of friction. The Germans accused Britain, Russia and France of trying to "encircle" them; they were also disappointed with the results of their expansionist policies, known as Weltpolitik - literally "world policy". Although they had taken possession of some islands in the Pacific and some territory in Africa, their empire was small in comparison with those of the other European powers, and not very rewarding economically.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a more interesting item than a simple grievance. Three things follow from it.

1. **Disappointment is not the same as deprivation.** Germany had an empire; it was small and unprofitable relative to expectation. The grievance is about status and return, not about survival, which is why it produces resentment rather than war.
2. **It connects imperial policy to naval policy.** ⚠️ A world policy that has not delivered a rewarding empire creates pressure to acquire the instrument that is believed to produce one - which is exactly the reasoning Lesson 5 examines in the naval race.
3. **It is a domestic as well as an external fact.** ⚠️ A policy publicly announced as world policy and privately assessed as disappointing is a political liability at home, and it belongs with the domestic material of Lesson 9.

### 4. The recorded verdict: colonial rivalry as a cause

✅ **Fact:** the source assesses this claim directly and rejects it as a direct cause. It records that the argument that German disappointment with their imperial gains and resentment at the success of other powers helped cause the war is not convincing; that although there had certainly been disputes, they had always been settled without war; and that in early July 1914 Anglo-German relations were good, an agreement favourable to Germany having just been reached over a possible partition of Portuguese colonies in Africa.

✅ **Fact:** it then names the exception: there was one side effect of colonial rivalry which did cause dangerous friction - naval rivalry.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the same position in its comparison table, listing colonial rivalry as present but less convincing as a direct cause, with the recommended use being to mention it and then qualify it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the early-July agreement is the single most efficient piece of evidence in this lesson, and it should be deployed rather than described. It is a dated fact that falls inside the crisis itself and that contradicts the colonial-rivalry thesis at exactly the moment the thesis needs to be true.

### 5. Comparison: three imperial arguments, three different verdicts

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "imperialism caused the war" is at least three separate claims, and they do not stand or fall together.

| Claim | What it asserts | Verdict on this evidence | How to use it |
|---|---|---|---|
| Colonial rivalry as direct cause | The powers fought because they were competing for territory overseas | ✅ Rejected: disputes were always settled without war, and Anglo-German colonial relations were good in early July 1914 | Mention, then qualify - and use the July 1914 agreement as the refutation |
| Imperial competition as source of habit and mood | Thirty years of measuring each other produced a politics of relative standing and prestige | ⚠️ Supported as a background condition, not as a cause of the July decisions | Use in the system paragraph, never in the decision paragraph |
| Naval rivalry as a side effect of imperial ambition | The pursuit of a world empire generated a fleet programme that threatened Britain directly | ✅ Recorded as the one side effect that did cause dangerous friction | Promote it, and then apply the 1913-14 qualification taught in Lesson 5 |

### 6. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ A well-read candidate may object that Marxist and later world-systems interpretations treat imperial competition as the fundamental cause, and that rejecting it is a choice of school rather than a finding.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The objection has force, and the honest answer concedes part of it. ✅ A version of this argument is recorded - that the desire for economic mastery of the world caused German businessmen and capitalists to want war with Britain, and that Marxist historians like this theory because it puts the blame for the war on the capitalist system. ✅ So is the critics' reply: Germany was already well on the way to economic victory, and a leading German industrialist remarked in 1913 that three or four more years of peace would make Germany the unchallenged economic master of Europe - on which argument the last thing Germany needed was a major war. ⚠️ The correct examination position is therefore not to dismiss the economic interpretation but to state it, state the objection to it, and note that the strongest form of the economic argument points away from war rather than towards it.

**The residual.** ⚠️ What remains unresolved is the weight of imperial competition in forming the mental habits of the decision-makers. That is a question about political culture, and this evidence base does not measure it. It is recorded as an open uncertainty.

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the syllabus clause names "colonization" among its sub-clauses, and that sub-clause is answered by the New Imperialism material rather than here. ⚠️ This matters for routing: a question about colonisation belongs to the New Imperialism owner, and a question about the outbreak of the war belongs here. A script that answers an outbreak question with a colonisation essay has misrouted its own answer.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the role of imperial rivalry in the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ "The First World War was the price Europe paid for the Scramble for Africa." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the economic interpretation of the origins of the First World War.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ In any of these, do not open by refuting the premise. Open by conceding the strongest version - the powers were empires, they had spent three decades in competitive expansion, and German disappointment with Weltpolitik was real and politically consequential - and then apply the two pieces of dated evidence that limit it: disputes had always been settled without war, and Anglo-German colonial relations were good in early July 1914. Close by promoting the one channel that survives, which is naval rivalry. ⚠️ That structure earns more than a flat rejection because it demonstrates that the candidate can grade evidence rather than choose a side.

### Traps

- Writing that the powers went to war over colonies. ✅ The recorded assessment is that colonial disputes had always been settled without war.
- Dating the Scramble from the 1914 crisis rather than from the burst of expansion after 1880.
- Saying Germany had no empire. ✅ It had islands in the Pacific and territory in Africa; the recorded complaint is that this was small in comparison with others and not very rewarding economically.
- Dropping China from the imperial picture and thereby losing the non-European dimension.
- Presenting the economic interpretation without its recorded rebuttal, or the rebuttal without the interpretation.
- Using "imperialism" as an undifferentiated cause when the evidence separates direct cause, background habit and naval side effect.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Imperialism defined: the building up of an empire by seizing territory overseas.
2. ✅ Great burst of imperialist expansion in the years after 1880.
3. ✅ Most of Africa taken over in the Scramble for Africa; motive mainly control of new markets and new sources of raw materials.
4. ✅ Intervention in the crumbling Chinese Empire; European powers, the USA and Japan all forced concessions at different times; Chinese overthrew the Manchu dynasty and set up a republic in 1911.
5. ✅ German Weltpolitik: some Pacific islands and African territory; empire small compared with others and not very rewarding economically; Germans accused Britain, Russia and France of encirclement.
6. ✅ Recorded verdict: colonial rivalry as a direct cause is not convincing; disputes had always been settled without war.
7. ✅ Decisive date: in early July 1914 Anglo-German relations were good, with an agreement favourable to Germany over a possible partition of Portuguese colonies in Africa.
8. ✅ The exception: one side effect of colonial rivalry did cause dangerous friction - naval rivalry.
9. ✅ The economic interpretation exists and is recorded, as is its rebuttal: a leading German industrialist in 1913 expected three or four more years of peace to make Germany the unchallenged economic master of Europe.
10. ⚠️ Three imperial claims, three verdicts: direct cause rejected; background habit accepted; naval side effect promoted.
11. ⚠️ Routing discipline: colonisation questions belong to the New Imperialism owner; outbreak questions belong here.

### Concept check

**Question:** An answer asserts that "the First World War was fundamentally a war between empires competing for colonies". Using only dated evidence, show why this cannot be sustained in its strong form, and identify the one channel through which imperial competition genuinely contributed.

**Model answer:** The strong form fails on three dated grounds. First, the record of colonial disputes between the great powers in the thirty years after 1880 is a record of settlement without general war, including both Moroccan crises, which ended in a conference at Algeciras in January 1906 and in a negotiated exchange in 1911 in which Germany recognised the French protectorate in return for two strips of territory in the French Congo. Second, and decisively, the state of colonial relations between the two powers whose enmity mattered most was good at the moment the crisis began: in early July 1914 Britain and Germany had just reached an agreement favourable to Germany over a possible partition of Portuguese colonies in Africa. A thesis that requires colonial antagonism to be at its height in July 1914 is contradicted by July 1914. Third, the economic version of the thesis points the wrong way: a leading German industrialist said in 1913 that three or four more years of peace would make Germany the unchallenged economic master of Europe, so on the economic reading war was the one thing that could interrupt the outcome Germany was already obtaining. The channel that survives is naval rivalry, which the source identifies as the one side effect of colonial rivalry that did cause dangerous friction - and even that channel has to be qualified by the fact that Germany reduced naval spending early in 1913 to concentrate on the army.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting a refuted strong claim into no claim at all. Imperial competition is not the cause of the war and is not irrelevant to it: it supplied the politics of prestige and relative standing within which the July decisions were taken, and it generated the fleet programme that made Britain treat Germany as its principal rival. The examiner is testing grading, not rejection.

*The next lesson takes up the structure that everyone names and few explain. Europe had two alliance blocs by 1907. The question is not whether they existed but what they obliged their members to do - and the recorded answer is: remarkably little.*

## Lesson 4 - How Europe acquired two blocs, and what those blocs actually obliged anyone to do

Progress: 4/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the step-by-step construction of the alliance system from 1882 to 1907; the Anglo-Japanese alliance of 1902; the composition of the Triple Alliance and the Triple Entente; the "armed camps" and the brushes with war since 1900; Kennan's position that the 1894 alliance sealed Europe's fate; the four recorded instances of allies not helping each other; Italy's position and its 1915 entry; the difference between an alignment and an obligation; how to use the blocs without determinism

CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 13
