━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the alliance system built step by step, and the record against determinism

```text
   HOW THE TWO CAMPS WERE ASSEMBLED
   --------------------------------
   1882   TRIPLE ALLIANCE        Germany + Austria-Hungary + Italy
   1894   France and Russia sign an alliance
   1902   Britain and Japan sign an alliance          [outside Europe]
   1904   Britain and France sign the ENTENTE CORDIALE
          ("friendly getting-together")
   1907   Britain and Russia sign an agreement
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   RESULT BY 1907
   TRIPLE ALLIANCE: Germany | Austria-Hungary | Italy
   TRIPLE ENTENTE : Britain | France          | Russia

   THE CLAIM THIS PICTURE INVITES          THE RECORD THAT TESTS IT
   ------------------------------          ------------------------
   "the blocs made war automatic"          1904-05 Russia at war with
                                           Japan -> FRANCE SENDS NO HELP
                                           1908 Russia protests the
                                           annexation of Bosnia
                                           -> FRANCE DOES NOT SUPPORT HER
                                           1906 and 1911 Germany fails
                                           in Morocco -> AUSTRIA-HUNGARY
                                           TAKES NO INTEREST
                                           1913 Second Balkan War
                                           -> GERMANY RESTRAINS AUSTRIA
                                           FROM ATTACKING SERBIA
                                           1915 ITALY, a member of the
                                           Triple Alliance, ENTERS THE
                                           WAR AGAINST GERMANY
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   NO POWER ACTUALLY DECLARED WAR BECAUSE OF ONE OF THESE
   TREATIES OF ALLIANCE.
```

*The diagram carries the lesson's whole argument. The left column is what a map of two blocs makes you want to say; the right column is five recorded instances in which the blocs did not behave as the map predicts. ⚠️ An answer that reproduces the left column and ignores the right column is describing a diagram rather than explaining an event.*

### 1. Why the tidy answer predicts the wrong things

Alliances look like the perfect explanation. Two teams, everyone committed, one member attacked, everybody joins, local war becomes general war. It is tidy, it is memorable, and it is the version most candidates have absorbed before they open a book.

The difficulty is that it predicts the wrong things. If alliances were automatic, then the crisis of 1908 should have produced a general war, because Austria-Hungary annexed a province Serbia wanted and Russia backed Serbia. It did not. The crisis of 1911 should have produced one, because Germany sent a warship to a port France was claiming and Britain warned Germany off. It did not. And a member of one alliance ended up fighting on the other side.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the alliances are real and important, but their importance is of a different kind from the one the diagram suggests. They did not compel; they made it more expensive to back down, because a power that abandoned a partner expected to lose standing. That is a psychological and reputational mechanism, not a legal one, and it explains the 1914 outcome far better than an automatic-trigger model - because a reputational cost can be paid five times and refused the sixth.

### 2. The construction, step by step

✅ **Fact:** the source's time chart records the formation of the camps with exact dates, under the heading that Europe divides into two armed camps.

| Year | ✅ Instrument | ⚠️ What changed |
|---:|---|---|
| 1882 | Triple Alliance of Germany, Austria-Hungary and Italy | The first of the two camps exists; a continental bloc is formed around the strongest continental power |
| 1894 | France and Russia sign an alliance | The strategic problem of a two-front war is created for Germany; Kennan's position, below, dates the decisive moment here |
| 1902 | Britain and Japan sign an alliance | Britain's isolation is broken outside Europe; a non-European great power is bound into the European alliance structure |
| 1904 | Britain and France sign the Entente Cordiale | The two colonial rivals settle; ✅ the recorded understanding was that France would recognise Britain's position in Egypt in return for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco |
| 1907 | Britain and Russia sign an agreement | The third side of the Entente closes; ✅ the Germans saw it as confirmation of their fears of encirclement |

✅ **Fact:** the resulting membership is recorded exactly: the Triple Alliance comprised Germany, Austria-Hungary and Italy; the Triple Entente comprised Britain, France and Russia. ✅ In addition, Japan and Britain had signed an alliance in 1902.

✅ **Fact:** the source also records the temperature of the system: friction between the two main groups, sometimes called "the armed camps", had brought Europe to the verge of war several times since 1900.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the shape of that last sentence, because it is an argument in both directions. It concedes that the system repeatedly approached war - which supports the structural reading. It also records that the system repeatedly stopped short - which refutes the automatic reading. Both halves belong in an answer.

### 3. The entente distinction, which most scripts miss

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two camps were not the same kind of thing, and the difference matters for Britain's position in July 1914.

| Feature | Triple Alliance | Triple Entente |
|---|---|---|
| Legal form | A formal alliance treaty | ✅ An *entente* - the source glosses the Entente Cordiale as a "friendly getting-together"; ✅ the 1907 Anglo-Russian instrument is recorded as an agreement settling differences in Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet, explicitly **not a military alliance** |
| What it settled | Mutual support arrangements among three continental states | Colonial and regional disputes between former rivals |
| Military content | Alliance obligations, subject to the qualifications below | ✅ Anglo-French "military conversations" followed the Moroccan Crisis of 1905-06; conversations are not a treaty obligation |
| Consequence for 1914 | Germany and Austria-Hungary acted together from 5 July onward | Britain was not treaty-bound to fight for France, which is why its entry required a different trigger - Belgium |

✅ **Fact:** the source states the British point in its factual-risk controls: do not say Britain entered because of the alliance with France; Britain had no binding alliance obligation of that kind, and Belgian neutrality was the immediate legal-political issue.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this single distinction disposes of a great many weak answers. If the Entente had been an alliance, Britain's entry would need no explanation; because it was not, Britain's entry is a separate decision with a separate cause, and Lesson 15 treats it as such.

### 4. The determinist case, stated fairly

✅ **Fact:** the source records the strongest version of the alliance explanation and attributes it. The American diplomat and historian George Kennan believed that once the 1894 alliance had been signed between France and Russia, the fate of Europe was sealed. As suspicions mounted between the two opposing camps, Russia, Austria-Hungary and Germany got themselves into situations which they could not escape from without suffering further humiliation; war seemed to be the only way for them to save face.

✅ **Fact:** the source records Kennan's position in the same terms - alliance blocs made war highly likely - and recommends using it as long-term background that is not sufficient alone.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Kennan's argument is better than the version of it that circulates. It is not a claim that treaties compelled action; it is a claim about the *psychology of standing* inside a bipolar structure - that once two camps exist, every retreat is legible as humiliation, and states will eventually prefer risk to loss of face. Stated that way, it survives the counter-evidence in section 5, because a reputational cost is exactly the kind of cost that can be absorbed several times and then refused.

### 5. The recorded case against automatic alliances

✅ **Fact:** the source sets out the counter-evidence in a single passage, and every item is dated and specific. Many historians think the alliance explanation is not convincing; there had been many crises since 1904, and none of them had led to a major war. In fact, there was nothing binding about these alliances:

- ✅ when Russia was struggling in the war against Japan in 1904-05, the French sent no help;
- ✅ nor did they support Russia when she protested at the Austrian annexation of Bosnia;
- ✅ Austria-Hungary took no interest in Germany's unsuccessful attempts to prevent France from taking over Morocco in the Morocco and Agadir crises;
- ✅ Germany had restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War;
- ✅ Italy, though a member of the Triple Alliance, was on good terms with France and Britain, and entered the war against Germany in 1915.

✅ **Fact:** the passage concludes with the sentence that should be quoted in substance in any answer on this question: no power actually declared war because of one of these treaties of alliance.

✅ **Fact:** the source independently records the Italian point in its factual-risk controls: do not write that alliance treaties obliged every signatory to fight in 1914, because Italy was in the Triple Alliance and entered on the Allied side in 1915.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Italian case deserves more than a footnote, because it is the strongest single refutation available. A member of one camp fought in the other camp. No account of the alliances as binding machinery can accommodate that, and an answer that deploys it in one clause has closed the determinist reading with a fact rather than with an assertion.

### 6. So what did the alliances actually do?

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the constructive answer has four parts, and all four are supported by material in this session.

| Function | What the alliances did | Evidence in this session |
|---|---|---|
| **Signalling** | They told each government which other governments would probably matter in a crisis, which raised the stakes of every regional dispute | ✅ The Germans read the 1907 Anglo-Russian agreement as confirmation of encirclement even though it was not a military alliance |
| **Emboldening** | Assurance of support made a client or partner readier to take risks | ✅ Russian backing probably made Serbia more reckless in her anti-Austrian policy than she might otherwise have been; ✅ the unconditional German assurance of July 1914 enabled the Austrian hard line |
| **Restraining** | The same relationship could be used to hold a partner back | ✅ Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War - the same instrument, the opposite effect |
| **Raising the cost of retreat** | Each retreat was scored as a loss of standing, so the next crisis began from a worse position | ✅ After 1908 the Russians were determined to avoid any further humiliation; ⚠️ this is Kennan's mechanism, and it is the one that survives |

⚠️ The restraining row is the one to memorise, because it is decisive and counter-intuitive: the same alliance that is blamed for causing the war is recorded restraining its partner one year earlier. ⚠️ The difference between 1913 and 1914 is therefore not the alliance; it is the decision taken inside the alliance. That sentence is worth carrying into an answer verbatim.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the alliances did not oblige anyone to do anything, why did five great powers and Serbia end up at war within nine days of 28 July?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because obligation is not the only way to produce coordinated behaviour. The actual sequence, taught in Lesson 15, runs through decisions and mechanisms rather than through treaty clauses: an assurance freely given, a mobilisation ordered for reasons of prestige and Balkan position, a counter-mobilisation demanded by an operational plan, an attack on a state with which there was no specific quarrel, and a violated neutrality that gave a non-allied power a legal-strategic ground to enter. ⚠️ None of those steps is a treaty performance; each is a choice made inside an alignment.

**The residual.** ⚠️ What cannot be settled from this evidence base is the counterfactual weight of the alignments - whether Russia would have mobilised without the French connection, or whether Vienna would have moved without the German assurance. ✅ On the second point the evidence base itself divides: Williamson's recorded position is that the Austrian leaders made their own decisions and that German pressure and promises of support were not important, while the Fischer tradition treats the assurance as crucial. That disagreement is displayed in Lesson 17 and not resolved here.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's directive map records that a question on the role of nationalism, imperialism or militarism is testing the candidate's ability to isolate one factor while showing its interaction, and that the wrong response is to reproduce the whole causes essay. ⚠️ The alliance question is the purest example, because the temptation to reproduce everything is strongest there.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The alliance system made the First World War inevitable." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Examine the role of the alliance system in converting a Balkan crisis into a European war.
> ⚠️ How far were the pre-1914 alliances responsible for the outbreak of the First World War?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The highest-scoring structure on this question is: concede Kennan's mechanism in its strongest form; produce the five counter-instances as a single compressed clause; state the four functions the alliances actually performed; and conclude that the alliances explain the *shape* of the war - who fought whom - far better than they explain its *occurrence*. ⚠️ That last distinction, shape versus occurrence, is the sentence that separates a good script from a competent one on this question.

### Traps

- Writing that the alliances obliged the powers to fight. ✅ No power actually declared war because of one of these treaties of alliance.
- Treating the Triple Entente as an alliance. ✅ The 1907 instrument is recorded as an agreement and explicitly not a military alliance.
- Forgetting Italy's 1915 entry against Germany.
- Forgetting that Germany restrained Austria-Hungary in 1913.
- Dating the Entente Cordiale to 1907 or the Anglo-Russian agreement to 1904.
- Omitting the 1902 Anglo-Japanese alliance, which is the alliance system's one non-European member and a route to the globalisation of the war.
- Presenting Kennan as a straw man. ✅ His recorded claim is that the fate of Europe was sealed by 1894, and it deserves to be met at full strength.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1882 Triple Alliance: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy.
2. ✅ 1894 France and Russia sign an alliance.
3. ✅ 1902 Britain and Japan sign an alliance.
4. ✅ 1904 Britain and France sign the Entente Cordiale; the understanding covered Egypt and Morocco.
5. ✅ 1907 Britain and Russia sign an agreement settling differences in Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet; not a military alliance.
6. ✅ Triple Entente: Britain, France, Russia. Friction between the "armed camps" had brought Europe to the verge of war several times since 1900.
7. ✅ Kennan: once the 1894 Franco-Russian alliance was signed, the fate of Europe was sealed; states could not escape without humiliation and war seemed the only way to save face.
8. ✅ Five counter-instances: no French help to Russia in 1904-05; no French support over Bosnia; Austrian indifference to Germany's Moroccan setbacks; German restraint of Austria-Hungary in the Second Balkan War; Italy entering against Germany in 1915.
9. ✅ No power actually declared war because of one of these treaties of alliance.
10. ⚠️ Four real functions: signalling, emboldening, restraining and raising the cost of retreat.
11. ⚠️ The decisive contrast: the same alliance restrained Vienna in 1913 and backed it in 1914. The variable is the decision, not the treaty.
12. ⚠️ Answer line: the alliances explain the shape of the war better than its occurrence.

### Concept check

**Question:** Kennan held that the fate of Europe was sealed once France and Russia allied in 1894. State the strongest version of that claim, then show precisely what it can and cannot explain about 1914.

**Model answer:** The strongest version is not that the treaties compelled performance but that a two-camp structure changes the meaning of retreat. Once Europe divided into opposed groups, every regional dispute became a test of standing between the groups, so that Russia, Austria-Hungary and Germany found themselves in positions from which they could not withdraw without further humiliation, and war came to look like the only way to save face. That claim explains three things well: why crises multiplied from 1900 onward and repeatedly brought Europe to the verge of war; why the 1908 humiliation left Russia determined never to be humiliated again and therefore rearming; and why the shape of the 1914 war - who fought alongside whom - follows the 1907 map almost exactly. It cannot explain three other things. It cannot explain the four recorded refusals of support, in which France gave Russia no help against Japan and no support over Bosnia, Austria-Hungary took no interest in Germany's Moroccan defeats, and Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia in the Second Balkan War. It cannot accommodate Italy, a Triple Alliance member that entered the war against Germany in 1915. And it cannot explain timing: a structure in place since 1894 and complete since 1907 produced containment in 1905-06, 1908, 1911, 1912 and 1913 and war in 1914, so something other than the structure distinguishes the sixth case. That something is the set of decisions taken between 5 July and 4 August, which is why the alliances belong in the structural paragraph and not in the decision paragraph.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the counter-evidence as proof that the alliances did not matter. They mattered enormously - as signals, as sources of confidence, as instruments of restraint and as an accumulating ledger of standing. What the counter-evidence destroys is only the automatic-trigger model, and destroying it is what allows the answer to explain why 1914 differed from 1908.

*The next lesson takes up the second half of the structural argument. Alignments told the powers who would probably matter; armaments told them how much time they had. The naval race is the most famous of these competitions and, by 1914, the least active - which is exactly the kind of finding that separates a prepared answer from a confident one.*

## Lesson 5 - Militarism and the arms competition: what was built, by whom, and whether it was still a live cause in 1914

Progress: 5/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Mahan and the doctrine of sea power; Tirpitz's Navy Law of 1897; the Dreadnought of 1906 and the obsolescence effect; the capital-ship counts at the end of 1911; the naval race as the main bone of contention; the German reduction of naval spending early in 1913 and Churchill's recorded assessment; Russian military rebuilding and railway improvement after 1908; German army expansion blocked by the Reichstag's refusal of taxes; French loans to Russia; how to argue militarism without overstating it

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two arms competitions on different timetables

```text
   THE NAVAL COMPETITION                    THE LAND COMPETITION
   ---------------------                    --------------------
   1897  TIRPITZ'S NAVY LAW                 1908  BOSNIA CRISIS
         Germany intends to build                 Russia humiliated
         up a fleet; Mahan's doctrine              |
         that sea power is the key                 v
         to a great empire                  MASSIVE RUSSIAN MILITARY
          |                                 BUILD-UP AND MODERNISATION
          v                                 of the army, PLUS improvement
   1906  BRITISH DREADNOUGHT                of the railway system to allow
         makes all other battleships        FASTER MOBILISATION
         obsolete -> Germany can now         |
         build on equal terms                v
          |                                 RUSSIA FINANCED BY HUGE LOANS
          v                                 FROM THE FRENCH GOVERNMENT
   END OF 1911                               |
         BRITAIN 8 Dreadnought-type          v
         GERMANY 4                          GERMAN ARMY EXPANSION HAMPERED
          |                                 by Reichstag opposition: it
          v                                 REFUSED TO SANCTION THE
   EARLY 1913                                NECESSARY TAX INCREASES
         GERMANY REDUCES NAVAL SPENDING      |
         to concentrate on the ARMY          v
          |                                 THE GERMAN GENERALS CONCLUDE
          v                                 THAT TIME IS RUNNING OUT
   SPRING-SUMMER 1914
         Churchill: naval rivalry has
         CEASED to be a cause of friction
         because Britain could not be
         overtaken in capital ships
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   THE FAMOUS RACE IS OVER BY 1914. THE UNFAMOUS ONE IS ACCELERATING.
   THAT INVERSION IS THE LESSON.
```

*The diagram is the lesson. Almost every script writes the naval race as a live cause of the 1914 war and omits the land competition entirely. ⚠️ The evidence supports the opposite emphasis: the naval race had been decided in Britain's favour and had stopped being a source of friction, while the land and railway competition was running hard and was directly feeding the German argument that war must come before the end of 1914.*

### 1. What militarism has to mean before it explains anything

"Militarism" is one of those words that does all the work in a script and none of the work in an argument. It usually means something like: they had big armies, they admired soldiers, so they fought.

That is not an explanation, because every European state in every previous decade also had armies and admired soldiers. To make militarism explanatory you have to show a mechanism - something that arms competition did that made a decision for war more likely at a particular moment. There are exactly two such mechanisms in this topic, and both are datable.

The first is **threat perception**: a programme that a rival reads as directed at itself changes the rival's politics. The German fleet did that to Britain.

The second, and more powerful, is **the closing window**: when a state's leaders believe their relative military position will be worse in two years than it is today, the argument for acting now becomes available. That is what the Russian rebuilding did to the German general staff.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second mechanism is the one that connects militarism to the July decisions. It is also the one most scripts omit.

### 2. The naval race: doctrine, programme and instrument

✅ **Fact:** the source records the intellectual origin. The German government had been greatly influenced by the writings of an American, Alfred Mahan, who believed that sea power was the key to the successful build-up of a great empire. It followed that Germany needed a much larger navy capable of challenging the world's greatest sea power, Britain.

✅ **Fact:** the programme is dated. Starting with Admiral Tirpitz's Navy Law of 1897, the Germans made a determined effort to expand their navy. ✅ The time chart records the same item: Admiral Tirpitz's Navy Law of 1897 - Germany intends to build up a fleet.

✅ **Fact:** the British response and its unintended effect are recorded exactly. The rapid growth of the German fleet probably did not worry the British too much at first because they had an enormous lead. However, the introduction of the powerful British Dreadnought battleship in 1906 changed all this, because it made all other battleships obsolete. This meant that the Germans could begin building Dreadnoughts on equal terms with Britain. ✅ The time chart dates it: 1906, Britain builds first Dreadnought battleship.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the most instructive ironies in the topic and is worth a sentence in any militarism answer. Britain's technical leap destroyed the value of its own existing lead. A qualitative advantage was converted into a quantitative race that started, in the new class of ship, from something much closer to parity. ⚠️ It is a clean example of a security measure producing insecurity - and it can be stated in one line without any theoretical vocabulary.

✅ **Fact:** the race's status is recorded: the resulting naval race was the main bone of contention between the two right up to 1914, and for many of the British the new German navy could mean only one thing - that Germany intended making war against Britain.

### 3. The numbers this session is allowed to use

✅ **Fact:** exactly one pair of counts is recorded in this evidence base, and it is dated. At the end of 1911, the British had built eight of the new and more powerful Dreadnought-type battleships, compared with Germany's four.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that ratio is the evidence for two different claims, and it is worth being clear which one you are making. It shows that the race was real and expensive. It also shows that Britain was winning it - which is the premise of the 1913-14 qualification below.

**Evidentiary control.** ⚠️ No other quantitative evidence about military or economic capacity is available here. This session gives no naval budget, no total fleet strength, no army size, no mobilisation figure and no comparative spending series for any power, because none is sourced. ⚠️ The standard literature contains such series; an answer that wants them must take them from a source that holds them, and an answer that invents them is taking an unnecessary risk in a paper where the argument, not the arithmetic, carries the marks. ⚠️ Ordinary numbers of date, interval and structure - years, the length of an ultimatum, the number of weeks a plan allowed itself - are not quantitative capacity evidence and are used throughout.

### 4. The qualification that most scripts never reach

✅ **Fact:** the source does not leave the naval race as a live 1914 cause. It records that early in 1913 the Germans had actually reduced naval spending in order to concentrate more on strengthening the army. ✅ And it records Winston Churchill's assessment, which it endorses: in the spring and summer of 1914, naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction, because it was certain that Britain could not be overtaken as far as capital ships were concerned.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow, and they are worth more than the fact itself.

1. **Chronological precision beats enthusiasm.** The naval race is a cause of Anglo-German estrangement across roughly 1897-1912. It is not a cause of the July 1914 decisions. An answer that distinguishes the two periods is doing history; an answer that runs them together is doing atmosphere.
2. **The German shift of resources from sea to land is itself a cause.** ⚠️ A state that decides in early 1913 to concentrate on its army is a state preparing for a continental war rather than for a naval confrontation - and Lesson 6's operational plan is a continental plan.
3. **It weakens one blame thesis and strengthens another.** ⚠️ If the fleet had stopped being the issue, then Britain's entry cannot be explained by naval fear alone, and the Belgian trigger of Lesson 15 becomes indispensable rather than decorative.

### 5. The land competition, which is where the live cause was

✅ **Fact:** the source records the Russian programme and its origin precisely. After the Bosnia Crisis of 1908, the Russians were determined to avoid any further humiliation and embarked on a massive military build-up and modernisation of the army, together with an improvement in their railway system to allow faster mobilisation. They intended to be prepared if Serbia should ever appeal for help again.

✅ **Fact:** it records the German constraint. German army expansion was being hampered by opposition from the Reichstag, which refused to sanction the necessary tax increases. ✅ On the other hand the Russians had been helped by huge loans from the French government.

✅ **Fact:** it records the conclusion the German general staff drew. Von Jagow, who was German Foreign Minister at the outbreak of war, reported comments made earlier in 1914 in which Moltke stated that there was no alternative for the Germans but to make "preventive" war in order to defeat their enemies before they became too powerful. The German generals had decided that a preventive war, a war for survival, was necessary, and that it must take place before the end of 1914. They believed that if they waited longer than that, Russia would be too strong.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most important causal chain in the militarism material, and it should be written as a chain rather than as a list.

```text
   1908  Russia humiliated over Bosnia
      -> decides never to be humiliated again
      -> rebuilds the army AND the railways
         (railways = speed of mobilisation = the variable that
          German planning is most sensitive to)
      -> financed by huge French government loans
                         |
   MEANWHILE in Germany
      Reichstag refuses the tax increases the army expansion needs
      -> German growth constrained exactly while Russian growth is
         subsidised
                         |
   GERMAN GENERAL STAFF CONCLUSION, reported for early 1914
      there is no alternative but preventive war
      it must take place BEFORE THE END OF 1914
      because after that Russia will be too strong
                         |
   => MILITARISM SUPPLIES NOT AN APPETITE FOR WAR
      BUT A DEADLINE FOR IT
```

⚠️ The last line is the answer-grabbing formulation for this lesson. Arms competition in 1914 did not make the powers want war; it made one general staff believe that if there was going to be a war it had to be soon. That is a far more precise and far more defensible claim than "militarism caused the war", and it connects directly to the July decisions.

### 6. Comparison: the two competitions, scored against each other

| Axis | Naval competition | Land and railway competition |
|---|---|---|
| Began | ✅ Navy Law of 1897; transformed by the Dreadnought of 1906 | ✅ Accelerated after the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 |
| Principal antagonists | Germany and Britain | Russia and Germany, with France financing Russia |
| Recorded status at the end of 1911 | ✅ Britain 8 Dreadnought-type ships to Germany's 4 | Russian rebuilding under way; German expansion constrained by the Reichstag |
| Status in 1914 | ✅ Ceased to be a cause of friction; Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships | ⚠️ Running hard, and producing an explicit deadline in German staff thinking |
| Causal role in the July decisions | ⚠️ Indirect at most; its work was done earlier, in fixing British perceptions of Germany | ⚠️ Direct: it supplies the "before the end of 1914" reasoning |
| Where it belongs in an answer | The estrangement paragraph, dated 1897-1912 | The decision paragraph, dated 1913-14 |

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the naval race had ceased to matter by 1914, why does every account still treat it as a major cause?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because it was a major cause of something else. It is the principal reason Britain came to see Germany as its chief rival, and perceptions formed over fifteen years do not dissolve when a spending line falls. ✅ The source itself calls it the main bone of contention between the two right up to 1914 in one passage and records Churchill's assessment that it had ceased to be a cause of friction in another. ⚠️ Those statements are compatible if you separate the *stock* of mistrust from the *flow* of new provocation: the stock was high, the flow had stopped. An answer that says exactly that is handling a tension in its own source honestly, which is a marks-earning move.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the preventive-war reasoning not simply prove the Fischer thesis that Germany planned the war?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, and the distinction matters. A general staff's conclusion that war must come before a deadline is evidence of a war plan's timing logic; it is not by itself evidence of a decision by the political leadership to provoke a general war. ✅ Lesson 17 records that Fischer's stronger claim - deliberate planning and provocation to make Germany the dominant world power - is contested, that Koch dismissed the evidentiary weight given to the 1912 meeting, and that a third position holds that the Kaiser and Bethmann-Hollweg did not want a major war at all and expected firmness to keep Russia neutral. ⚠️ The militarism evidence is compatible with more than one of those readings, and a careful answer says so.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Unresolved here: how far civilian leaders shared the generals' deadline. The evidence base records the military reasoning and records separately that the Kaiser and Bethmann made last-minute attempts to avoid war on 30 July. Those two facts are both recorded and are not reconciled in this evidence base.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source lists militarism among the structural causes and identifies mobilisation plans, especially Germany's move through Belgium, as the operational cause that explains why a local war became a general war. ✅ The subject's revision chart lists the naval arms race and imperial rivalry under the pre-war armed peace of 1871-1914.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the contribution of militarism and the arms race to the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ "The Anglo-German naval rivalry was the principal cause of the First World War." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Account for the outbreak of the First World War with special reference to alliance politics and mobilisation.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ On the naval question specifically, the structure that scores is: establish the race with the doctrine, the 1897 law, the 1906 Dreadnought and the 1911 counts; then apply the 1913 spending reduction and Churchill's assessment to date its decline; then transfer the causal weight to the land and railway competition and the deadline it produced. ⚠️ A script that can say "by 1914 the naval race was over and the land race was not" has a thesis; a script that lists both has a paragraph.

### Traps

- Writing that Germany overtook Britain at sea. ✅ At the end of 1911 the counts were eight to four in Britain's favour, and by 1914 Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships.
- Presenting the naval race as a live cause of the July decisions without the 1913-14 qualification.
- Omitting that Germany cut naval spending early in 1913 to strengthen the army.
- Treating "militarism" as an attitude rather than as a mechanism. The mechanisms are threat perception and the closing window.
- Omitting the Reichstag's refusal of the tax increases, which is what makes the German window look like it is closing.
- Omitting the French loans that financed Russian rebuilding, which is what makes the Russian side of the window open.
- Giving budget or fleet figures other than the recorded eight-to-four count.
- Using the preventive-war reasoning as proof of the strongest German-guilt thesis without the recorded objections.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Mahan's doctrine: sea power is the key to the successful build-up of a great empire; it greatly influenced the German government.
2. ✅ Tirpitz's Navy Law, 1897: Germany intends to build up a fleet.
3. ✅ British Dreadnought, 1906: made all other battleships obsolete and let Germany build on equal terms in the new class.
4. ✅ End of 1911: Britain 8 Dreadnought-type battleships, Germany 4.
5. ✅ The naval race was the main bone of contention between Britain and Germany right up to 1914.
6. ✅ Early 1913: Germany reduced naval spending to concentrate on the army.
7. ✅ Churchill, for spring and summer 1914: naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction because Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships.
8. ✅ After 1908 Russia undertook a massive military build-up and army modernisation plus railway improvement for faster mobilisation.
9. ✅ Russia was helped by huge loans from the French government; German army expansion was hampered by the Reichstag's refusal to sanction the necessary tax increases.
10. ✅ Moltke, reported by von Jagow for early 1914: no alternative but preventive war, to be made before the end of 1914, because after that Russia would be too strong.
11. ⚠️ The formulation: arms competition in 1914 supplied a deadline, not an appetite.
12. ⚠️ Stock versus flow: the stock of Anglo-German mistrust was high in 1914; the flow of new naval provocation had stopped.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the arms competition of the pre-war years contributed to the decisions of July 1914, and explain why an answer that concentrates on the Anglo-German naval race gets the mechanism wrong.

**Model answer:** The contribution runs through a deadline rather than through an appetite. After the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 Russia resolved never to be humiliated again and undertook a massive military build-up and modernisation of the army together with an improvement of the railway system specifically to permit faster mobilisation, and this was financed by huge loans from the French government. In the same years German army expansion was hampered because the Reichstag refused to sanction the necessary tax increases. The result was that German strength was constrained exactly while Russian strength was subsidised, and the German general staff drew the conclusion reported for early 1914 by von Jagow: that Moltke saw no alternative to a preventive war to defeat their enemies before they became too powerful, that such a war was necessary, and that it must take place before the end of 1914 because after that Russia would be too strong. That is a mechanism with a date attached, and it bears directly on why Berlin gave an unconditional assurance in July rather than the restraint it had applied in 1913. Concentrating on the naval race gets this wrong in two ways. First, it points at the wrong pair of states: the live competition in 1913-14 was Russo-German on land, not Anglo-German at sea. Second, the naval race had effectively been decided: at the end of 1911 Britain had eight Dreadnought-type battleships to Germany's four, Germany reduced naval spending early in 1913 to concentrate on the army, and Churchill's assessment for the spring and summer of 1914 was that naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction because Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships. The naval race explains why Britain regarded Germany as its principal rival; it does not explain the timing of July 1914.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from the 1913-14 qualification that the naval race was unimportant. It did the work that mattered earlier, by fixing British perceptions of German intent across fifteen years; what it had stopped doing by 1914 was generating new provocations. Keeping the stock of mistrust separate from the flow of provocation lets an answer use both findings without contradiction.

*The next lesson takes the land competition one step further, into the place where it became a constraint on politics. An army that is being enlarged is a fact about capacity. A plan that specifies which frontier that army crosses, in what order and on what railway schedule, is a fact about choice - and it is the point at which soldiers begin to set limits on what diplomats may do.*

## Lesson 6 - War plans: how an operational timetable became a political constraint, and whether the plan existed as described

Progress: 6/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what mobilisation meant and why it was not the same as war; the plan attributed to Schlieffen and its assumptions; Ritter's assessment of rigidity; Taylor's railway-timetable argument; the recorded exchange of 30-31 July between Wilhelm II, Bethmann-Hollweg and Moltke; the claim that the generals took over from the politicians; Zuber's challenge and the at-least-five-alternatives finding; how to write about the Schlieffen Plan safely

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the mobilisation plans of the great powers; Ritter's judgement on rigidity; Taylor's railway-timetable argument; the plan's assumptions and the Liege requirement; the recorded exchange of 30-31 July; the conclusion that the generals had taken over; and Zuber's documentary challenge
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 13
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what a mobilisation plan does to a political decision

```text
   THE POLITICAL WORLD                    THE OPERATIONAL WORLD
   -------------------                    ---------------------
   a crisis begins                        armies exist on paper and in
   diplomats exchange notes               depots; they are useless until
   proposals are floated                  assembled and moved
   time is the diplomat's                 ASSEMBLY AND MOVEMENT RUN ON
   only real resource                     RAILWAY TIMETABLES FIXED IN
          |                               ADVANCE; time is the planner's
          |                               scarcest resource
          v                                        |
   RUSSIA ORDERS GENERAL MOBILISATION              |
   -> in the political world this is a             |
      signal and a bargaining position             |
   -> "Russian mobilisation did not                |
      necessarily mean war - their troops          |
      could be halted at the frontiers"            |
                                                   v
                              GERMAN PLANNING READS IT DIFFERENTLY
                              -> Moltke demands IMMEDIATE German
                                 mobilisation so the plan can run
                              -> 30 July: Kaiser and Bethmann urge
                                 Vienna to negotiate with Serbia
                              -> Wilhelm proposes PARTIAL mobilisation
                                 against Russia only, hoping Britain
                                 stays neutral if France is not attacked
                              -> MOLTKE REFUSES: no time to change all
                                 the railway timetables
                              -> IT WAS THE FULL PLAN OR NOTHING,
                                 even though Germany had no specific
                                 quarrel with France
                                          |
                                          v
                      "IT LOOKS AS THOUGH THE GENERALS HAD TAKEN OVER
                       CONTROL OF AFFAIRS FROM THE POLITICIANS."
```

*The diagram shows the exact moment at which an operational constraint overrode a political preference, and it is the single most quotable sequence in the topic. ⚠️ Note what it does and does not prove. It does not prove that Germany's leaders wanted a world war; on the contrary, the same sequence records them trying to avoid one on 30 July. It proves something narrower and stranger: that by 31 July the German political leadership could not obtain the limited option it wanted, because the instrument it would have had to use did not exist in a limited form.*

### 1. Mobilisation is not war: the word that causes the confusion

Start with the word that causes most of the confusion. **Mobilisation** is not war. It is the process of calling up reserves, concentrating them at assembly points and moving them by rail towards a frontier. A state can mobilise and then stop.

✅ **Fact:** the source makes exactly this point: Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war, because their troops could be halted at the frontiers.

So why did mobilisation function as the trigger in 1914? Because one power's plan made it impossible to treat the other's mobilisation as a pause. If your plan requires you to defeat one enemy in six weeks before the other can arrive, then every day your opponent mobilises and you do not is a day subtracted from your own margin. Under that logic the other side's preparation is not a signal to be read; it is a clock to be beaten.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why "mobilisation plans" belong in the causation answer as an *operational* cause and not as an item of militarism. Militarism supplied the deadline; the plans supplied the trip-wire.

### 2. The plan as recorded, and its assumptions

✅ **Fact:** the source records the plan and its authorship. Gerhard Ritter, a leading German historian, believed that the German plan for mobilisation, known as the Schlieffen Plan, drawn up by Count von Schlieffen in 1905-06, was extremely risky and inflexible and deserved to be seen as the start of disaster both for Germany and Europe; it gave the impression that Germany was being ruled by a band of unscrupulous militarists.

✅ **Fact:** the plan's operational content is recorded. It assumed that France would automatically join Russia; the bulk of German forces were to be sent by train to the Belgian frontier, and through Belgium to attack France, which would be knocked out in six weeks; German forces would then be switched rapidly across Europe to face Russia, whose mobilisation was expected to be slow. ✅ It depended on the rapid capture of Liege in Belgium.

| Assumption | ✅ As recorded | ⚠️ Why each assumption is itself a cause |
|---|---|---|
| France would automatically join Russia | Stated as an assumption of the plan | It removes the possibility of a one-front eastern war at the planning stage, years before any crisis |
| France could be knocked out in six weeks | Stated | A six-week promise is what makes a two-front war look survivable and therefore thinkable |
| Russian mobilisation would be slow | Stated | ⚠️ This is precisely the assumption that the post-1908 Russian railway programme was attacking |
| The route ran through Belgium and required Liege quickly | Stated | It converts a Franco-German war into a violation of a neutral state, which is what brings Britain in |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the connection between Lesson 5 and Lesson 6 and should be made explicitly in any answer that uses both. The Russian railway improvement after 1908 was an attack on the central assumption of German planning. That is why the German staff's deadline and the German staff's plan are the same problem seen from two sides.

### 3. Taylor's argument: timetables as a political fact

✅ **Fact:** A. J. P. Taylor argued that these plans, based on precise railway timetables for the rapid movement of troops, accelerated the tempo of events and reduced almost to nil the time available for negotiation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Taylor's claim is not that railways caused the war. It is that railways changed the *unit of decision time* from weeks to hours, and that diplomacy is an activity that requires weeks. ⚠️ A crisis in 1878 could be referred to a congress; a crisis in 1914 had to be resolved before the trains moved. That is a structural change in how international politics could be conducted, and it is the most transferable idea in this lesson.

### 4. The recorded sequence of 30-31 July

✅ **Fact:** the source records the sequence in detail, and it is the evidentiary heart of the lesson.

1. ✅ Once Moltke knew that Russia had ordered a general mobilisation, he demanded immediate German mobilisation so that the plan could be put into operation as soon as possible.
2. ✅ Almost at the last minute the Kaiser and Bethmann tried to avoid war and urged the Austrians to negotiate with Serbia on 30 July.
3. ✅ Wilhelm suggested a partial mobilisation against Russia only, instead of the full plan; he hoped that Britain would remain neutral if Germany refrained from attacking France.
4. ✅ Moltke, nervous of being left at the post by the Russians and French, insisted on the full plan; he said there was no time to change all the railway timetables to send the troop trains to Russia instead of to Belgium.
5. ✅ The account's conclusion: it looks as though the generals had taken over control of affairs from the politicians.
6. ✅ A further consequence is recorded: it also suggests that a British announcement on 31 July of an intention to support France would have made no difference to Germany - it was the full plan or nothing, even though Germany at that point had no specific quarrel with France.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** point 6 is the one to carry into an answer about British responsibility. ✅ The source elsewhere records the blame position that Britain should have made its support for France clear earlier. ⚠️ The timetable evidence is the strongest available reply to that position: if the plan could not be altered on 31 July, a clearer British signal on 31 July could not have altered it either. The two recorded claims sit in tension, and displaying that tension is better than choosing one.

### 5. Zuber's challenge, stated fully

✅ **Fact:** the source records a serious documentary challenge to the standard picture. Doubt was cast on this theory by an American military expert and historian, Terence Zuber, in his book *Inventing the Schlieffen Plan*, published in 2002. Using documents from the former East German military archive, he argued that the Schlieffen Plan was only one of at least five alternatives being considered by the German high command in the years after 1900.

✅ **Fact:** the content of the challenge is recorded. One alternative dealt with the possibility of a Russian attack at the same time as a French invasion; in that case the Germans would transfer considerable forces by train to the east while holding the French at bay in the west. Schlieffen actually carried out a military exercise to test this plan towards the end of 1905. ✅ Zuber concluded that Schlieffen never committed himself to just one plan: he thought war in the west would begin with a French attack and never intended that the Germans should send all their forces into France to destroy the French army in one huge battle. ✅ It was only after the war that the Germans tried to blame their defeat on the rigidity and the constraints of the so-called Schlieffen Plan, which had in fact never existed in the form they tried to make out.

✅ **Fact:** A. J. P. Taylor later questioned rigid blueprint claims, as the source notes in its historian table.

✅ **Fact:** the source converts this into an explicit writing rule in its factual-risk controls: do not present the Schlieffen Plan as an uncontested fixed blueprint, because the folder records the point as disputed; the safe formulation is that German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the safe formulation is safe because it is supported on both sides of the dispute. Whatever the status of the plan as a single document, German troops did enter Belgium, and the operational requirement of speed through Belgium is what the political leadership was told it could not change on 31 July. ⚠️ An answer can therefore use the full political consequence of the plan while remaining agnostic about its documentary status - and saying so in one clause is a visible mark of a prepared candidate.

### 6. Comparison: three positions on the plans

| Position | Held by | Claim | What it explains well | What it cannot explain |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Rigidity as catastrophe | ✅ Ritter | The plan was extremely risky and inflexible and deserved to be seen as the start of disaster for Germany and Europe | Why the operational layer overrode the political layer at the decisive moment | It does not establish intent; a bad plan is not a plot |
| Acceleration | ✅ Taylor | Precise railway timetables accelerated the tempo of events and reduced negotiating time almost to nil | Why diplomacy had no room in the last week of July | It understates deliberate choice; the assurance of 5-6 July was not a timetable effect |
| Documentary scepticism | ✅ Zuber | The plan was one of at least five options; Schlieffen never committed to a single plan; the rigid version was constructed after the war to explain defeat | Why the post-war German account should be treated with suspicion | It does not remove the fact of the Belgian route or Moltke's recorded refusal to improvise on 31 July |

⚠️ These three are not mutually exclusive in the way a first reading suggests. ⚠️ It is coherent to hold that the plan as a single fixed blueprint is a post-war construction, that German planning nonetheless required an attack through Belgium, and that on 31 July the staff refused to improvise. That combined position is the most defensible one available on this evidence and is the one this session teaches.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the generals had taken over, does that not exonerate the politicians?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, for two reasons. First, the political leadership had already taken the decisive political step weeks earlier by giving Vienna an unconditional assurance, and the timetable did not force that. Second, a political leadership that has allowed its only usable military instrument to be built in a single, indivisible form has made a political choice about its own future options. ⚠️ The takeover of 31 July is the consequence of a constitutional and institutional arrangement, not an accident that befell innocent statesmen.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Russia mobilised first, so is Russia not responsible for the trip-wire?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Partly, and the evidence base records the blame position that Russia was the first to order full mobilisation. ✅ But it also records that Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war because troops could be halted at the frontiers, and that it was German planning which treated the Russian order as requiring an immediate attack in the west. ⚠️ The correct formulation is that Russia supplied the occasion for the German trip-wire and German planning supplied the trip-wire itself.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Whether an alternative German deployment was technically feasible in the last days of July cannot be settled from this evidence base. ✅ It records Moltke's claim that there was no time, and it records Zuber's finding that an eastern-transfer option had been exercised in 1905. Those two records do not settle the 1914 feasibility question, and this session leaves it open. ⚠️ A second residual sits inside Taylor's own position: the evidence base records both his argument that railway timetables reduced negotiating time almost to nil and his later questioning of rigid blueprint claims, without recording how he reconciled the two. That tension is displayed rather than resolved, and an answer that uses Taylor should use the timetable argument on its own terms and not as a settled verdict on the plan's status.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source identifies the operational cause as mobilisation plans, especially Germany's move through Belgium, and assigns it the answer function of explaining why a local war became a general war.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "By 1914 military planning had displaced diplomacy." Examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the role of mobilisation plans in the escalation of the July Crisis.
> ⚠️ Account for the outbreak of the First World War with special reference to alliance politics and mobilisation.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use this lesson for the escalation paragraph, never for the origins paragraph. The plans explain the last four days, not the first thirty. ⚠️ And use the safe formulation: German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium. That phrasing is correct under every position recorded here, and it protects the script from the one factual risk the source flags by name.

### Traps

- Writing that the Schlieffen Plan was a single fixed blueprint. ✅ The dispute is recorded, and the source prohibits the uncontested version.
- Writing that mobilisation meant war. ✅ Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war; troops could be halted at the frontiers.
- Attributing the 31 July refusal to the Kaiser. ✅ Wilhelm proposed the partial option; Moltke refused it.
- Dating the plan to 1914. ✅ It is recorded as drawn up in 1905-06.
- Saying Germany attacked France because of a quarrel with France. ✅ Germany at that point had no specific quarrel with France.
- Using the plan to prove deliberate German provocation. Rigidity is not intent.
- Treating Zuber's challenge as settling the matter in Germany's favour. It challenges a document's status, not the Belgian route.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Mobilisation is not war: Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war, because troops could be halted at the frontiers.
2. ✅ The plan known as the Schlieffen Plan is recorded as drawn up by Count von Schlieffen in 1905-06.
3. ✅ Ritter: extremely risky and inflexible; the start of disaster for Germany and Europe.
4. ✅ Plan assumptions: France would automatically join Russia; bulk of German forces by train to the Belgian frontier and through Belgium; France knocked out in six weeks; forces then switched east against a slow Russian mobilisation; success depended on the rapid capture of Liege.
5. ✅ Taylor: precise railway timetables accelerated the tempo of events and reduced negotiating time almost to nil.
6. ✅ 30 July: the Kaiser and Bethmann urged the Austrians to negotiate with Serbia.
7. ✅ Wilhelm proposed partial mobilisation against Russia only, hoping Britain would stay neutral if France were not attacked; Moltke refused - no time to change the railway timetables.
8. ✅ Conclusion recorded: it looks as though the generals had taken over control of affairs from the politicians; and a British announcement on 31 July would have made no difference.
9. ✅ Zuber, *Inventing the Schlieffen Plan*, 2002: at least five alternatives after 1900; an eastern-transfer option exercised in late 1905; Schlieffen never committed to one plan; the rigid version constructed after the war to explain defeat.
10. ✅ Safe formulation required by the source: German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium.
11. ⚠️ The plans belong in the escalation paragraph; they explain the last four days, not the first thirty.
12. ⚠️ The Russian railway programme of 1908 onward attacked the plan's central assumption of slow Russian mobilisation - which is why Lessons 5 and 6 are one argument.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how a military plan became a political constraint in the last days of July 1914, and state precisely what that episode does and does not prove about German responsibility.

**Model answer:** The constraint operated through an indivisible instrument. German planning assumed that France would automatically join Russia, sent the bulk of German forces by rail to the Belgian frontier and through Belgium to knock France out in six weeks, and then transferred them east against a Russian mobilisation expected to be slow; success depended on taking Liege quickly. Because the deployment existed only in that form, the political leadership's preferred option in the last days of July was unavailable. Once Moltke learned of the Russian general mobilisation he demanded immediate German mobilisation; on 30 July the Kaiser and Bethmann-Hollweg urged Vienna to negotiate with Serbia, and Wilhelm proposed a partial mobilisation against Russia alone in the hope that Britain would remain neutral if France were not attacked; Moltke refused on the ground that there was no time to change all the railway timetables. The recorded conclusion is that the generals appeared to have taken control of affairs from the politicians, and a further consequence is that a British declaration of support for France on 31 July would have made no difference, since it was the full plan or nothing even though Germany had no specific quarrel with France. What this proves is that the operational layer overrode the political layer at the decisive moment and that the German political leadership had allowed itself no limited military option. What it does not prove is intent: the same sequence records that leadership trying to avoid war on 30 July, and Zuber's documentary work argues that the single rigid plan was itself partly a post-war construction, with at least five alternatives under consideration after 1900 and an eastern-transfer option exercised in 1905. Rigidity and plot are different charges, and only the first is established by this episode.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "the generals took over" as an exculpation of the civilians. The political leadership gave Vienna an unconditional assurance in early July, weeks before any timetable was engaged, and it had accepted a military instrument built in a single indivisible form. Losing control on 31 July was the consequence of political choices already made, not a substitute for them.

*The next lesson moves from the machinery to the quarrel it was eventually used on. Every structural cause so far - blocs, ships, railways, plans - could in principle have been attached to any dispute. The dispute it was actually attached to arose in the Balkans, and it arose because one small kingdom's national programme and one large empire's survival were the same question asked from two sides.*

### Visual: why one secession is an empire-ending event
