```text
   IT IS A DYNASTIC STATE CONTAINING MANY NATIONALITIES:

     Germans | Hungarians | Magyars | Czechs | Slovaks | Italians
     Poles   | Romanians  | Ruthenians | Slovenes | Serbs | Croats

                                |
        SERBIA'S PROGRAMME: unite all Serbs and Croats into a large
        SOUTH SLAV KINGDOM (Yugoslavia)
                                |
        BUT MANY OF THOSE SERBS AND CROATS LIVE INSIDE THE EMPIRE
                                |
                                v
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  IF THE SERBS AND CROATS LEAVE THE FOLD,                     |
   |  MANY OF THE OTHERS WILL DEMAND INDEPENDENCE AS WELL,        |
   |  AND THE HABSBURG EMPIRE WOULD BREAK UP.                     |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
                                v
        SOME AUSTRIANS CONCLUDE: A "PREVENTIVE WAR" IS NEEDED TO
        DESTROY SERBIA BEFORE SHE BECOMES STRONG ENOUGH TO PROVOKE
        THE BREAK-UP OF THE EMPIRE
                                |
                                v
   FOR VIENNA THIS IS NOT A BORDER DISPUTE OR A PRESTIGE CONTEST.
   IT IS A QUESTION ABOUT WHETHER THE STATE CONTINUES TO EXIST.
```

*The diagram explains the single fact about 1914 that a purely diplomatic account cannot: why a great power was willing to risk a European war over a quarrel with a small neighbour. ⚠️ A state whose principle of cohesion is dynastic loyalty cannot concede a national claim once without conceding it eleven times. That is a structural property of the Habsburg state, not a psychological property of Austrian statesmen, and it is the reason Vienna's behaviour looks disproportionate until you see the mechanism.*

### 1. A state held together by a dynasty, in ordinary language

Imagine a state held together not by a shared language or a shared nation but by a dynasty. Eleven or twelve peoples live inside it; several of them have kinsmen living just outside it in independent states. Now suppose one of those independent neighbours announces, openly, that its purpose is to gather its kinsmen - including yours - into a single national kingdom.

For a nation-state, that is a foreign-policy problem. For this state, it is an existential one, because the principle involved does not stop where the first claim stops. If the South Slavs may leave on the principle of nationality, so may the Czechs, the Poles, the Romanians and the Italians. Conceding once concedes everything.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why "Austria-Hungary was aggressive" is a poor answer and "Austria-Hungary was structurally unable to compromise on this particular principle" is a good one. The first attributes a motive; the second identifies a constraint, and constraints are what examiners reward.

### 2. Nationalism, defined and dated

✅ **Fact:** the source defines the term inside its treatment of the Balkans: nationalism is the desire to free your nation from control by people of another nationality. ✅ It describes Serbian nationalism as probably the most dangerous cause of friction among all the causes it lists.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the definition is worth keeping exactly because it is the definition the topic actually needs. It is a definition about *control by another nationality*, which is why it is explosive inside a multi-national empire and comparatively harmless inside a nation-state. ⚠️ It also explains why Serbian nationalism is graded as more dangerous than, say, French revanchism: a French grievance concerned a province that had changed hands, while the Serbian programme concerned populations inside a neighbouring empire.

### 3. The 1878 grievance

✅ **Fact:** the source records the origin of the Serb grievance precisely. The Serbian nationalists bitterly resented the fact that by the Treaty of Berlin, signed in 1878, the Austrians had been allowed to occupy Bosnia, an area which the Serbs thought should be part of a Greater Serbia.

✅ **Fact:** the status arranged in 1878 is recorded separately and precisely in the Bosnia material: in 1878 the Congress of Berlin had reached the rather confusing decision that Bosnia should remain officially part of Turkey, but that Austria-Hungary should be allowed to administer it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "rather confusing decision" is doing real analytical work and should be reproduced in substance rather than skipped. An arrangement in which sovereignty belongs to one state and administration to another is a stable arrangement only while nobody wishes to test it. It left three parties with a grievance or an incentive - Serbia, which wanted the province; Austria-Hungary, which held it without owning it; and the Ottoman state, which owned it without holding it. ⚠️ Lesson 11 shows exactly how that ambiguity detonated in 1908.

### 4. The Serbian dynastic change of 1903, and why it is the turning point

✅ **Fact:** the source records the political sequence inside Serbia with unusual precision, and it is the part of this topic most often omitted.

| Period | ✅ Recorded position | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| From 1882 | The Serbian government of King Milan had been pro-Austrian | A Serbia aligned with Vienna is not a threat to the Habsburg state; the South Slav question is dormant |
| From 1893 | His son Alexander, who came of age in 1893, followed the same policy | The alignment persists; nationalists regard it as betrayal |
| 1903 | The nationalists saw Alexander as a traitor; in 1903 he was murdered by a group of army officers, who put Peter Karageorgevic on the throne | A violent internal change of regime, carried out by serving officers, alters the foreign policy of the state |
| After 1903 | The change of regime caused a dramatic switch in Serbian policy: the Serbs now became pro-Russian and made no secret of their ambition to unite all Serbs and Croats into a large South Slav kingdom, Yugoslavia | The Austro-Serb antagonism of 1914 begins here, not in 1914 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points follow, and each earns marks.

1. **The Austro-Serb quarrel has a start date, and it is 1903.** ⚠️ An answer that dates it to the assassination has lost eleven years of accumulation.
2. **Serbian army officers had already shown that they would use violence to change their own state's policy.** ⚠️ That fact is relevant to Lesson 13's question about what may be attributed to whom in 1914, because it establishes that an officer network capable of acting outside government control existed in Serbia before the assassination. It is not evidence about any particular individual's involvement in 1914, and this session does not use it as such.
3. **The switch was to a pro-Russian orientation.** ⚠️ This is the point at which the Balkan quarrel acquires a great-power sponsor, and therefore the point at which it becomes capable of producing a European war.

### 5. The Habsburg composition and the domino logic

✅ **Fact:** the peoples of the empire are listed exactly: there were Germans, Hungarians, Magyars, Czechs, Slovaks, Italians, Poles, Romanians, Ruthenians and Slovenes, as well as Serbs and Croats. ✅ Many Serbs and Croats lived inside the borders of the Habsburg Empire, and if they were to break away to become part of a Greater Serbia it would threaten to break up the entire empire. ✅ The mechanism is stated directly: if the Serbs and Croats left the fold, many of the others would demand their independence as well, and the Habsburg Empire would break up.

⚠️ **Analytical inference on the list itself.** "Hungarians" and "Magyars" are not two separate nationalities; they are overlapping labels for the same people, "Magyar" being the name that people uses for itself. The list is reproduced here exactly as the source gives it, because altering a recorded enumeration is a worse fault than repeating its looseness, but an answer should name the Magyars once rather than twice. The substantive point survives either way: the state contained roughly a dozen nationalities and no majority nation.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the same conclusion in its own words - Serbian success threatened not just Austria's prestige but the internal survival of the Habsburg Empire itself - and its evidence bank uses precisely this point as one of the things a balance-of-power explanation cannot absorb.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last observation is the highest-value transfer in this lesson, and it belongs to the owned 2024 question. Balance-of-power theory treats states as units whose concern is their external ratio of strength. Austria-Hungary's concern in 1914 was internal composition. ⚠️ No amount of external equilibrium would have solved the problem of a national principle operating inside a dynastic state, which is why the Habsburg motive is evidence *against* the proposition that the war was fought essentially for the preservation of the balance of power.

### 6. The preventive-war current in Vienna, and the Russian counterpart

✅ **Fact:** the source records the conclusion drawn in Vienna: consequently some Austrians were keen for what they called a "preventive war" to destroy Serbia before she became strong enough to provoke the break-up of their empire. ✅ The Austrians also resented Russian support for Serbia.

✅ **Fact:** the Russian side is recorded in the list of frictions: the Russians were suspicious of Austrian ambitions in the Balkans and worried about the growing military and economic strength of Germany.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the phrase "some Austrians". ✅ The account attributes the preventive-war view to a current of opinion, not to the state as such, and this session preserves that precision. ⚠️ It matters for two reasons: it is accurate, and it is the reply to the objection that Vienna had decided on war long before 1914. ✅ Lesson 17 records Williamson's position that the government in Vienna had not taken a decision to attack Serbia before the assassinations, because it had other political priorities - a position that is compatible with a standing preventive-war current and incompatible with a settled state policy of war.

### 7. Comparison: three nationalisms in the pre-war system

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "nationalism caused the war" is another undifferentiated claim, and the differences between cases decide its strength.

| Case | ✅ What it claimed | Target | Why it was or was not explosive |
|---|---|---|---|
| Serbian and South Slav nationalism | Unite all Serbs and Croats into a large South Slav kingdom | Populations inside a neighbouring multi-national empire | ⚠️ Maximally explosive: the claim is directed at the composition of another great power, and conceding it once concedes the principle to eleven other peoples |
| French revanchism | ✅ Resentment at the loss of Alsace-Lorraine to Germany at the end of the Franco-Prussian War of 1871 | A province transferred between two nation-states | ⚠️ Serious but containable: it produced no French war of recovery in forty-three years, and France did not initiate the 1914 crisis |
| German national self-assertion | ✅ Expressed through Weltpolitik and the encirclement complaint | Status and standing rather than territory in Europe | ⚠️ Corrosive of trust but not territorially specific, which is why it generates arms competition rather than a casus belli |

⚠️ The comparison yields the formulation: in 1914 nationalism was dangerous in exact proportion to how far its object lay inside another great power's frontiers. That single sentence converts a vague cause into a graded one.

### 8. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If Serbian nationalism was so dangerous, why did it not produce a general war in 1903, 1908 or 1912-13, when it was equally active?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because a cause and an occasion are different things, and because the great-power response varied. ✅ In 1908 Russia dared not risk war without its allies' support and no help came for Serbia; ✅ in 1913 Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia. ⚠️ Nationalism supplied the standing quarrel in every one of those years; what varied was whether a great power chose to convert the quarrel into a war. That is exactly why this session places nationalism among the structural and regional causes and places the decisive variable in July 1914.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does this not make Serbia responsible, since it was Serbian policy that created the threat?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It makes Serbian policy part of the causal account, which is different from responsibility for the general war. ✅ The recorded facts are that Serbia's aim was openly declared after 1903 and that Russian backing probably made Serbia more reckless in her anti-Austrian policy than she might otherwise have been. ⚠️ But Serbia did not mobilise Russia, give Vienna an assurance, refuse to alter a railway timetable, or invade Belgium. ⚠️ The attribution discipline applies: Serbian policy is a cause of the Austro-Serb war; it is not an explanation of the European war.

**The residual.** ⚠️ This evidence base does not record the internal politics of Serbia between 1903 and 1914 - the relations between the civilian government, the dynasty and the officer networks. ✅ It records only the 1903 regime change and the declared programme. That gap is material to Lesson 13 and is recorded as an open uncertainty rather than filled.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's directive map records that a question on the role of nationalism is testing the isolation of one factor while showing its interaction, and that reproducing the whole causes essay is the wrong response. ✅ Its probable-questions list includes an item on how Balkan nationalism converted a regional crisis into a world war.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How did Balkan nationalism convert a regional crisis into a world war in 1914?
> ⚠️ Examine the role of Balkan nationalism in the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ "The First World War began in the Balkans, but the structure of European politics made escalation increasingly likely." Examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The structure that scores on the "Balkan nationalism" family is a three-step chain rather than a description: the programme (unite Serbs and Croats into a South Slav kingdom) - the target (populations inside the Habsburg Empire) - the consequence (a secession principle that the empire could not concede once without conceding many times, producing a preventive-war current in Vienna). ⚠️ Then add the great-power sponsor: the 1903 switch to a pro-Russian orientation, which is what allows a regional quarrel to reach St Petersburg. ⚠️ Without that fourth step the answer explains an Austro-Serb war and not a European one.

### Traps

- Dating the Austro-Serb antagonism to 1914 or to 1908. ✅ The dynastic change and the policy switch are 1903.
- Confusing the 1878 Treaty of Berlin with the 1908 annexation. ✅ 1878 allowed Austria-Hungary to administer a province that remained officially Turkish; 1908 was the formal annexation.
- Treating "Greater Serbia" and "Yugoslavia" as unrelated. ✅ The recorded ambition was to unite all Serbs and Croats into a large South Slav kingdom, named as Yugoslavia in the source.
- Omitting the Habsburg nationality list and therefore losing the domino mechanism.
- Writing that Austria-Hungary had decided on war before June 1914. ✅ "Some Austrians" favoured preventive war; Williamson's recorded position is that Vienna had taken no such decision before the assassinations.
- Saying "the Serbs" when the evidence supports a statement about a government, a dynasty or a named group.
- Using Serbian nationalism to explain the European war without the great-power sponsorship step.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Nationalism defined: the desire to free your nation from control by people of another nationality.
2. ✅ Serbian nationalism is recorded as probably the most dangerous cause of friction.
3. ✅ Treaty of Berlin, 1878: the Austrians were allowed to occupy Bosnia, which Serb nationalists thought should be part of a Greater Serbia; Bosnia remained officially part of Turkey while Austria-Hungary administered it.
4. ✅ Serbian policy was pro-Austrian under Milan from 1882 and under Alexander from 1893.
5. ✅ 1903: Alexander was murdered by a group of army officers who put Peter Karageorgevic on the throne; Serbian policy switched to a pro-Russian line and an open South Slav programme.
6. ✅ Habsburg nationalities: Germans, Hungarians, Magyars, Czechs, Slovaks, Italians, Poles, Romanians, Ruthenians, Slovenes, Serbs and Croats.
7. ✅ The domino mechanism: if the Serbs and Croats left the fold, many others would demand independence and the empire would break up.
8. ✅ Some Austrians favoured a preventive war to destroy Serbia before she became strong enough to provoke that break-up; the Austrians also resented Russian support for Serbia.
9. ✅ The Russians were suspicious of Austrian ambitions in the Balkans and worried about German strength.
10. ⚠️ Recorded transfer: Serbian success threatened the internal survival of the Habsburg Empire, which is what a balance-of-power explanation cannot absorb.
11. ⚠️ Graded formulation: nationalism was dangerous in proportion to how far its object lay inside another great power's frontiers.
12. ⚠️ Four-step answer chain: programme, target, empire-ending consequence, great-power sponsor.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why was Vienna prepared to risk a European war over a quarrel with a small neighbouring kingdom, when Britain, France and Germany had all settled comparable colonial quarrels without war? Answer in terms of structure rather than temperament.

**Model answer:** Because the quarrel was not about territory or prestige but about the principle on which the Habsburg state existed. The empire contained Germans, Hungarians, Magyars, Czechs, Slovaks, Italians, Poles, Romanians, Ruthenians, Slovenes, Serbs and Croats, and it was held together by dynastic allegiance rather than by nationality. Serbia's declared programme after the dynastic change of 1903 was to unite all Serbs and Croats into a large South Slav kingdom, and many of those Serbs and Croats lived inside the empire's borders. If that claim were conceded, the principle behind it - that a nation should not be governed by people of another nationality - would be available to every other people in the empire, and the recorded expectation was that many of the others would then demand independence and the empire would break up. A colonial dispute could be settled by exchanging territory, as Germany and France did in 1911 when Germany recognised the French protectorate over Morocco in return for two strips of the French Congo, because losing a colony does not threaten the existence of the metropolitan state. Losing Bosnia, or conceding the principle that would follow from losing it, did threaten the existence of Austria-Hungary. That is why some Austrians favoured a preventive war to destroy Serbia before she grew strong enough to provoke the break-up, and it is why the standard formulation that Serbian success threatened not merely Austrian prestige but the internal survival of the empire is the accurate one. The behaviour follows from the structure of the state, not from a national character.

**Misconception to avoid:** Describing the Habsburg Empire as a country with minority problems. It was not a nation-state with minorities but a dynastic state composed of nationalities, and the difference is the whole explanation. A nation-state can absorb a secession and remain itself; this state could not, which is why its leaders treated a border quarrel as a survival question and why an answer that calls them merely aggressive has explained nothing.

*The next lesson supplies the condition that made the Balkans a zone of competition in the first place. Serbia's programme and Vienna's fear both presuppose something: that there was territory in south-eastern Europe whose ownership was open. That was true because a large empire was retreating from it, and the politics of that retreat had a name.*

## Lesson 8 - The Eastern Question: an empire in retreat and the state system that grew in the space

Progress: 8/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Ottoman Empire as "the Sick Man of Europe"; the Balkan states as former Ottoman territory; the Young Turk government of 1908 and its effect on the Bosnian arrangement; the Balkan League's seizure of the opportunity; the Straits and the Dardanelles as the main Russian trade route; German influence in Bulgaria and Turkey and the strangulation fear; the naval agreement of February 1914; why the Eastern Question is the condition rather than the cause

### Visual: the vacuum and the claimants

```text
   THE STRUCTURAL CONDITION OF SOUTH-EASTERN EUROPE, c. 1878-1914

   A LARGE EMPIRE IS RETREATING FROM A REGION IT ONCE GOVERNED.
   "THE SICK MAN OF EUROPE."

        territory whose future ownership is genuinely OPEN
                                |
     +----------+----------+----------+----------+-----------+
     |          |          |          |          |           |
   SERBIA    BULGARIA   GREECE   MONTENEGRO   AUSTRIA-    RUSSIA
   wants     wants      wants    wants        HUNGARY     wants the
   a South   Macedonia  Aegean   coastal      wants to    STRAITS
   Slav                 lands    territory    BLOCK       open and
   kingdom                                    Serbian     Slav
   and an                                     growth      leadership
   outlet
   to the sea
     |          |          |          |          |           |
     +----------+----------+----------+----------+-----------+
                                |
                THE CLAIMS OVERLAP AND CANNOT ALL BE MET
                                |
                                v
   1912 FIRST BALKAN WAR  -> most remaining Turkish territory in
                             Europe is captured
   1913 SECOND BALKAN WAR -> the victors fight over the proceeds
                                |
                                v
   THE RUSSIAN INTEREST THAT MAKES THIS A GREAT-POWER QUESTION
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   THE DARDANELLES ARE THE OUTLET FROM THE BLACK SEA AND THE MAIN
   RUSSIAN TRADE ROUTE. IF GERMANY AND AUSTRIA CONTROL THEM
   THROUGH INFLUENCE OVER BULGARIA AND TURKEY, RUSSIAN TRADE CAN
   BE STRANGLED. RUSSIA THEREFORE READS BALKAN OUTCOMES AS
   QUESTIONS ABOUT ITS OWN ECONOMIC SURVIVAL.
```

*The diagram supplies the answer to a question most scripts never ask: why did great powers care about the Balkans at all? ⚠️ The answer is not sentiment about Slav solidarity, although that existed. It is that an empire's retreat had opened a zone in which no title was settled, and that one of the great powers had its principal trade route running through the exit of that zone.*

### 1. What happens when a large state withdraws from a region

When a large state withdraws from a region over several decades, three things happen. New states appear, and each of them has a maximum claim it regards as just. Those maximum claims overlap, because populations do not sort themselves neatly. And outside powers begin to treat the region as a place where their relative standing can be gained or lost cheaply.

That is the Eastern Question, and it had been running for a century before 1914. What makes the 1908-1914 phase dangerous is that the retreat accelerated, the new states got stronger, and one of the interested great powers had an existential economic stake in the region's exit.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a background condition in the strict sense defined in Lesson 1. It does not explain why war came in 1914 rather than 1912. It explains why the crisis that came arose in the Balkans rather than somewhere else - and an answer that cannot say that has to treat the location of the trigger as a coincidence.

### 2. The Ottoman position as recorded

✅ **Fact:** the source records the status of the Ottoman state in the phrase the other powers used. The Balkan League countries had all, at one time, been part of the Turkish - Ottoman - Empire; now that Turkey was weak, regarded by the other powers as "the Sick Man of Europe", they seized their chance to acquire more land at Turkey's expense, and they soon captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe.

✅ **Fact:** it records the constitutional change of 1908 and its immediate effect on the Bosnian arrangement. In 1908 there was a new government in Turkey, dominated by a group of army officers known as Young Turks, who resented the Austrian presence in Bosnia and were determined to assert Turkish control over the province.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Young Turk government is usually treated as an episode in Ottoman history and dropped from the European narrative. It belongs in the European narrative, because it is the proximate cause of the Bosnian annexation. ✅ The recorded sequence is that the Young Turks' determination to assert control gave the Austrians the chance to get in first, and they announced the formal annexation. ⚠️ In other words: a reform movement intended to strengthen the Ottoman state precipitated the loss of the province it was trying to recover, and in doing so produced the crisis that set the terms of 1914. That is a genuinely instructive chain and it is worth one sentence in an answer.

### 3. The Straits: where a regional question becomes a great-power question

✅ **Fact:** the source states the Russian interest with unusual economic precision. The Russians were worried about the situation in the Balkans, where both Bulgaria and Turkey were under German influence. This could enable Germany and Austria to control the Dardanelles, the outlet from the Black Sea. It was the main Russian trade route, and Russian trade could be strangled - and the account records that this happened to some extent during the war.

✅ **Fact:** it records the consequence for Russian conduct: thus Russia felt threatened, and once Austria declared war on Serbia, saw it as a struggle for survival. ✅ It adds the prestige dimension: the Russians must also have felt that their prestige as leader of the Slavs would suffer if they failed to support Serbia.

✅ **Fact:** the account records a specific 1914 instrument arising from this anxiety. In February 1914 the Tsar promised to help the Serbs in the event of an attack by Austria-Hungary, and signed a naval agreement with Britain which, it was hoped, would help safeguard Russian access to the Mediterranean if the Germans and Turks should ever try to block the Dardanelles.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the highest-yield paragraphs in the session, for three reasons.

1. **It converts "Russian prestige" into a material interest.** ⚠️ Prestige alone is a weak explanation of why a state would risk everything; a trade artery is a strong one. The recorded evidence supports both, and an answer that gives both is stronger than one that gives either.
2. **It dates a Russian commitment to Serbia to February 1914 - before the assassination.** ⚠️ That is important for Lesson 17, because it is the evidentiary basis of Mulligan's argument that Russian policy shifts helped produce the German decision.
3. **It supplies the reply to "why did Russia not back down again after 1908?"** ⚠️ Because by 1914 the question had been reframed from Slav leadership to trade survival, and because a commitment had already been given.

### 4. The Eastern Question and the Balkan state system, joined

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lessons 7 and 8 describe the same region through two different lenses, and the combination is what an examiner is testing when a question pairs "Balkan" with "world war".

| Lens | What it sees | What it explains | What it cannot explain alone |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nationalism (Lesson 7) | A South Slav programme directed at populations inside a multi-national empire | Why Vienna treated Serbia as an existential problem | Why any great power outside Vienna had to care |
| The Eastern Question (Lesson 8) | A retreating empire, open title to territory, and a trade artery at the exit of the Black Sea | Why Russia, Germany and Austria-Hungary all had stakes in Balkan outcomes | Why the particular quarrel was Austro-Serb rather than, say, Russo-Bulgarian |
| The two together | A region where every local settlement is simultaneously a national claim and a great-power ledger entry | Why a Balkan crisis was the most likely place for a general war to begin | Still not the timing: that requires the July decisions |

### 5. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the Eastern Question had been running for a century, why did it produce a world war only in 1914?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because two of its parameters changed in the six years before 1914. ✅ First, the retreat accelerated: the Balkan League captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe in 1912, which removed the buffer and left the victors facing each other and facing Austria-Hungary directly. ✅ Second, the great-power sponsorship hardened: Russia, humiliated in 1908, rebuilt its army and railways and by February 1914 had given the Serbs a promise of help. ⚠️ A long-running question with a new frontier geometry and a new commitment is a different question.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Is this not simply imperialism under another name, which Lesson 3 downgraded?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, and the distinction is precise. Colonial rivalry concerned territory outside Europe over which the great powers could trade compensation - and, as Lesson 3 showed, they repeatedly did. The Eastern Question concerned territory contiguous to two great powers, inhabited by populations who were claimed by an independent state and who lived inside an empire, with a trade artery attached. ⚠️ Compensation was not available in that currency, which is exactly why this quarrel could not be settled the way the Moroccan quarrels were.

**The residual.** ⚠️ This evidence base records German influence in Bulgaria and Turkey as a Russian perception and does not describe its extent or its instruments. That is recorded as an open uncertainty; no claim about German policy in Constantinople or Sofia is made anywhere in this session.

### 6. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the subject's revision chart records the Balkans under the pre-war armed peace and treats the Balkan Wars as part of the structural instability. ✅ The source lists Austrian and Russian rivalry in the Balkans among Lowe's sources of friction.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The Balkans were the powder keg of Europe." Examine with reference to the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the decline of the Ottoman Empire for European international relations before 1914.
> ⚠️ Why did the crisis that produced the First World War arise in south-eastern Europe?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use the Eastern Question early and briefly - it is a condition, and conditions belong in the second or third sentence of a causation answer, not in the verdict. ⚠️ The economical formulation is one sentence: a retreating Ottoman Empire left open title in a region where a national programme, a dynastic empire's survival and a great power's trade artery all intersected. ⚠️ Then move to the decisions.

### Traps

- Treating the Balkans as a "side-show". ✅ The source records this as an explicit misconception; the Balkan question was the immediate ignition zone.
- Presenting Ottoman decline as a nineteenth-century matter with no 1914 relevance. ✅ The Young Turk government of 1908 is the proximate cause of the annexation crisis.
- Reducing Russian involvement to Slav sentiment. ✅ The Dardanelles were the outlet from the Black Sea and the main Russian trade route.
- Omitting the February 1914 promise to Serbia and the naval agreement with Britain, which date Russian commitment before the assassination.
- Confusing the Straits question with the Bosnian question. They are different objects with different claimants.
- Making claims about German policy in Bulgaria or Turkey beyond the recorded Russian perception of influence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Balkan League states - Serbia, Greece, Montenegro and Bulgaria - had all at one time been part of the Turkish or Ottoman Empire.
2. ✅ Turkey was regarded by the other powers as "the Sick Man of Europe".
3. ✅ 1908: a new Turkish government dominated by army officers known as Young Turks resented the Austrian presence in Bosnia and was determined to assert Turkish control.
4. ✅ That determination gave the Austrians the chance to get in first with formal annexation.
5. ✅ In 1912 the Balkan League soon captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe.
6. ✅ Russia feared that Bulgaria and Turkey under German influence could let Germany and Austria control the Dardanelles.
7. ✅ The Dardanelles were the outlet from the Black Sea and the main Russian trade route; Russian trade could be strangled, and this happened to some extent during the war.
8. ✅ Russia also feared losing prestige as leader of the Slavs, and once Austria declared war on Serbia saw the matter as a struggle for survival.
9. ✅ February 1914: the Tsar promised to help the Serbs in the event of an Austro-Hungarian attack and signed a naval agreement with Britain to safeguard access to the Mediterranean.
10. ⚠️ The Eastern Question is a background condition: it explains where the crisis arose, not when.
11. ⚠️ Two parameters changed before 1914: the retreat accelerated in 1912, and Russian sponsorship hardened into a dated promise in February 1914.
12. ⚠️ Compensation logic worked in Morocco and could not work in the Balkans, because the currency of compensation was not available.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks why the crisis that produced a world war arose in south-eastern Europe rather than in Africa, where the great powers had competed more directly. Answer it using the structural difference between the two arenas.

**Model answer:** The difference is that African disputes were settleable in a currency the powers possessed and Balkan disputes were not. In Africa the object was territory outside Europe over which compensation could be exchanged, and the record shows exactly that: the Moroccan crises of 1905-06 and 1911 both ended in negotiated outcomes, the second with Germany recognising the French protectorate over Morocco in return for two strips of the French Congo, and in early July 1914 Britain and Germany had just reached an agreement favourable to Germany over a possible partition of Portuguese colonies in Africa. In south-eastern Europe the objects were not exchangeable. A retreating Ottoman Empire, regarded by the other powers as the Sick Man of Europe, had left open title to territory that new states claimed as national patrimony; Serbia's claim after 1903 was to unite Serbs and Croats into a South Slav kingdom, which required populations living inside Austria-Hungary, so the claim could not be satisfied by any compensation Vienna was able to offer without conceding a principle that would dissolve its own state. At the same time Russia's principal trade route ran through the Dardanelles, the outlet from the Black Sea, and Russia feared that German influence over Bulgaria and Turkey could let Germany and Austria close it and strangle Russian trade - an interest that no colonial exchange could compensate either. The region therefore combined a non-negotiable national claim, an existential imperial interest and a great power's economic lifeline in one small space, and that is why the crisis arose there.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the phrase "powder keg of the Balkans" as an explanation. It is a description, and used alone it implies that the region was inherently violent. The analytical content is the structural point: an imperial retreat created open title, the claims that filled the space were mutually exclusive, and two great powers had stakes that could not be bought out. Nothing about the peoples of the region explains the outcome; the configuration does.

*The next lesson turns from the international arena to the inside of the states themselves. Three of the arguments already met - the preventive-war deadline, the Russian shift after 1911 and the economic interpretation - all make claims about what was happening inside governments and societies. Those claims deserve to be examined on their own evidence rather than assumed.*

## Lesson 9 - Domestic politics and the economic setting: did governments want a foreign success?

Progress: 9/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the German Socialist Party's result in the 1912 elections; the Reichstag's vote of no confidence in January 1914 and the Kaiser's final say; the Fischer claim that war was a way of dealing with domestic tensions; the Muller diary and the meeting of 8 December 1912; Koch's objection; the Russian internal position after 1905, Stolypin's assassination in 1911 and the rise of jingoistic opinion; Durnovo's warning; the economic-rivalry interpretation and its recorded rebuttal; how to use domestic explanations without determinism

*The diagram shows why this material has to be taught as an argument. ⚠️ Every element on it is recorded: the election result, the vote of no confidence, the constitutional outcome, the diary, the remark, the objection, the Russian sequence and the warning. What is not recorded is a decision taken for domestic reasons. The evidence establishes motive and opportunity; it does not, by itself, establish the act.*

### 1. The trouble with domestic explanations

There is a familiar claim that governments start foreign wars to solve domestic problems. It is attractive because it is cynical and because it occasionally happens.

The difficulty is that it is very easy to assert and very hard to prove. Almost every government has domestic problems; if domestic problems explained war, there would be permanent war. To make the argument work you need three things: a documented internal threat to the government, a documented belief that a foreign success would relieve it, and a connection between that belief and the actual decision.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** for Germany this evidence base supplies the first two and leaves the third contested. For Russia it supplies a documented shift in the political climate and a documented warning against war from inside the government - which points in the opposite direction. ⚠️ That asymmetry is the finding, and it is more interesting than either a blanket endorsement or a blanket dismissal.

### 2. The German internal position, recorded exactly

✅ **Fact:** the source records the sequence as part of its exposition of Fischer's thesis. In the elections of 1912, the German Socialist Party - the SPD - won over a third of the seats in the Reichstag, making it the largest single party. Then in January 1914, the Reichstag passed a vote of no confidence in the Chancellor, Bethmann-Hollweg, but he remained in office because the Kaiser had the final say. Obviously a major clash was on the way between the Reichstag, which wanted more power, and the Kaiser and Chancellor, who were determined to resist change.

✅ **Fact:** it records the inference Fischer drew: a victorious war seemed a good way of keeping people's minds off the political problems; it would enable the government to suppress the SPD and keep power in the hands of the Kaiser and the aristocracy.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** connect this immediately to the regime table of Lesson 2, because that is where the argument gets its mechanism. A chancellor who loses a confidence vote and stays in office is, by definition, not accountable to the legislature. ⚠️ That constitutional fact does two things at once: it makes the domestic-diversion argument possible - a government can contemplate such a move without needing parliamentary consent - and it makes the argument necessary to state carefully, because the same fact means the government did not actually need a war to survive the vote. It had already survived it.

### 3. The evidentiary core and the objection to it

✅ **Fact:** the source records the specific evidence Fischer used. Fischer based his theory partly on evidence from the diary of Admiral von Muller, who wrote about a "war council" held on 8 December 1912; at this meeting, Moltke, Chief of the German General Staff, said: "I believe war is unavoidable; war the sooner the better."

✅ **Fact:** it records the counter-position and attributes it. Fischer's claims made him unpopular with West German historians, and another German, H. W. Koch, dismissed his theory, pointing out that nothing came of the "war council".

✅ **Fact:** it also records the reception politics: historians in Communist East Germany supported Fischer because his theory laid the blame on capitalists and the capitalist system, which they opposed.

✅ **Fact:** the source records Koch's position in its historian table as a challenge to the weight given to the 1912 meeting.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the shape of this dispute is worth understanding rather than memorising, because the same shape recurs throughout the topic. Fischer's evidence is a recorded statement of intention; Koch's objection is that no action followed. ⚠️ Both are true. The methodological question - whether a documented intention that produced no immediate action is evidence of policy or evidence of talk - is not resolved by more facts, and an answer that says so is demonstrating historical judgement rather than indecision.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reception detail is also worth one clause, because it warns against reading historiography as pure inquiry. ✅ Fischer was opposed by West German historians and supported by East German historians for reasons connected to what his thesis implied politically. ⚠️ Noting that does not settle who was right; it does show an examiner that the candidate understands that debates about 1914 have never been only about 1914.

### 4. The Russian internal position, which points the other way

✅ **Fact:** the source records the Russian domestic sequence. Russia's defeat by Japan in 1905 sparked off a revolution which severely weakened the government. ✅ Until 1911 the desire for peace was paramount in Russian foreign policy. ✅ In that year the Russian leading minister, Pyotr Stolypin, who favoured peace, was assassinated, and the government began to succumb to the growing jingoistic public opinion which demanded that action should be taken to increase Russian prestige.

✅ **Fact:** it records the diversionary possibility and the counter-evidence together. Possibly the government saw the war as a good idea to divert attention away from domestic problems, though they must also have been aware that involvement in a major war would be a dangerous gamble. ✅ Shortly before the outbreak of war, one of the Tsar's ministers, Durnovo, warned that a long war would put a severe strain on the country and could lead to the collapse of the tsarist regime.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three observations follow.

1. **The Russian case has a documented public-opinion mechanism that the German case does not.** ⚠️ The evidence base records opinion pressing on Russian policy after 1911 and records nothing comparable for the other powers. That asymmetry should be stated rather than smoothed over.
2. **The Russian case contains its own refutation of the diversion thesis.** ⚠️ A senior minister warned before the war that a long war could destroy the regime. A government told that, and proceeding anyway, is not acting on a calculation that war would stabilise it.
3. **The Durnovo warning is the most economical way to signal historical irony in an answer.** ✅ It was recorded before the war and it describes what happened. ⚠️ One clause is enough; it must not become a digression into 1917, which belongs to another owner.

### 5. The economic interpretation and its recorded rebuttal

✅ **Fact:** the source states the interpretation. It has been argued that the desire for economic mastery of the world caused German businessmen and capitalists to want war with Britain, which still owned about half the world's tonnage of merchant ships in 1914. Marxist historians like this theory because it puts the blame for the war on the capitalist system.

✅ **Fact:** it states the rebuttal. Critics of the theory point out that Germany was already well on the way to economic victory; one leading German industrialist remarked in 1913: "Give us three or four more years of peace and Germany will be the unchallenged economic master of Europe." On this argument, the last thing Germany needed was a major war.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the same item in its comparison table: a Marxist explanation exists, the source treats it cautiously, and its best use is as a contrastive point.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the rebuttal is stronger than it first appears because it is internal rather than external. It does not assert that economic motives are unimportant; it accepts the premise that Germany sought economic mastery and shows that the premise points away from war. ⚠️ That is the most persuasive form of counter-argument available in an essay, and it is worth naming the move: concede the motive, deny the inference.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is also a tension inside the German evidence that a careful answer can exploit. The economic argument says Germany needed only time; the military argument of Lesson 5 says the German general staff believed time was running out. ⚠️ Those two beliefs coexisted in the same state, held by different institutions, and the state acted on the second. That observation - that the civilian-economic and military-strategic clocks in Germany ran in opposite directions - is a genuinely high-level point and can be made in one sentence.

### 6. Comparison: four domestic arguments, scored

| Argument | ✅ Recorded evidence for | ✅ Recorded evidence against | ⚠️ Verdict for an answer |
|---|---|---|---|
| Germany: war as an answer to the Reichstag problem | SPD largest party after 1912; no-confidence vote of January 1914 survived because the Kaiser had the final say; Fischer's reading; the Muller diary and Moltke's 8 December 1912 remark | Koch: nothing came of the war council; the Chancellor had already survived the vote without a war | State it as a contested argument with dated evidence; do not assert it as fact |
| Germany: economic mastery required war | The interpretation exists and is recorded | The 1913 industrialist's remark; Germany already well on the way to economic victory | Concede the motive, deny the inference |
| Russia: war as a diversion from domestic problems | Possibly the government saw war as a good diversion; the government was weakened after 1905 | Durnovo's warning that a long war could bring down the regime | Weak; the internal counsel ran the other way |
| Russia: public opinion pushed policy | Stolypin's assassination in 1911; the government beginning to succumb to jingoistic opinion demanding prestige | None recorded here | The strongest documented public-opinion mechanism in this evidence base |

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Domestic explanations are unfalsifiable: every government has internal trouble, so a determined historian can always find some.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The objection is a good methodological instinct and this lesson accepts most of it. That is precisely why the standard applied here has three parts - documented threat, documented belief, documented connection to the decision - and why the German case is reported as contested rather than established. ⚠️ Note also that the objection cuts both ways: the fact that domestic explanations are easy to assert does not make them false, and in the German case the constitutional evidence of a chancellor surviving a no-confidence vote is not a vague impression but a dated fact with a specific implication.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does any of this bear on the actual July decisions?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Directly in one case. ✅ The recorded German reasoning about preventive war in early 1914 links the army's finance problem - the Reichstag's refusal to sanction the necessary tax increases - to the conclusion that war must come before the end of 1914. ⚠️ That is a domestic constraint producing an external deadline, and it is the one place in this evidence base where internal politics is demonstrably connected to the timing of the crisis.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Unsettled here: whether any political leader in any capital actually chose war in July 1914 for a domestic reason. ✅ The evidence base records the motives, the opportunity and the contested interpretation; it does not record such a decision. That gap is stated rather than bridged.

### 8. UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** domestic material is where a 20-mark answer earns its extra five marks over a 15-mark answer. ✅ The source's mark-scaled structure reserves the widening of scope for the 20-mark version, and the domestic and regime dimension is exactly the kind of widening it means. ✅ The source's evidence bank also lists domestic and regime pressures as one of the things balance-of-power explanation cannot see, because balance-of-power theory treats states as unitary.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The First World War was the product of domestic crises in the states that fought it." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the economic interpretation of the origins of the First World War.
> ⚠️ To what extent was Germany responsible for the outbreak of the First World War?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Deploy this material as a qualifying layer, never as a thesis. The structure that works is: state the recorded domestic facts for one state; state the recorded objection; then make the transfer point - that a unitary, external, balance-of-power account cannot accommodate any of this, which is why the domestic layer belongs in the answer even when it is not decisive.

### Traps

- Asserting that Germany went to war to suppress the SPD. ✅ That is Fischer's reading of a motive, and Koch's objection is recorded against it.
- Saying the Reichstag brought down Bethmann-Hollweg. ✅ It passed a vote of no confidence and he remained in office because the Kaiser had the final say.
- Dating the SPD result or the confidence vote loosely. ✅ Elections of 1912; vote of no confidence in January 1914.
- Presenting the 8 December 1912 meeting as a decision for war. ✅ Koch's recorded objection is that nothing came of it.
- Using the economic interpretation without the 1913 industrialist's remark, or the remark without the interpretation.
- Claiming Russian public opinion forced the Tsar's hand. ✅ The recorded formulation is that the government began to succumb to growing jingoistic public opinion after 1911.
- Extending Durnovo's warning into a narrative of 1917. That belongs to the Russian Revolution owner.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1912 elections: the SPD won over a third of Reichstag seats and became the largest single party.
2. ✅ January 1914: the Reichstag passed a vote of no confidence in Bethmann-Hollweg; he remained in office because the Kaiser had the final say.
3. ✅ Fischer's domestic reading: a victorious war would keep minds off political problems, allow suppression of the SPD and keep power with the Kaiser and aristocracy.
4. ✅ Evidence used: Admiral von Muller's diary on a "war council" of 8 December 1912, where Moltke said war was unavoidable and the sooner the better.
5. ✅ Koch's objection: nothing came of the war council.
6. ✅ East German historians supported Fischer because the thesis blamed capitalism; West German historians largely opposed him.
7. ✅ Russia: the 1905 defeat sparked a revolution that severely weakened the government; until 1911 the desire for peace was paramount.
8. ✅ 1911: Stolypin, who favoured peace, was assassinated; the government began to succumb to growing jingoistic opinion demanding prestige.
9. ✅ Durnovo warned shortly before the war that a long war would strain the country severely and could lead to the collapse of the tsarist regime.
10. ✅ Economic interpretation: desire for economic mastery; Britain still owned about half the world's tonnage of merchant ships in 1914; favoured by Marxist historians.
11. ✅ Rebuttal: a leading German industrialist said in 1913 that three or four more years of peace would make Germany the unchallenged economic master of Europe.
12. ⚠️ Germany's two clocks ran in opposite directions: the economic clock said wait, the military clock said act before the end of 1914 - and the state acted on the second.
13. ⚠️ Transfer point: none of this is visible to a balance-of-power account, which treats states as unitary.

### Concept check

**Question:** Evaluate the claim that the German government went to war in 1914 to solve a domestic political problem. Set out the evidence for it, the evidence against it, and the standard by which you would decide.

**Model answer:** The evidence for it is dated and specific. In the 1912 elections the Socialist Party won over a third of the Reichstag seats and became the largest single party; in January 1914 the Reichstag passed a vote of no confidence in Chancellor Bethmann-Hollweg, who nevertheless remained in office because the Kaiser had the final say, which shows both that a serious constitutional clash was building and that the executive was not accountable to the legislature. On that basis Fischer argued that a victorious war would keep people's minds off political problems, allow the suppression of the Socialists and preserve power in the hands of the Kaiser and the aristocracy, and he supported the reading with Admiral von Muller's diary account of a meeting on 8 December 1912 at which Moltke said war was unavoidable and the sooner the better. The evidence against is also specific. Koch dismissed the thesis on the ground that nothing came of that meeting. The Chancellor had already survived the confidence vote without needing a war, which weakens the claim that war was required to protect the executive. And the economic version of the domestic argument points the other way, since a leading German industrialist said in 1913 that three or four more years of peace would make Germany the unchallenged economic master of Europe. The standard for deciding should be a three-part test: a documented internal threat, a documented belief that a foreign success would relieve it, and a documented connection between that belief and the actual decision. The first two are met; the third is not, because no July decision is recorded as taken for a domestic reason. The defensible verdict is therefore that domestic politics supplied motive and opportunity and is a legitimate part of a full explanation, but that it has not been shown to be the operative reason for the decisions of July 1914 - with the one exception that the Reichstag's refusal of the tax increases the army wanted fed directly into the general staff's conclusion that war must come before the end of 1914.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "domestic causes" as a more sophisticated alternative to diplomatic causes, to be preferred because it sounds structural. The evidence here supports a layered account, not a substitution: the internal position of the German executive is part of the explanation of its freedom of action and part of the explanation of its financial constraint, and neither of those is the same as a decision for war taken to solve an internal problem.

*The next lesson begins the sequence of crises. Everything taught so far - blocs, ships, plans, nationalism, the Eastern Question, domestic strain - was in place by 1905. What happened next was a series of tests, and the first of them was fought out over a country in north-west Africa that none of the disputants intended to fight for.*

### Visual: the same object, twice, with a hinge in between

```text
                                |

         French troops occupy FEZ to put down a rebellion against

         -> Britain fears Agadir as a naval base threatening its

         -> LLOYD GEORGE at the MANSION HOUSE warns Germany that
            Britain will not be taken advantage of "where her

         -> SETTLEMENT: Germany recognises the French protectorate
            over Morocco in return for TWO STRIPS OF THE FRENCH
            CONGO

   TWICE THE ENTENTE WAS TIGHTENED INSTEAD.
```

*The diagram states the lesson's argument in one line at the bottom. ⚠️ Both Moroccan crises were German initiatives whose purpose was to demonstrate that the Entente was hollow. Both ended with the Entente demonstrably firmer - the first producing military conversations, the second producing a public British warning. ⚠️ A policy that produces the opposite of its object twice is worth analysing rather than narrating, and the analysis is the answer.*

### 1. Why two great powers nearly fought over Morocco

Why would two great powers nearly fight over Morocco, a country neither of them regarded as vital?

Because Morocco was not the object. The object was the Entente. Germany's reasonable assumption in 1905 was that an understanding between Britain and France, two states with a long history of hostility, would not survive a serious test. Testing it was a rational thing to do, and the cost of being wrong looked low - a diplomatic setback at worst.

The cost turned out to be high, because the test produced the opposite of the expected result, and because a failed test is not neutral: it converts an informal understanding into a demonstrated one. ⚠️ By trying twice to prove that the Entente was not real, German policy did more than any Anglo-French negotiation to make it real.

### 2. The First Moroccan Crisis, 1905-06

✅ **Fact:** the source records the setting and the German purpose. This was an attempt by the Germans to expand their empire and to test the recently signed Anglo-French Entente Cordiale of 1904, with its understanding that France would recognise Britain's position in Egypt in return for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco - one of the few remaining areas of Africa not controlled by a European power.

✅ **Fact:** the German move is recorded. The Germans announced that they would assist the Sultan of Morocco to maintain his country's independence, and demanded an international conference to discuss its future. ✅ A conference was duly held at Algeciras in southern Spain in January 1906.

✅ **Fact:** the British calculation is recorded. The British believed that if the Germans had their way it would lead to virtual German control of Morocco; this would be an important step on the road to German diplomatic domination and would encourage them to press ahead with their Weltpolitik. The British, who had just signed the Entente Cordiale with France, were determined to lead the opposition to Germany at the conference.

✅ **Fact:** the German expectation and the outcome are recorded together. The Germans did not take the Entente seriously because there was a long history of hostility between Britain and France. But to the amazement of the Germans, Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain supported the French demand to control the Moroccan bank and police. It was a serious diplomatic defeat for the Germans, who realised that the new line-up of Britain and France was a force to be reckoned with, especially as the crisis was soon followed by Anglo-French military conversations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points repay attention.

1. **Italy voted with France.** ⚠️ Italy was a member of the Triple Alliance. Its conduct at Algeciras is early evidence for the point Lesson 4 made with the 1915 entry: the blocs were not what the map implied.
2. **The military conversations are the real outcome.** ⚠️ A diplomatic defeat is embarrassing; staff conversations are structural. They are the beginning of the process by which an entente acquires military content without acquiring a treaty - which is exactly the ambiguity that makes British policy in July 1914 so difficult to read.
3. **The German objective was rational and the execution self-defeating.** ⚠️ This is the formulation to carry: testing an adversary's coalition is normal statecraft; testing it in a way that invites the coalition to demonstrate itself publicly is a mistake, and Germany made it twice.

### 3. The hinge: the Anglo-Russian agreement of 1907

✅ **Fact:** the source records the German reading first and the reality second. The agreement was regarded by the Germans as another hostile move. In fact it was a logical step, given that in 1894 Russia had signed an alliance with France, Britain's new partner in the Entente Cordiale.

✅ **Fact:** the reversal in British attitudes is explained. For many years the British had viewed Russia as a disgraceful example of corrupt, anti-democratic aristocratic government, and worse still the Russians were seen as a major threat to British interests in the Far East and India. However, the situation had recently changed: Russia's defeat by Japan in the war of 1904-05 seemed to suggest that the Russians were no longer much of a military threat; the outbreak of revolution in Russia in January 1905 had weakened the country internally; and the Russians were keen to end the long-standing rivalry and anxious to attract British investment for their industrial modernisation programme. ✅ In October 1905, when the Tsar granted the Russian people freedom of speech and the right to have an elected parliament, the British began to feel more kindly disposed towards the tsarist system.

✅ **Fact:** the content and the character of the agreement are recorded. It made agreement possible, and the two governments were able to settle their remaining differences in Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet. It was not a military alliance and not necessarily an anti-German move, but the Germans saw it as confirmation of their fears that Britain, France and Russia were planning to encircle them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the clearest case in the topic of a perception gap doing causal work, and it should be presented as such. ⚠️ The instrument settled colonial disputes in Asia and contained no military obligation. The German reading of it was that encirclement was now complete. Both statements are true, and the second is what shaped policy. ⚠️ That is worth generalising in one sentence: in this system, what a state believed about an instrument mattered more than what the instrument said - which is precisely the kind of observation that Lesson 18's treatment of misperception needs.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice also the chain running through Russia's defeat by Japan. ✅ That defeat weakened Russia militarily, sparked a revolution that weakened it internally, and made Britain willing to settle with it. ⚠️ A single event outside Europe therefore reshaped the European alignment - which is a useful, compact demonstration that the pre-war system was already global.

### 4. The Agadir Crisis, 1911

✅ **Fact:** the occasion is recorded. This crisis was caused by further developments in the situation in Morocco. French troops occupied Fez, the Moroccan capital, to put down a rebellion against the Sultan. It looked as if the French were about to annex Morocco.

✅ **Fact:** the German move and the British anxiety are recorded. The Germans sent a gunboat, the Panther, to the Moroccan port of Agadir, hoping to pressurise the French into giving Germany compensation, perhaps the French Congo. The British were worried in case the Germans acquired Agadir, which could be used as a naval base from which to threaten Britain's trade routes.

✅ **Fact:** the British intervention is recorded precisely. In order to strengthen French resistance, Lloyd George, Britain's Chancellor of the Exchequer, used a speech which he was due to make at the Lord Mayor of London's banquet at the Mansion House to warn the Germans off. He said that Britain would not stand by and be taken advantage of "where her interests were vitally affected".

✅ **Fact:** the settlement is recorded. The French stood firm, making no major concessions, and eventually the German gunboat was removed. The Germans agreed to recognise the French protectorate over Morocco - the right to protect the country from foreign intervention - in return for two strips of territory in the French Congo.

✅ **Fact:** the consequence is recorded. This was seen as a triumph for the Entente powers, but in Germany public opinion became intensely anti-British, especially as the British were drawing slowly ahead in the naval race: at the end of 1911 they had built eight of the new and more powerful Dreadnought-type battleships, compared with Germany's four.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Agadir settlement is the single best demonstration of the argument of Lesson 3 and the single best set-up for the argument of Lesson 8. ⚠️ A colonial dispute between two great powers, conducted with a warship in a foreign port and a public warning from a third power, was resolved by an exchange of territory. That is what a settleable dispute looks like. ⚠️ Nothing in the Balkan quarrels of 1908-14 was settleable in that currency, and stating the contrast in one sentence is worth more than narrating either crisis at length.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note also what Agadir did to opinion rather than to policy. ✅ The recorded outcome is that German public opinion became intensely anti-British. ⚠️ The Anglo-German colonial relationship, as Lesson 3 showed, was good again by July 1914; the *mood* created in 1911 was not repaired that quickly. Distinguishing a settled dispute from a persisting resentment is exactly the stock-and-flow distinction met in Lesson 5.

### 5. Comparison: what each crisis changed

| Crisis | ✅ Immediate outcome | ⚠️ Structural effect | ⚠️ Effect on the 1914 question |
|---|---|---|---|
| Morocco 1905-06 | Conference at Algeciras, January 1906; Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain support France; German diplomatic defeat | The Entente is tested publicly and holds; Anglo-French military conversations begin | Britain acquires informal military expectations towards France that are not a treaty - the ambiguity of July 1914 |
| Anglo-Russian agreement 1907 | Persian, Afghan and Tibetan differences settled; not a military alliance | The three Entente powers are now aligned across Europe and Asia | German encirclement perception is confirmed, feeding the preventive-war argument |
| Agadir 1911 | Panther withdrawn; French protectorate recognised; two strips of the French Congo to Germany | A second failed test; a public British warning; the naval count is running against Germany | German opinion turns intensely anti-British; but the colonial dispute itself is settled, which is why colonial rivalry is not the 1914 cause |

⚠️ **The row to remember is the last column of row one.** The military conversations that began after 1906 are the reason British policy in July 1914 was genuinely uncertain even to its own participants: France had expectations, Britain had no obligation, and Germany had to guess. ⚠️ That uncertainty is the factual basis of the recorded blame position that Britain should have spoken more clearly - and Lesson 6 supplies the reply to it.

### 6. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If both Moroccan crises were settled, do they not prove that the system worked - and therefore that 1914 was an aberration rather than a culmination?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Partly yes, and the owners say so. ✅ The source lists the Moroccan Crisis of 1905-06 and Agadir 1911 as evidence of a functioning balance system, and draws the inference explicitly: it shows that the system did not require war. ⚠️ But the crises had a cumulative cost that the settlements conceal. Each one hardened an alignment, confirmed a perception and enlarged a domestic constituency for firmness. ⚠️ The correct formulation is that the system worked in the sense that it produced settlements, and failed in the sense that each settlement left the next crisis harder to settle.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Was the German reading of encirclement simply paranoia?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, and saying so is not a concession to German policy. ✅ The facts are that France and Russia were allied from 1894, that Britain and France settled in 1904 and began military conversations after 1906, and that Britain and Russia settled in 1907. ⚠️ A state that finds its three principal rivals successively composing their differences is not irrational to notice. ⚠️ What was not rational was the inference that the only exit was a preventive war - and it is that inference, not the perception, that the responsibility debate turns on.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The extent and content of the Anglo-French military conversations are not recorded in this evidence base beyond the fact that they occurred. No claim about their commitments is made anywhere in this session.

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's evidence bank uses both Moroccan crises as evidence for the balance-of-power reading - as instances of a functioning system - while its second bank uses the contingency of July 1914 against that reading.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the Moroccan crises in the deterioration of European relations before 1914.
> ⚠️ "Every pre-war crisis was contained; 1914 was therefore a failure of statesmanship rather than of structure." Comment.
> ⚠️ How far is it correct to say that the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of balance of power?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ On the owned 2024 question, this lesson is where the *concession* comes from. The two Moroccan crises are the strongest evidence that the pre-war system operated as a balance system and that it produced settlements. ⚠️ Conceding that first, with dates, buys the right to argue afterwards that the 1914 motives were not balance motives. ⚠️ Examiners reward the concession because it shows the candidate is grading rather than asserting.

### Traps

- Confusing the two Moroccan crises. ✅ 1905-06 produced the Algeciras conference; 1911 produced the Panther at Agadir and the Congo exchange.
- Dating Algeciras to 1905. ✅ The conference was held in January 1906.
- Forgetting that Italy supported France at Algeciras despite being in the Triple Alliance.
- Omitting the Anglo-French military conversations, which are the crisis's most consequential outcome.
- Calling the 1907 Anglo-Russian agreement an alliance. ✅ It was not a military alliance.
- Saying Germany gained nothing at Agadir. ✅ It obtained two strips of territory in the French Congo in return for recognising the French protectorate.
- Attributing the Mansion House warning to the Foreign Secretary. ✅ It was Lloyd George, the Chancellor of the Exchequer.
- Treating German encirclement fear as mere propaganda without noting the sequence of 1894, 1904 and 1907 that produced it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Entente Cordiale bargain, 1904: France recognises Britain's position in Egypt; Britain approves a possible French takeover of Morocco.
2. ✅ 1905-06: Germany offers to assist the Sultan of Morocco and demands an international conference.
3. ✅ Algeciras conference, southern Spain, January 1906: Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain support the French demand to control the Moroccan bank and police.
4. ✅ Result: a serious diplomatic defeat for Germany, soon followed by Anglo-French military conversations.
5. ✅ 1907: Britain and Russia settle differences in Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet; not a military alliance; the Germans read it as confirmation of encirclement.
6. ✅ Background to 1907: Russia's defeat by Japan in 1904-05, the revolution of January 1905, Russian desire for British investment, and the Tsar's October 1905 grant of free speech and an elected parliament.
7. ✅ 1911: French troops occupy Fez; Germany sends the gunboat Panther to Agadir seeking compensation, perhaps the French Congo.
8. ✅ Britain feared Agadir as a naval base threatening its trade routes; Lloyd George warned Germany at the Mansion House that Britain would not be taken advantage of where her interests were vitally affected.
9. ✅ Settlement: Germany recognises the French protectorate over Morocco in return for two strips of the French Congo.
10. ✅ German public opinion became intensely anti-British; at the end of 1911 Britain had eight Dreadnought-type battleships to Germany's four.
11. ⚠️ Twice the German object was to break the Entente; twice the Entente was tightened instead.
12. ⚠️ Agadir is the model of a settleable dispute; the Balkan quarrels were not settleable in that currency.

### Concept check

**Question:** German policy in 1905-06 and again in 1911 aimed to demonstrate that the Entente was hollow. Explain why the attempt failed twice, and state what each failure added to the position Germany found itself in by 1914.

**Model answer:** It failed because the test was public and the coalition responded publicly. In 1905-06 Germany announced support for Moroccan independence and demanded an international conference, expecting an understanding between two historic rivals to collapse under pressure; at Algeciras in January 1906 Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain instead supported the French demand to control the Moroccan bank and police, which was a serious diplomatic defeat and was soon followed by Anglo-French military conversations. In 1911, after French troops occupied Fez, Germany sent the gunboat Panther to Agadir to extract compensation, and Britain, fearing a German naval base astride its trade routes, produced a public warning through Lloyd George at the Mansion House that it would not be taken advantage of where its interests were vitally affected; France stood firm, the gunboat was withdrawn, and Germany accepted two strips of the French Congo in exchange for recognising the French protectorate. Each failure added something durable. The first converted an informal colonial understanding into a relationship with military content, which is why British policy in July 1914 carried French expectations without a treaty obligation. The second demonstrated that Britain would speak publicly for France in a crisis, and left German opinion intensely anti-British at a moment when Britain was drawing ahead in capital ships, eight Dreadnought-type vessels to four at the end of 1911. Together with the Anglo-Russian settlement of 1907, which was not a military alliance but which Germany read as the completion of encirclement, the two failures left German policy with the perception that its rivals had composed their differences and that time was working against it - the perception on which the preventive-war reasoning of 1914 was built.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the Moroccan crises as near-misses that happened not to become wars. They were not near-misses; they were tests that produced settlements, including an exchange of territory in 1911. Their significance is cumulative rather than dramatic: each settlement hardened an alignment and confirmed a perception, so that the system kept producing peace while making the next crisis harder to resolve.

*The next lesson moves the crisis sequence from Africa to the region where the war would actually begin. Morocco was a test of a coalition. Bosnia was something different: a crisis in which a great power was humiliated, drew a permanent lesson from it, and rebuilt itself so as never to be humiliated again.*

## Lesson 11 - Bosnia 1908: the crisis that set the terms of 1914

Progress: 11/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the 1878 arrangement and its ambiguity; the Young Turk challenge and the Austrian pre-emption; the annexation as a blow at Serbia; the population of Bosnia as recorded; the Serbian appeal to Russia and the call for a conference; why France and Britain did not support Russia; the Russian retreat and the Austro-German triumph; the two recorded consequences - Serbian hostility and Russian rearmament; why this crisis, not Sarajevo, is where the 1914 question really begins

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 with the Congress of Berlin arrangement, the Young Turk challenge, the formal annexation, the deliberate blow at Serbia, the recorded population composition, the failed appeal power by power and the two recorded consequences

### Visual: a diplomatic victory with two delayed costs

```text
   THE SETTING
   1878 Congress of Berlin: BOSNIA REMAINS OFFICIALLY PART OF TURKEY
   BUT AUSTRIA-HUNGARY IS ALLOWED TO ADMINISTER IT
   ("a rather confusing decision")
```
