```text
   Announce the FORMAL ANNEXATION of Bosnia
                                |
        +-----------------------+------------------------+
        |                                                |
   WHY IT HURTS SERBIA                        THE APPEAL TO RUSSIA
   Serbia had also been hoping to take        Serbs appeal to their
   Bosnia; the province contained about       fellow Slavs, the Russians
   THREE MILLION SERBS among a mixed          Russia calls for a
   population of Serbs, Croats and            EUROPEAN CONFERENCE,
   Muslims                                    expecting French and
                                              British support
        |                                                |
        +-----------------------+------------------------+
                                |
                    THE SUPPORT DOES NOT COME
   Germany makes clear it will support Austria in the event of war
   -> FRANCE DRAWS BACK, unwilling to be involved in a Balkan war
   -> BRITAIN, anxious to avoid a breach with Germany, DOES NO MORE
      THAN PROTEST to Austria-Hungary
   -> RUSSIA, still smarting from defeat by Japan, DARES NOT RISK
      ANOTHER WAR WITHOUT ALLIES
                                |
   RESULT: NO HELP FOR SERBIA. NO CONFERENCE. AUSTRIA KEEPS BOSNIA.
   A TRIUMPH FOR THE AUSTRO-GERMAN ALLIANCE
                                |
        +-----------------------+------------------------+
        |                                                |
   COST ONE                                        COST TWO
   Serbia remains BITTERLY HOSTILE to        Russia is determined to
   Austria - and IT WAS THIS QUARREL         avoid ANY FURTHER
   WHICH SPARKED OFF THE OUTBREAK OF         HUMILIATION: massive
   WAR                                        military build-up, army
                                              modernisation, and
                                              RAILWAY IMPROVEMENT FOR
                                              FASTER MOBILISATION,
                                              intending to be prepared
                                              if Serbia should ever
                                              appeal for help again
```

*The diagram is the most important single causal picture in this session, because everything in July 1914 is visible in it six years early. ⚠️ The quarrel that sparked the war is identified; the Russian resolve that made a second retreat impossible is identified; and the German backing of Austria-Hungary that made Vienna confident is identified. ⚠️ What is missing in 1908 is only the decision, and that is the point: the same configuration produced peace in 1908.*

### 1. The cost of winning a crisis completely

A state wins a crisis completely. It gets the territory, its rival gets nothing, its rival's protector is forced to back down publicly, and its own ally's support proves decisive.

Six years later the same state, in the same quarrel, with the same ally, is at war with the whole continent. What went wrong is not that the 1908 victory was fragile. It is that the victory was total, and totality has costs. A rival that gets nothing has no reason to accept the outcome. A protector that is publicly humiliated rebuilds so as never to be humiliated again, and it also acquires a domestic obligation not to repeat the retreat.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most transferable idea in the lesson, and it is worth stating in an answer in general terms: in a system of standing, a complete diplomatic victory converts an adversary's grievance into a permanent one and a bystander's caution into a commitment. ⚠️ That is exactly what the recorded consequences of 1908 describe.

### 2. The arrangement and the pre-emption

✅ **Fact:** the source records the 1878 position. In 1878 the Congress of Berlin had reached the rather confusing decision that Bosnia should remain officially part of Turkey, but that Austria-Hungary should be allowed to administer it.

✅ **Fact:** it records the 1908 trigger inside the Ottoman state. In 1908 there was a new government in Turkey, dominated by a group of army officers known as Young Turks, who resented the Austrian presence in Bosnia and were determined to assert Turkish control over the province. ✅ This gave the Austrians the chance to get in first: they announced the formal annexation - takeover - of Bosnia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal shape here is worth one clause in an answer because it is counter-intuitive and precise. ⚠️ The annexation was not a long-planned expansion; it was a pre-emption of an Ottoman reform government's attempt to recover a province it legally still owned. ⚠️ Vienna acted quickly because the legal ambiguity of 1878 was about to be tested, and a title held by administration only is worth nothing once the owner asserts itself.

### 3. Why it struck at Serbia

✅ **Fact:** this was aimed at Serbia. It was a deliberate blow at the neighbouring state of Serbia, which had also been hoping to take Bosnia since it contained about three million Serbs among its mixed population of Serbs, Croats and Muslims.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two observations.

1. **The population figure is the only demographic quantity this session uses**, and it is used because the source records it. ⚠️ It matters because it converts the Serbian claim from an abstraction into a specific one: the province at issue contained a large Serb population, and the South Slav programme of Lesson 7 was therefore not a rhetorical ambition but a claim on identifiable people.
2. **The population was mixed.** ✅ Serbs, Croats and Muslims. ⚠️ That is why no settlement of Bosnia on the national principle was available: any allocation would leave large populations on the wrong side of it. ⚠️ Saying this in one clause prevents an answer from implying that the province was simply Serbian and unjustly withheld.

### 4. The appeal, and the reasons it failed

✅ **Fact:** the source records the appeal and the failure of support, power by power.

| Actor | ✅ What it did | ✅ Recorded reason |
|---|---|---|
| Serbia | Appealed for help to their fellow Slavs, the Russians | The province was one Serbia had hoped to take |
| Russia | Called for a European conference, expecting French and British support | The appeal came from the Slav state it patronised |
| Germany | Made clear it would support Austria in the event of war | Alliance backing, given decisively |
| France | Drew back | Unwilling to become involved in a war in the Balkans |
| Britain | Did no more than protest to Austria-Hungary | Anxious to avoid a breach with Germany |
| Russia, again | Dared not risk another war without the support of its allies | Still smarting from its defeat by Japan |

✅ **Fact:** the outcome is recorded flatly. There was to be no help for Serbia; no conference took place, and Austria kept Bosnia. It was a triumph for the Austro-German alliance.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this table is the single most effective refutation of the automatic-alliance model, and it is more powerful than the general statement in Lesson 4 because it shows the mechanism failing in the exact quarrel that later produced the war. ⚠️ France did not support its Russian ally; Britain did not support its Entente partner beyond a protest; Russia abandoned its client. ⚠️ If the blocs had operated as the diagram of two camps implies, 1908 is the year the war should have begun.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note also the asymmetry that the table reveals. The Triple Alliance side acted as a bloc; the Entente side did not. ⚠️ That asymmetry is itself a cause of 1914, because it taught Vienna that firm German backing produced results and taught St Petersburg that its partners would not necessarily appear. Both lessons were applied in July 1914 - and one of them had been overtaken by events.

### 5. The two recorded consequences

✅ **Fact:** the source sets out the costs under a heading of its own. The 1908 outcome was a triumph for the Austro-German alliance, but it had unfortunate results:

- ✅ Serbia remained bitterly hostile to Austria, and it was this quarrel which sparked off the outbreak of war;
- ✅ the Russians were determined to avoid any further humiliation and embarked on a massive military build-up and modernisation of the army, together with an improvement in their railway system to allow faster mobilisation. They intended to be prepared if Serbia should ever appeal for help again.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second consequence is the hinge of the whole session, and it should be traced deliberately.

```text
   1908 RUSSIAN HUMILIATION
        |
        +--> army modernisation
        |        -> by 1914 the German general staff believes
        |           Russia will soon be too strong
        |           -> preventive-war deadline "before the end of 1914"
        |
        +--> railway improvement for faster mobilisation
        |        -> attacks the central assumption of German planning
        |           (that Russian mobilisation would be slow)
        |           -> makes the German timetable more brittle
        |
        +--> political resolve not to retreat again
                 -> in July 1914 the option of a second retreat
                    is not politically available in St Petersburg
```

⚠️ Each of those three branches is separately fatal to a peaceful outcome in 1914, and all three are consequences of a crisis that ended without a shot. ⚠️ An answer that makes this point has explained why a successful containment can be a cause of a later war - which is a genuinely sophisticated claim and is fully supported by the recorded evidence.

### 6. Comparison: 1908 and 1914, the same configuration with a different outcome

| Element | 1908 | 1914 |
|---|---|---|
| The quarrel | Austro-Serb, over Bosnia | Austro-Serb, over the assassination and the ultimatum |
| Austrian action | ✅ Formal annexation | ✅ Ultimatum and declaration of war on 28 July |
| German position | ✅ Made clear it would support Austria in the event of war | ✅ Urged Vienna to attack Serbia and promised help without conditions |
| Russian position | ✅ Called for a conference, then dared not risk war without allies | ✅ Ordered mobilisation, anxious not to let the Serbs down again |
| French position | ✅ Drew back, unwilling to be involved in a Balkan war | Aligned with Russia; ✅ Germany declared war on France on 3 August |
| British position | ✅ Protest only, anxious to avoid a breach with Germany | ✅ Entered on 4 August over Belgium |
| Outcome | Contained | General war |

⚠️ **The comparison is the strongest anti-determinist evidence in the session,** and it is also the strongest evidence that the variable was decision rather than structure. ⚠️ Four of the six rows changed between 1908 and 1914, and every one of the four changed because a government chose differently - Germany from support-if-needed to unconditional encouragement, Russia from retreat to mobilisation, France from drawing back to alignment, Britain from protest to entry. ⚠️ None of those changes was compelled by a treaty.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If Russia's 1908 retreat made a second retreat politically impossible, then Russia's behaviour in 1914 was determined after all - so determinism returns by the back door.

**The reply.** ⚠️ It returns in a much weaker form, and the weakness is the interesting part. What 1908 created was a *high political cost* for retreat, not an impossibility. ✅ The evidence base records that Russian policy until 1911 was dominated by the desire for peace even after the 1908 humiliation, and that the shift came after Stolypin's assassination and the rise of jingoistic opinion. ⚠️ So the cost of retreat was raised in 1908 and became politically decisive only later, through a separate domestic development. ⚠️ Constraints of that kind shape probabilities; they do not remove choices.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Should Austria-Hungary be blamed for creating the quarrel by annexing Bosnia?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The annexation is certainly part of the causal chain, and the evidence base records that the resulting Serbian hostility was the quarrel which sparked off the war. ⚠️ But the annexation was also a pre-emption of an Ottoman attempt to reassert control, and Austria-Hungary had administered the province by international arrangement since 1878. ⚠️ The precise finding is not that Vienna manufactured a quarrel but that it converted an ambiguous title into an outright one at the cost of permanent Serbian hostility - and that it judged that cost acceptable because its ally's support had been decisive.

**The residual.** ⚠️ This evidence base does not record the internal Russian deliberations of 1908 or the terms on which the retreat was accepted, and no claim about them is made here.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source lists the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 in its crisis sequence with the recorded consequences: Austria annexed Bosnia, Serbia stayed bitter, and Russia resolved not to suffer humiliation again. ✅ Its evidence bank uses the Bosnia Crisis as an instance of Balkan outcomes being treated as power transfers, and it uses the Russian resolve as part of the escalation mechanism.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the Bosnian Crisis of 1908 for the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ "The July Crisis of 1914 was the Bosnian Crisis of 1908 with the roles reversed." Comment.
> ⚠️ Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war in 1914 when it had not done so in 1908?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The third framing is the one to prepare for, because it is the question that most cleanly separates strong from average scripts, and this lesson answers it directly through the six-row comparison. ⚠️ In a 250-word answer the comparison can be compressed into three sentences: name the four rows that changed, attribute each change to a decision, and conclude that the difference between 1908 and 1914 lies in the decisions rather than in the structure.

### Traps

- Writing that Austria-Hungary seized Bosnia from Serbia. ✅ Bosnia was officially Turkish and Austrian-administered since 1878; the annexation pre-empted a Turkish attempt to reassert control.
- Omitting the Young Turks and therefore losing the reason for the timing.
- Treating Bosnia as ethnically Serbian. ✅ It contained about three million Serbs among a mixed population of Serbs, Croats and Muslims.
- Saying Russia was betrayed by its allies in 1908 without the recorded reasons: France was unwilling to enter a Balkan war and Britain was anxious to avoid a breach with Germany.
- Presenting 1908 as a near-war. It was a crisis that ended in a complete diplomatic outcome without fighting.
- Omitting the Russian rearmament and railway consequence, which is the single most important effect of the crisis.
- Treating the crisis as a preliminary to 1914 rather than as a structural cause of it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1878 Congress of Berlin: Bosnia remains officially part of Turkey but Austria-Hungary may administer it - recorded as a rather confusing decision.
2. ✅ 1908: a Young Turk government determined to assert Turkish control gave Austria-Hungary the occasion to get in first with formal annexation.
3. ✅ The annexation was a deliberate blow at Serbia, which had hoped to take the province.
4. ✅ Bosnia contained about three million Serbs among a mixed population of Serbs, Croats and Muslims.
5. ✅ Serbia appealed to Russia; Russia called for a European conference expecting French and British support.
6. ✅ Germany made clear it would support Austria in the event of war; France drew back; Britain did no more than protest; Russia dared not risk war without allies.
7. ✅ Outcome: no help for Serbia, no conference, Austria kept Bosnia - a triumph for the Austro-German alliance.
8. ✅ Consequence one: Serbia remained bitterly hostile to Austria, and it was this quarrel which sparked off the outbreak of war.
9. ✅ Consequence two: Russia was determined to avoid further humiliation and began a massive military build-up, army modernisation and railway improvement for faster mobilisation, intending to be prepared if Serbia appealed again.
10. ⚠️ Three branches from the Russian resolve: the German preventive deadline, the attack on the slow-mobilisation assumption, and the political unavailability of a second retreat.
11. ⚠️ Six-row comparison of 1908 and 1914: four rows changed, and every change was a decision.
12. ⚠️ General formulation: a complete diplomatic victory converts a rival's grievance into a permanent one and a bystander's caution into a commitment.

### Concept check

**Question:** Austria-Hungary won the Bosnian Crisis of 1908 outright. Explain how that victory contributed to the general war of 1914, and use the episode to show why the alliance system cannot have functioned as an automatic trigger.

**Model answer:** The victory was complete and its costs were delayed. Austria-Hungary pre-empted a Young Turk government's attempt to assert Turkish control by formally annexing a province it had administered since the Congress of Berlin of 1878, and the move struck directly at Serbia, which had hoped to take a province containing about three million Serbs among a mixed population of Serbs, Croats and Muslims. Serbia appealed to Russia; Russia called for a European conference expecting French and British support; Germany made clear it would support Austria in the event of war; France drew back, unwilling to be involved in a Balkan war; Britain, anxious to avoid a breach with Germany, did no more than protest; and Russia, still smarting from its defeat by Japan, dared not risk war without its allies. Austria kept Bosnia. Two consequences followed that made 1914 possible. Serbia remained bitterly hostile, and that quarrel is the one which sparked off the outbreak of war. And Russia, determined never to be humiliated again, undertook a massive military build-up, modernised the army and improved the railway system specifically to mobilise faster, intending to be ready if Serbia appealed again - which simultaneously produced the German general staff's fear that time was running out, undermined the assumption of slow Russian mobilisation on which German planning rested, and made a second Russian retreat politically far more costly. The episode also disposes of the automatic-trigger model of the alliances, because the quarrel that later produced a world war produced none in 1908: France did not support its Russian ally, Britain did not support its Entente partner beyond a protest, and Russia abandoned its Serbian client. The blocs existed in 1908 exactly as in 1914; they did not behave as a mechanism.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 1908 as a rehearsal that merely raised tension. Its specific outputs are what matter: a permanent Serbian grievance, a Russian rearmament and railway programme aimed at faster mobilisation, and a Russian political commitment not to retreat again. Those are the three things that were different in July 1914, and all three were produced by a crisis that ended peacefully.

*The next lesson covers the last rehearsal, and it is the one that most directly shaped the calculations of 1914. Two short wars redrew the map of south-eastern Europe, left Serbia larger and more confident, left Vienna more determined to stop her, and left Berlin with a misleading impression of what Britain might do.*

### Visual: two wars, one year apart, and three different lessons learned

```text
   1912  FIRST BALKAN WAR
   THE BALKAN LEAGUE = SERBIA + GREECE + MONTENEGRO + BULGARIA
   all of them once part of the Turkish (Ottoman) Empire
   -> attack Turkey, now weak, the "Sick Man of Europe"
   -> SOON CAPTURE MOST OF THE REMAINING TURKISH TERRITORY IN EUROPE
                                |
   THE GREAT POWERS INTERVENE DIPLOMATICALLY, NOT MILITARILY
   Sir Edward Grey, British Foreign Secretary, TOGETHER WITH THE
   GERMAN GOVERNMENT, arranges a PEACE CONFERENCE IN LONDON
   -> anxious to avoid the conflict spreading
   -> and to demonstrate that Britain and Germany could still work
      together
   -> settlement divides the former Turkish lands among the Balkan
      states
                                |
   THE SERBIAN DISAPPOINTMENT
   Serbia wants ALBANIA, which would give an OUTLET TO THE SEA
   -> the Austrians, with GERMAN AND BRITISH support, insist that
      ALBANIA BECOME AN INDEPENDENT STATE
   -> a deliberate Austrian move to prevent Serbia becoming more

                                |
   1913  SECOND BALKAN WAR
   Bulgaria is dissatisfied: it wanted MACEDONIA, most of which went
   to Serbia -> BULGARIA ATTACKS SERBIA
   -> the plan misfires: GREECE, ROMANIA AND TURKEY rally to Serbia
   -> Bulgaria is defeated; TREATY OF BUCHAREST (1913) strips most of
      its gains from the first war
                                |
   WHAT LOOKED LIKE SUCCESS: Anglo-German influence seemed to have
   prevented escalation by restraining the Austrians, who were
   ITCHING to support Bulgaria and attack Serbia
                                |
   WHAT ACTUALLY FOLLOWED - THREE SERIOUS CONSEQUENCES
   (1) SERBIA had been strengthened and was determined to stir up
       trouble among the Serbs and Croats living inside
       Austria-Hungary
   (2) THE AUSTRIANS were equally determined to put an end to
       Serbia's ambitions
   (3) THE GERMANS took Grey's willingness to co-operate as a sign
       that BRITAIN WAS PREPARED TO BE DETACHED FROM FRANCE AND
       RUSSIA
```

*The diagram ends on the consequence that is least often noticed and most important for July 1914. ⚠️ Anglo-German cooperation in 1912-13 was a genuine success of crisis management and it produced a dangerous misreading: Berlin concluded from it that Britain might be separable from its Entente partners. ⚠️ A successful act of diplomacy therefore contributed to a fatal miscalculation two years later, and that is a point examiners reward because it cannot be reached by narration.*

### 1. The closest thing this topic has to a controlled experiment

The Balkan Wars are usually skipped or reduced to a sentence. That is a mistake, because they are the closest thing this topic has to a controlled experiment. Between 1912 and 1913 the region produced exactly the kind of crisis that was supposed to bring the great powers into collision: two wars, a redrawn map, a strengthened Serbia, an Austria-Hungary that wanted to intervene, and a Russia with a client in the field.

Nothing happened to the great powers. The wars stayed local, the powers conferenced in London, and Austria-Hungary was held back by its own ally.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that outcome is evidence for two opposite conclusions and an answer should use both. It is evidence that the system could contain a Balkan war - which is the case against inevitability. And it is evidence that containment was becoming more expensive, because the price of the 1913 containment was a Serbia that had grown, an Austria-Hungary that had been frustrated twice, and a Germany that had drawn a false conclusion about Britain.

### 2. The First Balkan War, 1912

✅ **Fact:** the source records the belligerents and the motive. The war began when Serbia, Greece, Montenegro and Bulgaria - calling themselves the Balkan League - launched a series of attacks on Turkey. These countries had all, at one time, been part of the Turkish - Ottoman - Empire. Now that Turkey was weak, regarded by the other powers as "the Sick Man of Europe", they seized their chance to acquire more land at Turkey's expense. ✅ They soon captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe.

✅ **Fact:** the great-power response is recorded with attribution. Together with the German government, Sir Edward Grey, the British Foreign Secretary, arranged a peace conference in London. He was anxious to avoid the conflict spreading, and also to demonstrate that Britain and Germany could still work together. ✅ The resulting settlement divided up the former Turkish lands among the Balkan states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the London conference deserves to be named in an answer for a reason beyond its content. ⚠️ It is the last occasion on which the great powers successfully convened to settle a Balkan crisis, and it was convened jointly by the two states whose antagonism is usually treated as the war's central axis. ⚠️ An answer that names it has a concrete instance of the system working in 1913, which is worth more than a general assertion that crises had been contained.

### 3. Albania and the outlet to the sea

✅ **Fact:** the source records the Serbian disappointment and the Austrian motive. The Serbs were not happy with their gains: they wanted Albania, which would give them an outlet to the sea, but the Austrians, with German and British support, insisted that Albania should become an independent state. ✅ This was a deliberate Austrian move to prevent Serbia becoming more powerful.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points.

1. **The Austrian objective is stated in the source and should be stated in an answer.** ⚠️ It was not a principled defence of Albanian independence; it was containment of Serbia by other means.
2. **Britain supported the Austrian position.** ⚠️ That is inconvenient for any account that treats the pre-war alignments as two fixed teams, and it supports the argument of Lesson 4.
3. **Serbia was denied the specific thing it most wanted.** ⚠️ Being enlarged and simultaneously blocked from the sea is a combination that produces confidence and resentment at once - which is precisely the temper the third section describes.

### 4. The Second Balkan War, 1913

✅ **Fact:** the source records the cause and the course. The Bulgarians were dissatisfied with their gains from the peace settlement and they blamed Serbia. They had been hoping for Macedonia, but most of it had been given to Serbia. Bulgaria therefore attacked Serbia, but their plan misfired when Greece, Romania and Turkey rallied to support Serbia. ✅ The Bulgarians were defeated, and by the Treaty of Bucharest of 1913 they forfeited most of their gains from the first war.

✅ **Fact:** the apparent success of great-power management is recorded. It seemed that Anglo-German influence had prevented an escalation of the war by restraining the Austrians, who were itching to support Bulgaria and attack Serbia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Second Balkan War is a compact demonstration of why the Eastern Question could not be settled by any single redistribution. ⚠️ The First Balkan War removed the Ottoman presence; the Second Balkan War was fought between the victors over the proceeds within a year. ⚠️ Removing an imperial power from a region of overlapping claims does not resolve the claims; it exposes them.

### 5. The three recorded consequences

✅ **Fact:** in reality the consequences of the Balkan Wars were serious, and three are recorded:

- ✅ Serbia had been strengthened and was determined to stir up trouble among the Serbs and Croats living inside Austria-Hungary;
- ✅ the Austrians were equally determined to put an end to Serbia's ambitions;
- ✅ the Germans took Grey's willingness to co-operate as a sign that Britain was prepared to be detached from France and Russia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these three consequences are the direct inputs into July 1914, and each maps onto a decision taken in that month.

| Consequence of 1912-13 | ⚠️ Where it appears in July 1914 |
|---|---|
| Serbia strengthened and determined to agitate among Serbs and Croats inside the empire | The Austrian conviction that an assassination in Bosnia was an instrument of Serbian policy, and the framing of the ultimatum around Serbian complicity |
| Austria-Hungary determined to put an end to Serbia's ambitions | The decision to treat the assassination as the occasion for settling the Serbian question by force rather than by diplomacy |
| Germany's belief that Britain might be detached from France and Russia | The expectation that a firm line would localise the war, and the failure to weigh British entry until too late |

⚠️ The third row is the most valuable, because it supplies a *documented* origin for the German misjudgement that Lesson 18 treats as the central contingency. ⚠️ It is far stronger to say that Berlin's expectation of British neutrality had an identifiable source in the Balkan diplomacy of 1912-13 than to say, vaguely, that the Germans miscalculated.

### 6. The decisive counter-evidence: German restraint in 1913

✅ **Fact:** the source records, in its assessment of the alliance explanation, that Germany had restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this single fact does more work than any other in the responsibility debate, and it should be deployed deliberately rather than mentioned.

```text
   SUMMER 1913
   Austria-Hungary wants to attack Serbia.
   GERMANY SAYS NO.
   -> no Austro-Serb war
   -> no European war

   SUMMER 1914
   Austria-Hungary wants to attack Serbia.
   GERMANY SAYS YES, WITHOUT CONDITIONS.
   -> Austro-Serb war
   -> European war within nine days

   THE STRUCTURE IS IDENTICAL. THE ALLIANCE IS IDENTICAL.
   THE BALKAN QUARREL IS IDENTICAL.
   THE VARIABLE IS THE GERMAN DECISION.
```

⚠️ This is the cleanest available demonstration that the German assurance of July 1914 was a choice and not a treaty performance, and it is also the cleanest available demonstration that the alliance could restrain as easily as it could enable. ⚠️ The source states the interpretive consequence directly in its later assessment: it is significant that Germany restrained the Austrians from declaring war on Serbia in 1913, but in 1914 encouraged them to go ahead, and the important question is why German policy towards Austria-Hungary changed.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the great powers contained the Balkan Wars, why is the containment not simply evidence that the system was robust?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because containment has outputs as well as an outcome, and the outputs were adverse in every case. ✅ Serbia emerged stronger and more determined; Vienna emerged more determined and twice frustrated; Berlin emerged with a false reading of British policy. ⚠️ A system that keeps producing peace while making each successive crisis harder to survive is not robust; it is running out of margin. ⚠️ That formulation - the system was solvent but its reserves were falling - is the accurate one and is worth a clause in an answer.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the German restraint of 1913 not simply prove Germany's good faith, and thereby undermine the case for German responsibility?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It proves that restraint was available, which is the opposite of exoneration. ⚠️ A state that has demonstrated in 1913 that it can hold its ally back cannot claim in 1914 that it was dragged along by alliance obligation. ✅ The source draws exactly this inference when it treats the change of German policy as the important question and records the interpretations offered for it.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Why German policy changed between 1913 and 1914 is the question the evidence base records as contested, and Lesson 17 sets out the competing answers - preventive timing, deliberate provocation, and a miscalculated attempt at deterrence - without choosing between them here.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source lists the First and Second Balkan Wars of 1912-13 in its crisis sequence, with the recorded outcome that Serbia emerged stronger and Austria became more determined to curb Serbia. ✅ Its evidence bank uses the Balkan Wars as an instance of regional outcomes being scored as power transfers by both sides.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the impact of the Balkan Wars of 1912-13 on the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ "The great powers managed every Balkan crisis until they managed one too many." Comment.
> ⚠️ To what extent was Germany responsible for the outbreak of the First World War?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ In a responsibility answer, the 1913-1914 contrast is the strongest single paragraph available and should be written as a two-sentence comparison rather than as narrative. ⚠️ In an escalation answer, the third consequence - Berlin's belief that Britain might be detached - is the item to carry, because it converts a vague charge of miscalculation into a documented expectation with a traceable origin.

### Traps

- Omitting the Balkan Wars from the crisis sequence. They are the last rehearsal and the most informative one.
- Reversing the two wars. ✅ 1912: the League against Turkey. 1913: Bulgaria against Serbia, with Greece, Romania and Turkey supporting Serbia.
- Forgetting the London conference and Grey's joint arrangement with the German government.
- Omitting Albania, and therefore losing the Austrian containment motive and the denial of a Serbian outlet to the sea.
- Attributing the Treaty of Bucharest to the wrong war. ✅ It ended the Second Balkan War in 1913.
- Omitting the third consequence - the German reading that Britain might be detached from France and Russia.
- Failing to use the 1913 German restraint against the alliance-obligation defence.
- Giving casualty or territorial figures. None is sourced here beyond the recorded outcomes.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Balkan League: Serbia, Greece, Montenegro and Bulgaria, all formerly part of the Ottoman Empire.
2. ✅ First Balkan War, 1912: attacks on Turkey; most remaining Turkish territory in Europe captured.
3. ✅ Sir Edward Grey, with the German government, arranged a peace conference in London, anxious to avoid the conflict spreading and to show Britain and Germany could still work together.
4. ✅ Serbia wanted Albania for an outlet to the sea; Austria-Hungary, with German and British support, insisted on an independent Albania - a deliberate move to prevent Serbia becoming more powerful.
5. ✅ Second Balkan War, 1913: Bulgaria, dissatisfied over Macedonia, attacked Serbia; Greece, Romania and Turkey rallied to Serbia; Bulgaria was defeated.
6. ✅ Treaty of Bucharest, 1913: Bulgaria forfeited most of its gains from the first war.
7. ✅ Anglo-German influence appeared to have prevented escalation by restraining the Austrians, who were itching to support Bulgaria and attack Serbia.
8. ✅ Consequence one: Serbia strengthened and determined to stir up trouble among Serbs and Croats inside Austria-Hungary.
9. ✅ Consequence two: the Austrians equally determined to end Serbia's ambitions.
10. ✅ Consequence three: the Germans took Grey's willingness to co-operate as a sign that Britain was prepared to be detached from France and Russia.
11. ✅ Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War; in 1914 it encouraged the attack. The change of German policy is the question.
12. ⚠️ Formulation: the system remained solvent while its reserves fell; each containment made the next crisis harder to contain.

### Concept check

**Question:** Use the Balkan Wars of 1912-13 to construct the single strongest paragraph available against the defence that Germany was dragged into war by its alliance obligations in 1914.

**Model answer:** The defence fails on a controlled comparison that the evidence base supplies directly. In the summer of 1913, during the Second Balkan War, Austria-Hungary was itching to support Bulgaria and attack Serbia, and Germany restrained it; no Austro-Serb war followed and no European war followed. In the summer of 1914, in the same quarrel, under the same alliance, with the same partner, Germany did the opposite: the Kaiser sent a telegram urging Vienna to attack Serbia and promising German help without any conditions attached, which was like giving the Austrians a blank cheque. The structure was identical, the treaty was identical and the Balkan dispute was identical; the variable was the German decision. That comparison forecloses the alliance-obligation defence, because a state that has demonstrated within twelve months that it can hold its ally back cannot claim that the same relationship compelled it to let the ally go forward. It also shifts the analytical question to the one the evidence itself poses: why German policy towards Austria-Hungary changed between 1913 and 1914 - to which the recorded candidate answers are the preventive-war timing of the general staff, Fischer's claim of deliberate provocation, and the suggestion that Wilhelm II and Bethmann-Hollweg believed a firm line would frighten Russia into neutrality. The defence of compulsion is not among the candidate answers.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Balkan Wars as background colour because no great power fought in them. Their outputs were the direct inputs into July 1914: a strengthened and more assertive Serbia, a twice-frustrated Austria-Hungary determined to end Serbian ambitions, and a German belief - derived from Grey's cooperation over the London conference - that Britain might be detached from France and Russia. The third of those is the documented origin of the expectation that the 1914 war could be localised.

*The next lesson reaches the event itself. Everything so far has been about conditions and rehearsals. On 28 June 1914 a specific thing was done by specific people in a specific street, and the question of who may be held answerable for it - the individuals, an organisation, a government, or a people - is the first and hardest attribution problem in the topic.*

## Lesson 13 - Sarajevo, 28 June 1914: who did what, and what may be attributed to whom

### Visual: five attribution levels, and what the evidence supports at each

```text
   WHAT HAPPENED: on 28 June 1914 at Sarajevo, the capital of Bosnia,
   Archduke Franz Ferdinand - nephew and heir to Emperor Franz Josef -
   and his wife were shot dead while on an official visit.

   LEVEL 1  THE INDIVIDUAL WHO FIRED
            Gavrilo Princip
            EVIDENCE: recorded directly in the source
            ATTRIBUTION: SECURE

   LEVEL 2  THE CONSPIRACY AROUND HIM
            a group of young Bosnian conspirators, one of whom had
            already thrown a bomb at the motorcade earlier that day;
            recruited and organised locally
            EVIDENCE: verified record
            ATTRIBUTION: SECURE AS TO EXISTENCE AND MEMBERSHIP

   LEVEL 3  THE ORGANISATION ACROSS THE FRONTIER
            a secret Serbian nationalist society, "Union or Death",
            commonly called the Black Hand, whose leader held a senior
            position in Serbian military intelligence, supplied arms
            and assistance
            EVIDENCE: verified record
            ATTRIBUTION: SECURE AS TO THE ORGANISATION;
            NOT EQUIVALENT TO THE SERBIAN STATE

   LEVEL 4  THE SERBIAN GOVERNMENT
            EVIDENCE: the source records only that
            "THE AUSTRIANS BLAMED THE SERB GOVERNMENT"
            ATTRIBUTION: NOT ESTABLISHED IN THIS EVIDENCE BASE
            -> this session states the Austrian allegation as an
               allegation and asserts nothing further

   LEVEL 5  "THE SERBS" AS A PEOPLE
            ATTRIBUTION: PROHIBITED. Collective attribution is an
            error of fact, not a matter of taste.
```

*The diagram is the lesson. ⚠️ Almost every weak script on this topic slides from Level 1 to Level 5 in a single sentence, usually through the phrase "Serbia assassinated the Archduke". The evidence supports Levels 1, 2 and 3; it records Level 4 as an Austrian allegation; and Level 5 is never available in historical writing at all.*

### 1. Two shots, and everything after them is attribution

Two people were shot in a street. That much is simple. Everything after it is an attribution question, and attribution questions are where most marks are lost in this topic.

The killer acted with others. Those others were helped from across a frontier by a secret organisation. That organisation's leader held a post in another state's military intelligence. Does that make the other state responsible? A government is not the same thing as an officer in its service, and an officer is not the same thing as a nation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest position is not agnosticism but precision. You can state with confidence who fired, that a conspiracy existed, and that an organisation across the frontier assisted it. You can state that the Austrian government blamed the Serbian government - because that is a recorded fact about Austrian conduct. What you cannot do, on this evidence base, is convert the Austrian allegation into a finding, and what you must never do is convert any of it into a statement about a people.

### 2. What the source records

✅ **Fact:** the assassination is recorded with its date, place and victims. This tragic event took place in Sarajevo, the capital of Bosnia, on 28 June 1914. ✅ It was the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia, which was soon to develop into the First World War.

✅ **Fact:** the victim's position is recorded. The Archduke, nephew and heir to the Emperor Franz Josef, was paying an official visit to Sarajevo when he and his wife were shot dead.

✅ **Fact:** the killer is named. They were shot dead by Gavrilo Princip, whom the source describes as a Serb terrorist.

✅ **Fact:** the Austrian response is recorded as an allegation and a decision. The Austrians blamed the Serb government and sent a harsh ultimatum. The Serbs accepted most of the demands in it, but the Austrians, with a promise of German support, were determined to use the incident as an excuse for war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note the exact phrasing of the recorded causal claim. It is the immediate cause *of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia*, not of the First World War. ⚠️ This session's trigger discipline is taken directly from that sentence, and an answer that reproduces it precisely has already avoided the topic's commonest error.

⚠️ **Analytical inference on the source's description of Princip.** ✅ The source calls him a Serb terrorist. ⚠️ That characterisation is reported here because it is what the source says, and it is not adopted as this session's own classification, because the word describes a political judgement rather than a fact and because the session's attribution discipline requires that individuals be described by what they did. ⚠️ The factual statement this session makes is the one the evidence supports without qualification: Gavrilo Princip shot and killed Archduke Franz Ferdinand and his wife at Sarajevo on 28 June 1914.

### 3. The conspiracy, from the verified record

✅ **Fact:** the shooting was the second attempt of that day; earlier in the morning another member of the group threw a bomb at the motorcade, which failed to kill the Archduke. ✅ **Fact:** the conspirators were a small group of young Bosnians, organised locally by one of their number, and associated with the nationalist movement known as Young Bosnia, whose programme was the liberation of the South Slav lands from Habsburg rule and their unification.

✅ **Fact:** the assistance came from a secret Serbian nationalist organisation formally called Union or Death and commonly known as the Black Hand, which supplied weapons and assistance for the plot, and whose leader held a senior position in Serbian military intelligence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow, and each is load-bearing for the July Crisis.

1. **The plot was cross-border.** ⚠️ Conspirators inside Habsburg territory were assisted from inside Serbia. That fact is what made a demand for Austrian participation in the investigation, which the ultimatum of Lesson 14 contained, politically intelligible rather than merely aggressive.
2. **The assisting body was an unofficial organisation with official penetration.** ⚠️ That is exactly the configuration that makes attribution hard: it is neither a purely private conspiracy nor a state operation, and the 1903 precedent of Lesson 7 - army officers killing their own king and changing their state's foreign policy - shows that such penetration had a history in Serbia.
3. **This is a reason for the Austrian allegation, not a proof of it.** ⚠️ The distinction is the whole of the attribution discipline. An answer may say that the Austrian allegation had a factual basis in the cross-border assistance; it may not say that the allegation was established.

**Control on this section.** ⚠️ This session makes no claim whatsoever about what the Serbian government knew, when it knew it, or what it did or failed to do. ✅ The evidence base records that the Austrians blamed the Serb government and nothing more, and the internal politics of Serbia between 1903 and 1914 are recorded in Lesson 7 as an open uncertainty. ⚠️ No number of conspirators, no count of weapons and no detail of the investigation appears here.

### 4. Why the choice of date and place matters

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the location is not incidental to the causation. The heir to the Habsburg throne was killed in Bosnia - the province annexed in 1908, containing about three million Serbs among a mixed population, and claimed by Serbian nationalists as part of a Greater Serbia. ⚠️ The crime therefore occurred at the precise point where the two structures of Lessons 7 and 11 intersect: the South Slav programme and the Austrian annexation. ⚠️ An answer that says the assassination happened "in the Balkans" has lost that; an answer that says it happened in the province annexed in 1908 has gained it in four words.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the victim also matters in a way that cuts against the simplest reading. Franz Ferdinand was the heir, not the ruler; the decision for war was taken by an emperor and his ministers who had lost a nephew and heir, not a sovereign. ⚠️ The evidence base records nothing about Franz Ferdinand's own policy views, and this session therefore makes no claim about them - including the frequently repeated claim that he favoured conciliation, which is not supported here.

### 5. The current linkage, and what it is used for

📰 **Current linkage.** The assassination remains the subject of an official annual commemoration in the city where it happened. The Museum of Sarajevo reported on 16 June 2026 that an exhibition titled "The Sarajevo Assassination - 112 Years Later", prepared through institutional cooperation between the International Burch University, the Sarajevo Museum and the Sarajevo VRX Museum, would open on Saturday 27 June 2026 as part of the official commemoration of the anniversary. Students of the university's Department of Graphic Design and Multimedia were set the task of creating the official poster and visual identity for the annual commemoration of the assassination of the Austro-Hungarian heir presumptive Archduke Franz Ferdinand and his wife Sophie, carried out in Sarajevo on 28 June 1914 by Gavrilo Princip; an expert jury drawn from the partner institutions selected from fifteen shortlisted posters, and one student's design was chosen as the official visual identity for the 2026 commemoration. The museum's own description of the event's historical significance is that the Sarajevo Assassination served as the immediate catalyst for the outbreak of the First World War.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the linkage is carried for one reason and is used for nothing else. ⚠️ The formulation used by the institution that commemorates the event - immediate catalyst - is the same distinction this session teaches from its historical sources, which is a useful demonstration that the trigger-cause separation is not an examination convention but the standard way the event is described where it is remembered most closely. ⚠️ The linkage supplies **no historical fact** used anywhere in this session, no claim about the conspiracy, no claim about responsibility and no claim about any state. It is the only current item carried anywhere in this file, and the other nineteen lesson checklists record truthfully that no current affairs were sought.

### 6. Comparison: three ways the assassination is described, and what each licenses

| Description | ✅ Source of the phrase | What it licenses you to assert | What it does not license |
|---|---|---|---|
| "The immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia" | The core historical account | That 28 July follows from 28 June | Nothing about August, about Russia, about France or about Britain |
| "The immediate catalyst for the outbreak of the First World War" | The commemorating institution's description, 2026 | That the event began the sequence | Not that it caused the sequence; a catalyst initiates a reaction it does not supply the materials for |
| "The cause of the First World War" | No source in this evidence base | Nothing | Everything; the formulation is prohibited in this session |

⚠️ The middle row is worth a sentence in an answer because the word "catalyst" is unusually precise: a catalyst starts a reaction whose energy comes from elsewhere. ⚠️ That is exactly the relation between Sarajevo and the structures of Lessons 4 to 12.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the assassination was merely a trigger, would not some other incident have served? And if so, does the event matter at all?

**The reply.** ⚠️ This is the single most important objection in the topic and the evidence base answers it directly - in the negative. ✅ Lesson 17 records Williamson's position that the government in Vienna had not taken a decision to attack Serbia before the assassinations at Sarajevo, because they had other political priorities; and that the murders therefore really did provide the decisive moment, so that without them there would have been no decision for war in Vienna and therefore no general conflict. ⚠️ On that position the event is not interchangeable: it is the necessary occasion. ⚠️ An answer that treats the assassination as an arbitrary spark has adopted one contested position while believing itself to be neutral.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Is the refusal to attribute the plot to the Serbian government not simply an evasion?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, because the refusal is specific rather than general. This session states positively what the evidence supports at three levels, including cross-border assistance by an organisation with official penetration. ⚠️ What it declines to do is convert an allegation made by an interested party in July 1914 into a historical finding. ⚠️ That is ordinary evidential discipline, and it is also practically useful: an answer that says "the Austrian government blamed the Serbian government, and the assistance given by an organisation with links to Serbian military intelligence gave that allegation a factual basis" is both more accurate and more impressive than one that says "Serbia did it".

**The residual.** ⚠️ Recorded and not filled: the relationship between the Serbian civil government, the dynasty and the officer networks in 1914; the content and outcome of the Austrian investigation; and the question of prior knowledge in Belgrade. ⚠️ None of these is asserted anywhere in this session.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's must-know list records that Gavrilo Princip assassinated Archduke Franz Ferdinand at Sarajevo on 28 June 1914, and its trap warns against reducing the cause of the war to the assassination alone, recording that the source treats it as the trigger and not the complete cause.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Sarajevo was the occasion, not the cause, of the First World War." Examine.
> ⚠️ How far did the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand determine the course of events in July 1914?
> ⚠️ Account for the outbreak of the First World War.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use the assassination in exactly two places and nowhere else: once in the opening, to fix the trigger and date the sequence, and once in the contingency paragraph, where Williamson's position gives it back its necessity. ⚠️ Do not narrate the day. ⚠️ The details of the route, the bomb and the wrong turning are absent from this evidence base and are not needed: what an answer needs is the date, the victims, the place and the attribution levels.

### Traps

- Writing "Serbia assassinated the Archduke". ✅ The recorded position is that Gavrilo Princip shot the Archduke and his wife, and that the Austrians blamed the Serb government.
- Writing about "the Serbs" as a collective actor. Prohibited under the attribution discipline.
- Saying the assassination caused the First World War. ✅ It was the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia.
- Calling Franz Ferdinand the Emperor. ✅ He was the nephew and heir to Emperor Franz Josef.
- Placing Sarajevo in Serbia. ✅ It was the capital of Bosnia, the province annexed by Austria-Hungary in 1908.
- Asserting that the Serbian government organised or knew of the plot. Not established in this evidence base.
- Asserting Franz Ferdinand's own political views. Not recorded here.
- Treating the assassination as an interchangeable spark without noticing that this contradicts Williamson's recorded position.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 28 June 1914, Sarajevo, capital of Bosnia: Archduke Franz Ferdinand, nephew and heir to Emperor Franz Josef, and his wife were shot dead while on an official visit.
2. ✅ The killer was Gavrilo Princip.
3. ✅ The event was the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia.
4. ✅ The Austrians blamed the Serb government and sent a harsh ultimatum; the Serbs accepted most of the demands.
5. ✅ The Austrians, with a promise of German support, were determined to use the incident as an excuse for war.
6. ✅ **Fact:** an earlier bomb attempt that morning failed; the conspirators were a locally organised group of young Bosnians associated with the Young Bosnia movement.
7. ✅ **Fact:** weapons and assistance came from the secret Serbian organisation Union or Death, known as the Black Hand, whose leader held a senior post in Serbian military intelligence.
8. ⚠️ Five attribution levels: the individual (secure), the conspiracy (secure), the organisation (secure), the Serbian government (an Austrian allegation only), the Serbian people (prohibited).
9. ⚠️ Place matters: Bosnia was annexed in 1908 and claimed by Serbian nationalists, so the crime occurred where the two central structures intersect.
10. 📰 Single current linkage: the Museum of Sarajevo's exhibition "The Sarajevo Assassination - 112 Years Later", opening 27 June 2026 as part of the official commemoration; the institution describes the event as the immediate catalyst for the outbreak of the war.
11. ⚠️ Williamson's position, recorded in Lesson 17, denies that the trigger was interchangeable: without the murders there would have been no decision for war in Vienna.
12. ⚠️ Answer discipline: use the assassination twice - in the opening and in the contingency paragraph - and never narrate the day.

### Concept check

**Question:** Set out precisely what can and cannot be asserted about responsibility for the events of 28 June 1914, and explain why the distinction matters for the rest of the answer.

**Model answer:** Four things can be asserted. Gavrilo Princip shot and killed Archduke Franz Ferdinand, nephew and heir to Emperor Franz Josef, and his wife at Sarajevo, the capital of Bosnia, on 28 June 1914 during an official visit. He acted as part of a locally organised group of young Bosnian conspirators associated with the Young Bosnia movement, one of whom had already thrown a bomb at the motorcade earlier that morning. That group was supplied and assisted from across the frontier by the secret Serbian nationalist organisation Union or Death, commonly called the Black Hand, whose leader held a senior position in Serbian military intelligence. And the Austrian government blamed the Serbian government and sent a harsh ultimatum, to which Serbia acceded on most points. Two things cannot be asserted. The first is that the Serbian government organised, authorised or knew of the plot: the evidence base records the Austrian allegation as an allegation and records the internal politics of Serbia in this period as unknown to it, so an allegation made by an interested party in July 1914 must not be converted into a historical finding. The second is any statement about "the Serbs" as a people, which is an error of fact rather than of manners, since a people is not an agent and cannot form an intention. The distinction matters for everything that follows, because the Austrian case for war rested on attributing the crime to the Serbian state, and an answer that makes that attribution silently has adopted Vienna's position while appearing to describe events. It also matters because the crime's significance in the causation argument is that it is the immediate cause of one declaration of war, on 28 July, and not of the general war, whose explanation lies in the decisions of the following week.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that declining to attribute the plot to the Serbian government is the same as exonerating Serbian actors. It is not. The evidence supports cross-border assistance by an organisation with penetration into Serbian military intelligence, and that is a serious statement which an answer should make. What it does not support is the further step to state responsibility, and the gap between those two statements is exactly where the July Crisis was argued.

*The next lesson enters the crisis itself. The assassination created an occasion; it did not create a policy. Between 28 June and 28 July a series of decisions converted the occasion into a war with Serbia, and the first and most consequential of those decisions was taken not in Vienna but in Berlin.*

## Lesson 14 - The July Crisis, first movement: the assurance from Berlin, the ultimatum, and the declaration on Serbia

Progress: 14/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the interval between 28 June and 23 July; the unconditional German assurance and the blank-cheque formulation; why German policy changed from 1913; the ultimatum and its 48-hour limit; the Serbian reply and the acceptance of most demands; the Austrian determination to use the incident as an excuse for war; the declaration of war on Serbia on 28 July; the expectation of localisation; what this movement does and does not explain

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the Austrian ultimatum and the Serbian acceptance of most of its demands; the determination, with a promise of German support, to use the incident as an excuse for war; the declaration of war on Serbia on 28 July; and the change in German policy between 1913 and 1914, with the telegram urging attack and promising help without conditions
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 13
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the first movement as a decision chain, not a drift

```text
   28 JUNE      the assassination
                -> an occasion exists; no policy yet exists
                    |
                    v
   EARLY JULY   THE DECISION THAT MATTERS MOST
                Vienna asks Berlin where it stands.
                The Kaiser sends a telegram URGING the Austrians to
                ATTACK SERBIA and PROMISING GERMAN HELP WITHOUT ANY
                CONDITIONS ATTACHED.
                -> "like giving the Austrians a BLANK CHEQUE"
                -> compare 1913, when Germany said NO
                    |
                    v
   23 JULY      THE ULTIMATUM
                Austria-Hungary sends Serbia a HARSH ULTIMATUM with a
                48-HOUR TIME LIMIT
                    |
                    v
   25 JULY      THE SERBIAN REPLY
                Serbia ACCEPTS MOST OF THE DEMANDS - but not all
                Austria-Hungary breaks off diplomatic relations
                    |
                    v
   28 JULY      THE DECLARATION
                "the Austrians, with a promise of German support, were
                determined to use the incident as an EXCUSE FOR WAR"
                AUSTRIA-HUNGARY DECLARES WAR ON SERBIA
                    |
                    v
   THE EXPECTATION HELD IN VIENNA
   that the war would remain LOCALISED, like the Balkan Wars
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   NOTHING IN THIS MOVEMENT EXPLAINS A EUROPEAN WAR.
   IT EXPLAINS AN AUSTRO-SERB WAR THAT VIENNA EXPECTED TO CONTAIN.
```

*The diagram makes a point that scripts routinely miss. ⚠️ The most consequential decision of the first movement was taken in Berlin, not in Vienna, and it was taken before the ultimatum was drafted. ⚠️ And the movement ends with an expectation - localisation - that was wrong. The wrongness of that expectation, not the declaration itself, is what makes the second movement possible.*

### 1. The month that nobody explains

An heir is murdered. Governments send condolences. A month passes. Then an ultimatum arrives with a two-day deadline, most of it is accepted, and war is declared anyway.

The month is the interesting part. It is not the behaviour of a state that has been provoked beyond endurance; it is the behaviour of a state that has decided to use a provocation and has spent four weeks making sure of its backing and preparing its case. ⚠️ The source says exactly this: the Austrians were determined to use the incident as an excuse for war. ⚠️ The word "excuse" is the source's own, and it is the single most important word in the first movement.

### 2. The assurance from Berlin

✅ **Fact:** the source records the change of German policy and the instrument. It is significant that Germany restrained the Austrians from declaring war on Serbia in 1913, but in 1914 encouraged them to go ahead. The Kaiser sent them a telegram urging them to attack Serbia and promising German help without any conditions attached. This was like giving the Austrians a blank cheque to do whatever they wanted.

✅ **Fact:** the source then identifies the analytical question this raises: the important question is why German policy towards Austria-Hungary changed, and it records that this question has caused great controversy among historians, setting out three interpretations - Fischer's, the preventive-timing reading and the miscalculated-deterrence reading - which Lesson 17 examines.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the same item in its comparison table: the German blank cheque was a crucial enabling factor for the Austrian hard line and is the core blame point.

✅ **Fact:** the assurance was given in Berlin on 5-6 July 1914, when an envoy from Vienna carried the question to the German capital and received the unconditional answer from the Kaiser and the Chancellor.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four things follow from the assurance, and each is separately examinable.

1. **It is a decision, not a performance of an obligation.** ⚠️ Lesson 12's controlled comparison settles this: the same alliance produced restraint twelve months earlier.
2. **It was unconditional.** ⚠️ The absence of conditions is what makes it a transfer of judgement: Berlin gave Vienna the power to determine when Germany would be at war. ⚠️ A conditional assurance - support if Russia intervenes, support provided the demands are moderate - would have preserved German control, and none was attached.
3. **It preceded the ultimatum.** ⚠️ The sequence matters for responsibility: the German commitment was given before the terms that provoked the crisis were known, which is why the recorded verdict that Germany risked a major war is a statement about the assurance rather than about the ultimatum.
4. **It made localisation more plausible in Vienna, not less.** ⚠️ This is the subtle point. From Vienna's perspective, firm German backing was the very thing that should deter Russian intervention and keep the war local - which is why the assurance both enabled the Austrian decision and encouraged the expectation that turned out to be false.

### 3. The ultimatum and the reply

✅ **Fact:** the source records the instrument and the response. The Austrians blamed the Serb government and sent a harsh ultimatum. The Serbs accepted most of the demands in it, but the Austrians, with a promise of German support, were determined to use the incident as an excuse for war.

✅ **Fact:** the ultimatum was delivered on 23 July 1914 with a 48-hour time limit expiring on 25 July; Serbia replied on 25 July accepting most but not all of the demands, and Austria-Hungary broke off diplomatic relations the same day.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structure of this exchange is what an examiner is testing when a question uses the word "excuse" or "pretext".

| Element | What it shows |
|---|---|
| A 48-hour limit | ⚠️ A deadline that short is not designed to produce negotiation; it is designed to produce either capitulation or a casus belli |
| Serbia accepts most demands | ✅ Recorded directly; it removes the argument that Serbia was defiant and forced Vienna's hand |
| Austria-Hungary breaks relations the same day | ⚠️ A same-day rupture on a partial acceptance indicates that the object was not compliance |
| War declared three days later | ✅ 28 July |
| The source's own word: "excuse" | ⚠️ The account characterises the Austrian purpose as using the incident rather than responding to it |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** be careful to state the Serbian reply exactly as recorded - most of the demands, not all. ⚠️ A script that says Serbia accepted the ultimatum in full has overstated the case and invited the correction; a script that says Serbia rejected it has understated it and lost the point. ⚠️ The recorded formulation does the work by itself: a state that accepts most of a harsh ultimatum within forty-eight hours and is attacked anyway is not the state that chose war.

### 4. The declaration of 28 July, and the expectation attached to it

✅ **Fact:** on 28 July, Austria-Hungary declared war on Serbia.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the Austrian expectation and the Austrian motive together. The Austrians genuinely felt that if Serb and Slav nationalist ambitions for a state of Yugoslavia were achieved, it would cause the collapse of the Habsburg Empire; Serbia must be curbed. ✅ In fairness, they probably hoped the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the localisation expectation is the hinge between the two movements of the July Crisis and deserves a sentence of its own in any answer. ⚠️ If Vienna expected a local war, then the general war was not Vienna's object, and the general war must be explained by what happened between 29 July and 4 August. ⚠️ It also means that the Austrian decision, however reckless, is not the same kind of act as a decision to start a European war - which is precisely why the responsibility question of Lesson 17 has two levels rather than one.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the expectation was not unreasonable on the record available to Vienna. ✅ In 1908 Russia had backed down; ✅ in 1912-13 two Balkan wars had been contained by great-power diplomacy; ✅ and Germany's unconditional backing was precisely the factor that had produced the Russian retreat in 1908. ⚠️ Vienna was reasoning from the last comparable case and did not know that Russia had spent six years ensuring the last comparable case would not repeat. ⚠️ That is the structure of a miscalculation rather than of a plot, and Lesson 18 develops it.

### 5. Comparison: what changed between 1913 and 1914, item by item

| Element | Summer 1913 | July 1914 |
|---|---|---|
| Austrian intention | ✅ Itching to support Bulgaria and attack Serbia | ✅ Determined to use the incident as an excuse for war |
| German response | ✅ Restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia | ✅ Telegram urging an attack and promising help without conditions |
| Occasion | A war between Balkan states | ✅ The assassination of the heir to the throne |
| Russian posture | Not engaged in the Bulgarian quarrel | ✅ Anxious not to let the Serbs down again; ✅ a promise of help given in February 1914 |
| Outcome | No Austro-Serb war | ✅ Declaration of war on 28 July |

⚠️ Two of the five rows are decisions and one is a prior commitment. ⚠️ The occasion row is the only one that is not a choice - which is why Williamson's position that the murders supplied the decisive moment is coherent, and why the alliance-obligation defence is not.

### 6. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Was Austria-Hungary not entitled to act after the murder of its heir by conspirators assisted from across a frontier?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Entitlement to act and the particular action taken are different questions, and the answer should separate them. ⚠️ A state whose heir has been killed with cross-border assistance is entitled to demand an investigation, and Lesson 13 records that the assistance is established. ⚠️ What the record does not support is the proposition that the action taken was a response proportionate to the demand: the ultimatum carried a forty-eight-hour limit, most of its demands were accepted, relations were broken the same day, and the recorded characterisation of the Austrian purpose is that the incident was used as an excuse for war. ⚠️ The distinction between a grievance and the use made of a grievance is the analytical content of this lesson.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Is the blank cheque not simply a colourful phrase for an ally honouring a commitment?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No. ✅ The recorded content is that the telegram urged an attack and promised help without any conditions attached. ⚠️ Urging is not honouring; it is initiating. ⚠️ And an unconditional promise is the specific thing that distinguishes 1914 from 1913, when the same relationship produced a refusal. ⚠️ The phrase is not decorative: it names the transfer of the decision for war from the state that would fight the general war to the state that would fight the local one.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Recorded and unresolved: whether German backing was in fact decisive for the Austrian choice. ✅ Williamson's recorded position is that the Austrian leaders made their own decisions and that German pressure and promises of support were not important; ✅ the Fischer tradition and Hamilton and Herwig treat the assurance as central. ⚠️ Lesson 17 displays both and this session does not adjudicate between them.

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's mains frame places the German blank cheque in the escalation step, alongside Russian mobilisation and the Belgian question, and its directive map records that a question asking who was responsible is testing two levels - decision-makers and system.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The decisive step in July 1914 was taken in Berlin, not in Vienna." Examine.
> ⚠️ To what extent was Germany responsible for the outbreak of the First World War?
> ⚠️ Account for the outbreak of the First World War.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Write the first movement as three dated decisions and one expectation: the unconditional assurance of early July, the ultimatum of 23 July with its forty-eight-hour limit, the declaration of 28 July, and the expectation of localisation. ⚠️ Four clauses will carry it, and the fourth is the one that earns the mark, because it sets up the escalation paragraph that follows.

### Traps

- Saying Serbia rejected the ultimatum. ✅ Serbia accepted most of the demands.
- Saying Serbia accepted the ultimatum in full. ✅ It accepted most, not all.
- Treating the blank cheque as a treaty obligation. ✅ It was a telegram urging attack and promising unconditional help.
- Dating the declaration of war on Serbia to June or to 1 August. ✅ It was 28 July 1914.
- Omitting the localisation expectation, which is what makes the second movement necessary to explain.
- Writing that Austria-Hungary intended a European war. ✅ It probably hoped the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars.
- Treating the month between 28 June and 23 July as inactivity. It is the period in which the assurance was obtained.
- Giving the ultimatum's clauses. They are not held in this evidence base and are not needed.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Germany restrained Austria-Hungary in 1913 and encouraged it in 1914; the change of German policy is the analytical question.
2. ✅ The Kaiser's telegram urged an attack on Serbia and promised German help without any conditions attached - like a blank cheque.
3. ✅ **Fact:** the assurance was given in Berlin on 5-6 July 1914 through an envoy sent from Vienna.
4. ✅ The Austrians blamed the Serb government and sent a harsh ultimatum.
5. ✅ **Fact:** the ultimatum was delivered on 23 July with a 48-hour limit; Serbia replied on 25 July; Austria-Hungary broke off relations the same day.
6. ✅ The Serbs accepted most of the demands, but the Austrians, with a promise of German support, were determined to use the incident as an excuse for war.
7. ✅ 28 July 1914: Austria-Hungary declared war on Serbia.
8. ✅ The Austrians genuinely felt that South Slav success would cause the collapse of the Habsburg Empire and that Serbia must be curbed.
9. ✅ They probably hoped the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars.
10. ⚠️ The assurance was unconditional, preceded the ultimatum, and transferred the timing of a possible European war to Vienna.
11. ⚠️ Vienna reasoned from 1908 and 1912-13 and did not price in the changes Russia had made since 1908.
12. ⚠️ Nothing in this movement explains a European war; it explains a local war that Vienna expected to contain.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the German assurance of early July 1914 is treated as the decisive step of the first movement, and state the strongest recorded objection to that view.

**Model answer:** It is treated as decisive for four reasons that follow from its recorded content and its timing. First, it was a reversal: in the summer of 1913 Germany had restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia, and in July 1914 the Kaiser sent a telegram urging Vienna to attack Serbia and promising German help without any conditions attached, which the source describes as like giving the Austrians a blank cheque. Because the alliance, the partner and the quarrel were identical in both years, the variable is the German decision, and the defence that Germany was performing a treaty obligation is unavailable. Second, the assurance was unconditional, and the absence of conditions transferred to Vienna the effective power to determine when Germany would find itself at war. Third, it preceded the ultimatum, so the German commitment was made before the terms that produced the crisis were known, which is why the charge against Berlin is that it risked a major war rather than that it endorsed particular demands. Fourth, it shaped the Austrian expectation: firm German backing was precisely what had produced the Russian retreat of 1908, so the assurance simultaneously enabled the Austrian decision and encouraged the belief that the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars. The strongest recorded objection is Samuel Williamson's, that the government in Vienna made its own decisions and that German pressure and promises of support were not important, a position he combines with the claim that Vienna had taken no decision to attack Serbia before the assassinations. If that is right, the assurance was permissive rather than decisive, and the weight of responsibility shifts towards Vienna's own choice.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the month between 28 June and 23 July as a period of drift or of mourning. It was the period in which the decisive external commitment was obtained and the instrument was prepared, and the source's own word for the Austrian purpose - using the incident as an excuse for war - describes a policy rather than a reaction.

*The next lesson covers the nine days in which a local war became a general one. This is the part of the topic where precision about dates and sequence is not pedantry: the whole argument about responsibility, contingency and mechanism depends on knowing what happened in which order, and on being honest about the two points where the evidence base disagrees with itself.*
