```text
             - even though Germany at that point had NO SPECIFIC
               QUARREL WITH FRANCE
                                |
   BELGIUM   German troops enter Belgium on the way to invade France
             *** SECOND INTERNAL DISAGREEMENT: the source's
                 narrative dates the crossing to 4 AUGUST; its own
                 prologue implies entry two days before the night of
                 5 August, i.e. 3 August. The standard date carried
                 by the verification pass is 4 AUGUST. ***
                                |
   4 AUGUST  BRITAIN ENTERS THE WAR
             Britain, WHO IN 1839 HAD PROMISED TO DEFEND BELGIAN
             NEUTRALITY, demanded their withdrawal; the demand was
             ignored
                                |
   5-16 AUG  THE FIRST MAJOR ENGAGEMENT
             German assault columns converge on LIEGE; halted by fire
             from the outlying forts; siege tactics with heavy
             howitzers firing shells that plunged from a height of
             12 000 feet; the first fort surrendered on 13 AUGUST and
             Liege was under German control three days later
                                |
   6 AUGUST  AUSTRIA-HUNGARY DECLARES WAR ON RUSSIA
                                |
             OTHER COUNTRIES JOINED LATER -> the next owner
```

*The diagram is deliberately annotated with its own disagreements. ⚠️ A live session that smooths over a conflict inside its own evidence base is training a candidate to assert things that a better-informed examiner will know are contested. Both disagreements here are minor in themselves and instructive in method: the Russian one because the partial-general distinction is exactly what the German decision turned on, and the Belgian one because it shows how easily a date can drift between the narrative and the chronology of the same book.*

### 1. The point at which the crisis stopped being about Serbia

From 28 July the crisis stops being about Serbia. Nothing that happens after that date is about the assassination, the ultimatum or the Balkans. Russia mobilises because it will not accept a second humiliation; Germany demands a cancellation and, not getting it, attacks in the west; Britain enters because a neutral country has been invaded.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the most useful way to hold the second movement in mind is that each state entered for a reason that had nothing to do with the state it was fighting. ⚠️ Germany declared war on France over a quarrel with Russia; Britain went to war with Germany over Belgium; Austria-Hungary, which had begun the crisis, declared war on Russia last. ⚠️ A system in which the belligerents' reasons do not match their opponents is a system that has stopped being about its original dispute, and saying so in one sentence is a strong closing move in an escalation answer.

### 2. The Russian mobilisation, and the disagreement about its stages

✅ **Fact:** the source records the decision and its motive. The Russians, anxious not to let the Serbs down again, ordered a general mobilisation, which its narrative and its time chart both date to 29 July.

✅ **Fact:** the standard chronology distinguishes two stages: Russia ordered partial mobilisation in support of Serbia on 29 July and general mobilisation across the empire on 30 July.

⚠️ **How this session handles the disagreement.** Both records are displayed. ⚠️ The distinction is not trivial, because the difference between a partial mobilisation directed at Austria-Hungary and a general mobilisation that includes the German frontier is precisely the difference between a measure aimed at one adversary and a measure that German planning had to treat as aimed at itself. ⚠️ An answer may safely write that Russia mobilised on 29-30 July, that the general mobilisation was the decisive step for German planning, and that the exact staging is recorded differently in different accounts. ⚠️ It should not assert one dating as certain while implying that the other does not exist.

✅ **Fact:** the motive is recorded in more than one place and is consistent. ✅ The Russians were anxious not to let the Serbs down again - a reference to 1908. ✅ Russia saw the matter as a struggle for survival once Austria declared war on Serbia, because of the Dardanelles and the trade route. ✅ Russian prestige as leader of the Slavs would suffer if they failed to support Serbia. ✅ And in February 1914 the Tsar had promised to help the Serbs in the event of an attack by Austria-Hungary.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the February 1914 promise converts the Russian decision from an improvised reaction into the honouring of a commitment given five months earlier, which is a materially different act. ⚠️ It also gives Lesson 17's Mulligan argument its evidentiary base.

### 3. The German demand and the declarations

✅ **Fact:** the source records the demand and the refusal. The German government demanded that the Russian mobilisation should be cancelled on 31 July, and when the Russians failed to comply, Germany declared war on Russia on 1 August and on France on 3 August.

✅ **Fact:** the time chart records the same dates: 1 August, Germany declares war on Russia; 3 August, Germany declares war on France.

✅ **Fact:** the source also records the crucial asymmetry: Germany at that point had no specific quarrel with France.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that sentence is the most efficient single piece of evidence for the operational-cause argument of Lesson 6 and should be used wherever the mobilisation question arises. ⚠️ A state that declares war on another state with which it has no quarrel is not acting from a dispute; it is executing a plan whose first requirement is that the western enemy be engaged first. ⚠️ Without the plan, the declaration of 3 August is inexplicable; with it, it is the only intelligible step.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** connect this to Lesson 6's exchange of 30 July. ✅ Wilhelm had proposed exactly the alternative that would have avoided the French declaration - a partial mobilisation against Russia only - and ✅ Moltke refused on timetable grounds. ⚠️ So the French war was not merely a consequence of the plan; it was a consequence of the plan surviving a political attempt to set it aside.

### 4. Belgium, and the recorded tension about the date

✅ **Fact:** the source records the entry and its consequence. When German troops entered Belgium on their way to invade France, Britain - who in 1839 had promised to defend Belgian neutrality - demanded their withdrawal; when this demand was ignored, Britain entered the war on 4 August.

✅ **Fact:** the same account, in its section on mobilisation plans, records that the plan depended on the rapid capture of Liege in Belgium and involved the first aggressive act outside the Balkans, when German troops crossed the frontier into Belgium on 4 August, thus violating Belgian neutrality.

✅ **Fact:** the same account's prologue records that late on the night of 5 August 1914 five columns of German assault troops, which had entered Belgium two days earlier, were converging on the town of Liege - which places the entry on 3 August.

✅ **Fact:** the standard chronology records a German ultimatum to Belgium on 2 August, the entry of German troops into Luxembourg on 2 August, the declaration of war on France and the invasion of Belgium on 3-4 August, and the British declaration on 4 August.

⚠️ **How this session handles the disagreement.** ⚠️ The safe and accurate formulation is that German forces entered Belgium on 3-4 August, that the violation of Belgian neutrality was complete by 4 August, and that Britain entered on 4 August after its demand for withdrawal was ignored. ⚠️ The internal inconsistency between a book's narrative and its own prologue is recorded rather than resolved, because resolving it would require evidence this session does not hold.

✅ **Fact:** the 1839 guarantee was given by the Treaty of London, to which the major European powers including Britain and Prussia were parties, and which guaranteed Belgian independence and neutrality.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Belgian question does three distinct things at once, and separating them is worth marks.

| Function | What Belgium supplied |
|---|---|
| Legal | ✅ A specific treaty obligation dating from 1839 that gave Britain a ground for action independent of any commitment to France |
| Strategic | ⚠️ A hostile great power established on the Channel coast opposite the British Isles - the classic threat that British policy had resisted for centuries |
| Political | ⚠️ A violation of a small neutral state's rights, which made intervention defensible to a public and a cabinet that had no appetite for a continental war |

⚠️ All three were needed. ⚠️ A purely strategic case would have divided the British cabinet; a purely legal case without the strategic threat would probably not have produced an army; the combination produced entry within hours of the deadline.

### 5. Britain's entry: why it requires its own explanation

✅ **Fact:** the source records the writing rule in its factual-risk controls: do not say Britain entered because of the alliance with France; Britain had no binding alliance obligation of that kind, and Belgian neutrality was the immediate legal-political issue. ✅ Its misconception list records the same point: Britain did not enter only because of France.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the point at which Lessons 4, 10 and 15 converge, and the convergence is worth setting out explicitly.

```text
   WHY BRITAIN'S ENTRY IS A SEPARATE PROBLEM
   -----------------------------------------
   1904  Entente Cordiale = a "friendly getting-together", not an
         alliance
   1906  Anglo-French MILITARY CONVERSATIONS follow the first
         Moroccan crisis -> expectations without obligations
   1907  Anglo-Russian agreement = NOT a military alliance
                                |
                                v
   JULY 1914  Britain is therefore NOT TREATY-BOUND to fight
              -> its entry cannot be explained by the alliance system
              -> Germany can reasonably hope it will stay out
              -> the 1912-13 London conference had encouraged Berlin
                 to believe Britain might be DETACHED from France
                 and Russia
                                |
                                v
   4 AUGUST   Britain enters on a DIFFERENT ground entirely:
              the 1839 promise to defend Belgian neutrality,
              a demand for withdrawal, and its rejection
```

⚠️ This is also the answer to the recorded blame position that Britain failed to warn Germany clearly enough. ✅ Some blame the British for not making it clear that they would definitely support France, on the argument that if the Germans had known this they would not have declared war on France and the fighting could have been restricted to eastern Europe. ⚠️ But the same account records the reply from the operational side: a British announcement on 31 July would have made no difference, because it was the full plan or nothing. ⚠️ Both positions are recorded; the second is the stronger, and an answer that gives both and then prefers the second with a reason is doing exactly what a "to what extent" question asks.

### 6. The last entries, and the handover

✅ **Fact:** Austria-Hungary declared war on Russia on 6 August. ✅ Other countries joined later.

✅ **Fact:** the first major engagement is recorded. Under cover of darkness late on the night of 5 August 1914, five columns of German assault troops converging on the town of Liege expected little resistance and were halted by determined fire from the town's outlying forts. ✅ The Germans were forced to resort to siege tactics, using heavy howitzers whose shells plunged from a height of 12 000 feet to shatter the armour-plating of the forts. ✅ On 13 August the first fort surrendered and three days later Liege was under German control. ✅ This was the first major engagement of the First World War.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ Liege is recorded here because it is the point at which this topic ends and the next begins: the plan's first requirement met its first resistance, and the six-week promise of Lesson 6 began to fail on day one. ⚠️ Nothing further about the campaign, the Marne, the entry of later belligerents or the conduct of the war appears in this session; all of it belongs to the First World War and aftermath owner.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Russia mobilised first, so Russia converted a local war into a general one.

**The reply.** ⚠️ The objection is a recorded blame position and deserves a serious answer rather than a dismissal. ✅ It is true that Russia was the first to order a general mobilisation and that it was this Russian mobilisation which provoked Germany to mobilise. ⚠️ Three qualifications apply. ✅ First, Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war, because troops could be halted at the frontiers; it was German planning that could not treat it as a pause. ✅ Second, Russia mobilised in support of a state that had already been attacked, after a February 1914 promise, and after a humiliation in 1908 that it had spent six years ensuring would not recur. ✅ Third, Austria-Hungary, in the recorded judgement, ought to have realised how difficult it would be for Russia to stay neutral in the circumstances. ⚠️ The accurate formulation is that Russian mobilisation was the occasion for the German trip-wire, not the cause of it.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Would a clearer British position have prevented the war?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The evidence base holds both positions and they are not reconciled. ✅ One recorded blame position says yes, and that the fighting could have been restricted to eastern Europe. ✅ The operational evidence says no, because on 31 July it was the full plan or nothing. ⚠️ This session's assessment is that the second is stronger on the record, because it rests on a documented refusal to alter the deployment rather than on a counterfactual about persuasion. ⚠️ But the disagreement is displayed, and a candidate should display it too.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Recorded and unresolved: the exact staging of the Russian mobilisation, and the exact date of the German entry into Belgium as between the two records inside this evidence base.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's escalation chain runs from Sarajevo through the ultimatum and declaration, Russian mobilisation, German backing and counter-mobilisation, the attack on France through Belgium, and Britain's entry on 4 August, concluding that a Balkan nationality quarrel became a general European war. ✅ Its directive map records that a question asking why a Balkan crisis became a world war is testing the escalation mechanism and not the origin.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war in 1914?
> ⚠️ Examine the role of mobilisation and the violation of Belgian neutrality in the escalation of the July Crisis.
> ⚠️ "The First World War began in the Balkans, but the structure of European politics made escalation increasingly likely." Examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Write this movement as a chain with the decision-maker named at each link, not as a list of dates. ⚠️ The chain that scores is: Vienna declares, expecting localisation; St Petersburg mobilises, honouring a February commitment and refusing a second 1908; Berlin demands cancellation, is refused, and executes a plan that requires attacking a state with which it has no quarrel; Brussels is invaded; London enters on an 1839 guarantee. ⚠️ Five clauses, five decision-makers, one mechanism.

### Traps

- Writing that Britain entered because of its alliance with France. ✅ Britain had no binding alliance obligation of that kind.
- Omitting the 1839 guarantee, which is the legal basis of the British demand.
- Writing that Germany declared war on France because of a Franco-German dispute. ✅ Germany at that point had no specific quarrel with France.
- Asserting a single certain date for the Russian general mobilisation without acknowledging the recorded difference between 29 and 30 July.
- Asserting a single certain date for the German entry into Belgium when the evidence base itself carries both 3 and 4 August.
- Dating Britain's entry to 3 August or the Austro-Hungarian declaration on Russia to 1 August. ✅ 4 August and 6 August respectively.
- Carrying the campaign beyond Liege. That belongs to the next owner.
- Treating the second movement as automatic. Every link is a decision by a named government.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 28 July: Austria-Hungary declares war on Serbia.
2. ✅ 29 July: Russia orders mobilisation, anxious not to let the Serbs down again. ✅ **Fact:** partial on 29 July, general on 30 July; the source dates general mobilisation to 29 July. Both records are held.
3. ✅ 30 July: the Kaiser and Bethmann urge Vienna to negotiate; Wilhelm proposes partial mobilisation against Russia only; Moltke refuses on railway-timetable grounds.
4. ✅ 31 July: Germany demands cancellation of the Russian mobilisation; Russia does not comply.
5. ✅ 1 August: Germany declares war on Russia.
6. ✅ 3 August: Germany declares war on France, although Germany at that point had no specific quarrel with France.
7. ✅ German troops enter Belgium on the way to invade France; the source's narrative gives 4 August and its prologue implies 3 August; the standard date is 4 August.
8. ✅ Britain, which in 1839 had promised to defend Belgian neutrality, demanded withdrawal; the demand was ignored and Britain entered on 4 August.
9. ✅ 6 August: Austria-Hungary declares war on Russia. Other countries joined later.
10. ✅ First major engagement: German columns at Liege from the night of 5 August; forts halted them; heavy howitzers firing shells from 12 000 feet; first fort surrendered 13 August; Liege under German control three days later.
11. ⚠️ Belgium did three things at once - legal ground, strategic threat, political justification - and all three were needed.
12. ⚠️ The signature observation: each belligerent entered for a reason unconnected with the state it was fighting.

### Concept check

**Question:** Write the escalation from 28 July to 4 August as a chain of decisions with the decision-maker named at each link, and identify the one link that cannot be explained without reference to a military plan.

**Model answer:** Vienna declared war on Serbia on 28 July, having been determined to use the assassination as an excuse for war and expecting, like the Balkan Wars, that the conflict would remain localised. St Petersburg then ordered mobilisation on 29-30 July - partial first and general immediately afterwards, with the source dating general mobilisation to 29 July - because it was anxious not to let the Serbs down again after 1908, because the Tsar had promised in February 1914 to help Serbia if Austria-Hungary attacked, and because it regarded Austrian control of the Balkans as a threat to the Dardanelles and therefore to its main trade route. Berlin demanded on 31 July that the Russian mobilisation be cancelled, and when Russia did not comply declared war on Russia on 1 August. It then declared war on France on 3 August and sent troops into Belgium, whereupon London, which in 1839 had promised to defend Belgian neutrality, demanded their withdrawal and, when the demand was ignored, entered the war on 4 August; Vienna completed the circle by declaring war on Russia on 6 August. The link that cannot be explained without a military plan is the declaration on France, because the source records that Germany at that point had no specific quarrel with France. The explanation lies in a deployment which assumed that France would automatically join Russia and which sent the bulk of German forces through Belgium to knock France out in six weeks before turning east - and in Moltke's refusal on 30 July of Wilhelm's proposed partial mobilisation against Russia alone, on the ground that there was no time to change the railway timetables. Without the plan the French declaration is unintelligible; with it, it is the plan's first step.

**Misconception to avoid:** Describing the escalation as automatic or mechanical. Every link was an order given by an identifiable government that had an alternative, including the German political leadership, which on 30 July was actively seeking a narrower option and was told by its own general staff that the option did not exist. What was mechanical was the deployment, not the decision to use it.

*The remaining lessons move from narrative to judgement. Everything needed to explain the outbreak has now been taught; what has not been done is to sort it. The next lesson builds the causal architecture that every answer on this topic has to carry, and it builds it from the owners' own layered scheme rather than from a generic list.*

## Lesson 16 - Sorting the causes: structural, regional, great-power, operational and proximate

Progress: 16/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the layered causal scheme and its exam function; what each layer explains and what it cannot; the ordering problem and why lists lose marks; necessary and sufficient conditions applied to 1914; the balance-of-power reading tested layer by layer; the concession-and-limit structure; assembling a causal paragraph under time pressure

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the five-layer causes table with its content and stated exam use for each layer; the statement that the Austro-Serb quarrel does not by itself explain the general war; the answer architecture with its directive map, thesis templates, mark-scaled structures and both evidence banks; and the ordering of the suggested reasons for escalation
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 13
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the five layers, with the work each one does

```text
   LAYER FIVE   PROXIMATE / IMMEDIATE
                the assassination at Sarajevo and the Austrian
                ultimatum
                EXPLAINS: why the crisis began on that date
                CANNOT EXPLAIN: why it did not end where 1908 and
                1913 ended
                                |
   LAYER FOUR   REGIONAL
                the Austro-Serb struggle over Bosnia and South Slav
                nationalism
                EXPLAINS: why Vienna would not compromise and why
                the crisis arose in the Balkans
                CANNOT EXPLAIN: why any great power outside Vienna
                had to be involved
                                |
   LAYER THREE  GREAT-POWER
                Russian backing for Serbia and German backing for
                Austria-Hungary
                EXPLAINS: how a regional dispute reached St
                Petersburg and Berlin - the ESCALATION MECHANISM
                CANNOT EXPLAIN: why the escalation ran at the speed
                it did, or why France and Britain were engaged
                                |
   LAYER TWO    OPERATIONAL
                mobilisation plans, especially the German move
                through Belgium
                EXPLAINS: why a local war became a GENERAL war, why
                France was attacked, and why Britain had a trigger
                CANNOT EXPLAIN: why anyone wanted a war at all
                                |
   LAYER ONE    STRUCTURAL
                alliance blocs, nationalism, imperial tension, naval
                and military competition, the Eastern Question
                EXPLAINS: why the system was fragile and why every
                regional dispute became a test of standing
                CANNOT EXPLAIN: timing - the same structures
                produced containment in 1905-06, 1908, 1911, 1912
                and 1913
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE ARCHITECTURE IS THE ANSWER. A LIST OF FIVE ITEMS IS NOT.
   EACH LAYER EXISTS BECAUSE THE LAYER ABOVE IT LEAVES A GAP.
```

*The diagram is drawn from the bottom up on purpose. ⚠️ Most scripts build from the trigger downward and end with a list. The productive method is the reverse: identify what the structural account leaves unexplained, add the layer that fills the gap, and repeat. ⚠️ An answer written that way reads as an argument rather than as a recitation, and it automatically produces the graded verdict that "how far" questions require.*

### 1. The layered scheme, recorded exactly

✅ **Fact:** the source sets out the five layers with their content and their stated exam function.

| Layer | ✅ Recorded content | ✅ Recorded exam use |
|---|---|---|
| Immediate cause | The assassination at Sarajevo and the Austrian ultimatum to Serbia | Opening paragraph, or the trigger |
| Structural cause | Alliance blocs, nationalism, imperial tension, naval rivalry | Long-term causes |
| Regional cause | The Austro-Serb struggle over Bosnia and South Slav nationalism | Balkan focus |
| Great-power cause | Russian backing for Serbia and German backing for Austria-Hungary | Escalation mechanism |
| Operational cause | Mobilisation plans, especially Germany's move through Belgium | Why a local war became a general war |

✅ **Fact:** the source attaches the limiting statement directly to the scheme: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak, but by itself does not fully explain why it became a European and then a world war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the value of this scheme is not that it is a taxonomy but that each layer has a *job*. ⚠️ If a script contains all five and cannot say what job each is doing, it has memorised the table without acquiring the argument.

### 2. Necessary and sufficient, applied

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sharpest way to handle this material in a "how far" or "to what extent" question is through necessity and sufficiency. The test is simple: remove the factor and ask whether the outcome still follows.

| Factor | Necessary for the general war of 1914? | Sufficient on its own? | Test applied |
|---|---|---|---|
| The alliance blocs | ⚠️ Probably yes - without the alignments the quarrel has no route to St Petersburg or Berlin | ✅ No - the same blocs produced containment in 1905-06, 1908, 1911, 1912 and 1913 | Remove them and the quarrel stays Austro-Serb |
| Balkan nationalism and the Habsburg nationality problem | ⚠️ Yes for this particular war - it supplies the non-negotiable quarrel | ⚠️ No - it had existed since 1903 without producing a general war | Remove it and the 1914 crisis has no object |
| The German assurance of early July | ⚠️ Very probably - ✅ Germany's refusal in 1913 produced no war | ⚠️ No - an assurance is permissive, not causal, and Williamson disputes even its necessity | Compare 1913 with 1914 |
| Russian mobilisation | ⚠️ Yes as the occasion for the German trip-wire | ✅ No - ✅ mobilisation did not necessarily mean war | Remove it and the German plan has no activating condition |
| The German mobilisation plan and the Belgian route | ⚠️ Yes for the general war's form and speed, and for British entry | ⚠️ No - a plan requires a decision to execute it | Remove it and there is no reason to attack France or violate Belgium |
| The assassination | ⚠️ Contested - ✅ Williamson holds that without it there would have been no decision for war in Vienna and therefore no general conflict | ✅ No - explicitly the trigger and not the complete cause | Remove it and the Vienna decision may not occur at all |
| Naval rivalry | ⚠️ No - ✅ it had ceased to be a cause of friction by spring 1914 | ✅ No | Remove it and the July sequence is unchanged |
| Colonial rivalry | ⚠️ No - ✅ Anglo-German colonial relations were good in early July 1914 | ✅ No | Remove it and the July sequence is unchanged |

⚠️ The last two rows are what make this table worth constructing. ⚠️ Two of the four items that every script lists as major causes fail the necessity test on the evidence base's own record. ⚠️ An answer that says so, with the dates attached, is demonstrating exactly the grading capacity that a "how far" question is designed to test.

### 3. The ordering problem

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there are three defensible ways to order a causation answer, and choosing deliberately is worth marks in itself.

| Order | Structure | Best for | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Chronological** | 1871 or 1882 -> 1914 -> August | "Account for the outbreak" | Turns into narrative; the verdict arrives too late |
| **Layered** | Structural -> regional -> great-power -> operational -> proximate | "Examine the causes"; the default | Becomes a list if each layer is not tied to a gap |
| **Gap-driven** | Start from the trigger, ask what it fails to explain, add a layer, repeat | "How far", "to what extent", "who was responsible" | Requires confidence; rewards it heavily |

⚠️ For the owned 2024 question - a "how far" demand - the gap-driven order is the strongest available, because the question itself is an invitation to show what one proposed explanation fails to cover.

### 4. The balance-of-power reading, tested layer by layer

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the section that converts the whole session into an answer to the topic's verified question, and it is built by running the balance-of-power proposition down the five layers.

| Layer | Does balance of power explain it? | ✅ Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Structural | **Yes, strongly.** The blocs were counterweights; the German encirclement complaint is a balance-of-power fear by definition; the naval competition was a contest about a ratio, not about territory | Triple Alliance 1882; Franco-Russian alliance 1894; Entente Cordiale 1904; Anglo-Russian agreement 1907; the recorded German complaint of encirclement |
| Regional | **No.** Vienna's motive was the internal survival of a multi-national empire, and Serbia's was the creation of a new state | ✅ Serbian success threatened the internal survival of the Habsburg Empire; ✅ the South Slav programme sought to create a state, not to preserve an equilibrium |
| Great-power | **Partly.** Russian prestige and Balkan position are standing-in-the-system motives; the Dardanelles interest is a material one | ✅ Russian prestige as leader of the Slavs; ✅ the Straits as the main Russian trade route |
| Operational | **No.** A railway timetable is not a strategic purpose | ✅ Moltke's refusal to alter the deployment; ✅ Germany had no specific quarrel with France |
| Proximate | **No.** An assassination and an ultimatum are not equilibrium calculations | ✅ The assassination as the immediate cause of the declaration on Serbia |
| Britain's entry | **Yes, substantially.** A hostile great power on the Channel coast is the classic balance threat, reinforced by a legal obligation | ✅ The 1839 promise and the demand for withdrawal; ✅ Britain entered on 4 August |

⚠️ **The verdict that follows from the table** is the one the source records: balance-of-power calculation was the organising logic of the system that turned a Balkan quarrel into a continental war, but it was not the purpose for which the belligerents chose war in July 1914. ⚠️ Expressed as a shape: the first and last links of the chain are cleanly balance-of-power; the middle links are imperial, national and operational. ⚠️ That asymmetry is the answer to "how far".

### 5. The concession-and-limit structure

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** every "how far" answer on this topic has the same skeleton, and it is worth rehearsing until it can be produced without thought.

```text
   STEP 1  CONCEDE PRECISELY, WITH DATES
           name what the proposed explanation genuinely covers
           -> for balance of power: the bloc structure 1882-1907,
              the encirclement complaint, the naval ratio contest,
              and Britain's entry
   STEP 2  IDENTIFY THE GAP, WITH EVIDENCE
           name what it cannot absorb
           -> Habsburg imperial survival; South Slav state-creation;
              French revanchism; preventive timing; operational
              rigidity; the contingency of July
   STEP 3  EXPLAIN WHY IT CANNOT ABSORB IT
           give the reason, not just the item
           -> a multi-national empire's fear of dissolution is not a
              ratio calculation; preventive war seeks to CHANGE a
              future balance, which is the opposite of preserving one
   STEP 4  GRADE
           "a necessary part of the explanation and not the essential
           one" - a verdict, not a balance of assertions
   STEP 5  QUALIFY
           name what would change the verdict
           -> if the question is about Britain's entry alone, the
              balance-of-power reading is much stronger
```

⚠️ Step 5 is the step most candidates omit and the one that distinguishes a first-class script: it shows that the verdict is sensitive to the question rather than recited.

### 6. Comparison: three rival organising theses

| Thesis | Strongest evidence | Strongest objection | Where it belongs |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Structural**: the system made war likely | ✅ Two armed camps; crises since 1900 repeatedly bringing Europe to the verge of war; arms competition; Balkan instability | ✅ The same system produced containment five times; ✅ nothing was binding about the alliances | The opening of a causation answer, never the verdict |
| **Decisional**: specific men chose war | ✅ The German reversal between 1913 and 1914; the unconditional assurance; Moltke's refusal on 30-31 July; the Russian mobilisation order | ⚠️ Decisions were taken under constraints not of the deciders' making | The centre of a responsibility answer |
| **Mechanical**: timetables and plans drove events | ✅ Taylor on railway timetables; the refusal to alter the deployment; the declaration on France without a quarrel | ✅ Zuber's challenge to the plan's status; ⚠️ the assurance of early July preceded any timetable | The escalation paragraph |

⚠️ The three are complementary if ordered correctly: structure explains the danger, decision explains the event, mechanism explains the form and the speed. ⚠️ A script that assigns each to its proper paragraph has an architecture; a script that argues for one against the others has picked a side unnecessarily.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is the layered scheme not simply a way of avoiding a verdict by saying everything mattered?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It would be, if the layers were merely listed. ⚠️ The protection against that is the necessity test of section 2, which produces differentiated results: two commonly cited causes fail it outright, several pass it as necessary but not sufficient, and one is recorded as contested. ⚠️ A layered account that grades its layers is the opposite of an evasion; an ungraded one is exactly the failure the objection describes.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the scheme not understate contingency by making the outcome look over-determined?

**The reply.** ⚠️ That risk is real and Lesson 18 addresses it directly. ⚠️ The safeguard inside this lesson is the repeated finding that the structural layer cannot explain timing, and that the same structures produced five containments. ⚠️ A layered account whose bottom layer is explicitly unable to explain the event is not an over-determined account.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The relative weight of the layers cannot be settled by evidence, because it depends on what is being asked. ⚠️ This session therefore teaches the weights as question-dependent rather than fixed, and Lesson 20 gives the mapping.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's directive map records six demand types and what each is really testing, including that "how far" demands require a graded verdict and that "why did a Balkan crisis become a world war" is testing the escalation mechanism rather than the origin. ✅ Its mark-scaled structures allocate two causal layers at 10 marks, three plus the escalation chain at 15, and the addition of war aims, the non-European dimension and the inevitability question at 20.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Account for the outbreak of the First World War.
> ⚠️ How far is it correct to say that the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of balance of power?
> ⚠️ "Sarajevo lit the match, but Europe had already filled the room with fuel." Examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ At 10 marks, use two layers and the escalation chain in one paragraph. ⚠️ At 15, use the concession-and-limit structure. ⚠️ At 20, add the inevitability question and the non-European dimension of Lesson 19. ⚠️ In all three, the verdict must answer the exact wording of the question and not the topic in general.

### Traps

- Listing five causes without saying what each explains.
- Treating "long-term, short-term and immediate" as a sufficient scheme. It omits the regional and operational layers, which is where this topic's specific content lies.
- Asserting that all causes were equally important.
- Putting the operational layer in the origins paragraph or the structural layer in the verdict.
- Omitting the necessity test and therefore giving naval and colonial rivalry the same weight as the Balkan quarrel.
- Producing a balanced-sounding answer with no verdict. ✅ The source's directive map records this as the failure mode of "how far" questions.
- Forgetting that the verdict changes if the question narrows to Britain's entry.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Five layers: immediate, structural, regional, great-power, operational - each with a recorded exam use.
2. ✅ Immediate: the assassination and the ultimatum; use in the opening.
3. ✅ Structural: alliance blocs, nationalism, imperial tension, naval rivalry; use as long-term causes.
4. ✅ Regional: the Austro-Serb struggle over Bosnia and South Slav nationalism; use for Balkan focus.
5. ✅ Great-power: Russian backing for Serbia and German backing for Austria-Hungary; use as the escalation mechanism.
6. ✅ Operational: mobilisation plans, especially Germany's move through Belgium; use to explain why a local war became general.
7. ✅ Limiting statement: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak but not why it became a European and then a world war.
8. ⚠️ Necessity test: naval rivalry and colonial rivalry fail it; the blocs, the Balkan quarrel, the assurance, the mobilisation and the plan pass as necessary but not sufficient; the assassination's necessity is contested.
9. ⚠️ Three orders: chronological, layered, gap-driven. The gap-driven order is best for "how far".
10. ⚠️ Balance of power explains the structural layer and Britain's entry and fails at the regional, operational and proximate layers.
11. ⚠️ Five-step concession-and-limit skeleton: concede with dates, identify the gap, explain why it cannot be absorbed, grade, qualify.
12. ⚠️ Three organising theses - structural, decisional, mechanical - are complementary when each is assigned to its proper paragraph.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the necessity test, identify the two most commonly cited causes of the First World War that do not survive it, and explain what their failure does to the shape of a causation answer.

**Model answer:** The two are the Anglo-German naval race and colonial rivalry. The naval race fails because it had ceased to operate by the time of the crisis: at the end of 1911 Britain held eight Dreadnought-type battleships to Germany's four, Germany reduced naval spending early in 1913 in order to concentrate on the army, and Churchill's assessment for the spring and summer of 1914 was that naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction because Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships. Remove the naval race from July 1914 and every step of the sequence still follows. Colonial rivalry fails because the disputes it generated had always been settled without war, because both Moroccan crises ended in negotiated outcomes including a territorial exchange in 1911, and because in early July 1914 Anglo-German relations were good, an agreement favourable to Germany having just been reached over a possible partition of Portuguese colonies in Africa. Remove colonial rivalry from July 1914 and again nothing changes. Their failure has three consequences for the shape of an answer. It frees space that most scripts waste, so that the Balkan quarrel, the German assurance, the Russian mobilisation and the operational plan can be treated properly. It converts the answer from a list into a graded account, because two items on the standard list are now demoted with dated evidence rather than simply omitted. And it protects the candidate from the commonest criticism of causation answers, which is that everything is asserted to matter equally: an answer that can say precisely which standard causes do not survive a test has demonstrated judgement, which is what a "how far" question is set to measure. The correct demotion is not to silence but to a stated role - the naval race explains why Britain regarded Germany as its principal rival, and colonial competition explains the politics of relative standing within which the decisions were taken.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that a cause which fails the necessity test should be deleted from the answer. It should be reassigned, not deleted, and the reassignment is itself a scoring move: naming what a factor does explain while showing that it was not necessary for the outcome is a more sophisticated operation than either including it uncritically or leaving it out.

*The next lesson takes up the question that the causal architecture makes unavoidable. If specific decisions converted a containable crisis into a general war, then specific people are answerable for them - and historians have been arguing about who, and how much, for more than a century.*

## Lesson 17 - Who was to blame? The historiography, stated accurately

Progress: 17/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the four blame positions recorded in the source; Fischer's thesis and its evidence; Koch's objection and the politics of reception; the preventive-timing reading; the miscalculated-deterrence reading; Ritter and Taylor on the plans; Zuber's documentary challenge; Turner's tragedy of miscalculation; Hamilton and Herwig; Afflerbach and Stevenson on improbability; Williamson on Vienna's autonomy; Rohl on Wilhelm II; Mulligan on Russia; the source's own synthesis; how to write historiography without name-dropping

*The diagram is long because the debate is, and because a candidate who reproduces it in outline can answer any responsibility question that can be set. ⚠️ Read it as three tiers: the four accusations, the three answers to the German question, and the refinements that redistribute weight. ⚠️ Notice that the recorded final conclusion is neither Fischer's nor Williamson's but a graded distribution across three states - which is itself the model for the verdict an answer should give.*

### 1. The four blame positions, and why they are not equivalent

✅ **Fact:** the source opens its assessment by recording that it is difficult to analyse why the assassination developed into a world war, and that even now historians cannot agree. ✅ It lists four positions: some blame Austria for being the first aggressor by declaring war on Serbia; some blame the Russians because they were the first to order full mobilisation; some blame Germany for supporting Austria; and others blame the British for not making it clear that they would definitely support France - on the argument that if the Germans had known this, they would not have declared war on France and the fighting could have been restricted to eastern Europe.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four are not four versions of one claim. They are claims about four different things, and separating them is the first step of a good answer.

| Position | What kind of blame | Strength on this evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Austria-Hungary as first aggressor | Blame for starting a war | ⚠️ Strong on the local war; ✅ weakened for the general war by the recorded expectation of localisation |
| Russia as first to order full mobilisation | Blame for the escalation trigger | ⚠️ Serious; ✅ but mobilisation did not necessarily mean war, and the commitment dated from February 1914 |
| Germany for supporting Austria-Hungary | Blame for enabling and for timing | ⚠️ The strongest, because of the 1913 comparison and the unconditional form of the assurance |
| Britain for insufficient clarity | Blame for an omission | ⚠️ The weakest; ✅ the operational evidence records that a British announcement on 31 July would have made no difference |

⚠️ Note the fourth row carefully. ⚠️ It is a genuine recorded position and should be named, but it is also answerable from within the same evidence base - which makes it an excellent demonstration of critical handling rather than a filler point.

### 2. Fischer, and the reception politics

✅ **Fact:** the source records the historiographical trajectory. After the war, the victorious powers felt the need to justify the harsh Versailles settlement by putting all the blame for the war on Germany; most non-German historians went along with this, though German historians were naturally not happy with this interpretation; after a few years opinion began to move away from laying sole blame on Germany and accepted that other powers should take some of the blame.

✅ **Fact:** then in 1967 a German historian, Fritz Fischer, caused a sensation when he suggested that Germany should, after all, take most of the blame, because they risked a major war by sending the blank cheque to Austria-Hungary. ✅ He claimed that Germany deliberately planned for, and provoked, war with Russia, Britain and France in order to make Germany the dominant power in the world, both economically and politically, and also as a way of dealing with domestic tensions.

✅ **Fact:** the evidence and the objection are recorded, as set out in Lesson 9: the von Muller diary, the "war council" of 8 December 1912, Moltke's recorded remark, Koch's dismissal on the ground that nothing came of it, and the East and West German reception.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things about Fischer are worth knowing and are routinely got wrong.

1. **He was German.** ⚠️ The thesis that Germany bore most of the responsibility was advanced by a German historian against the prevailing German consensus, which is why it caused a sensation. ⚠️ An answer that presents the German-guilt thesis as an Allied position has the historiography backwards.
2. **His claim is stronger than "Germany was responsible".** ⚠️ It is a claim of deliberate planning and provocation for world-power objectives and domestic management. ⚠️ Many scholars who accept substantial German responsibility do not accept that stronger claim - including, on the record, Hamilton and Herwig, whose conclusion is a war for survival rather than for world domination.
3. **The dispute over his evidence is specific.** ⚠️ It concerns the evidentiary weight of a single recorded meeting, not the whole documentary record.

### 3. The three answers to the German question

✅ **Fact:** the source organises the interpretations as three answers to the question of why German policy towards Austria-Hungary changed.

| Reading | ✅ Content | ✅ Evidence recorded for it | ⚠️ What it implies for blame |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Deliberate provocation** (Fischer) | Germany deliberately planned for and provoked war to become the dominant world power and to manage domestic tensions | The blank cheque; the SPD's 1912 result; the January 1914 no-confidence vote; the Muller diary and the December 1912 meeting | Heavy and primary German responsibility, of the strongest kind |
| **Preventive timing** | Germany felt the net was closing in: British naval superiority, massive Russian military expansion, Reichstag refusal of army taxes, French loans to Russia; Moltke, reported by von Jagow, saw no alternative to preventive war before the end of 1914 | The recorded constraints; the reported staff conclusion | Heavy German responsibility of a different kind - a war of choice taken under perceived necessity rather than for domination |
| **Miscalculated deterrence** | Germany did not want a major war at all; Wilhelm II and Bethmann-Hollweg believed a strong line in support of Austria would frighten Russia into neutrality - "a tragic miscalculation, if true" | The 30 July attempt to urge Vienna to negotiate; Wilhelm's proposal of partial mobilisation | Responsibility for recklessness rather than for intent |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three are not equally supported and they are also not mutually exclusive in practice. ⚠️ It is coherent to hold that the general staff reasoned preventively, that the political leadership reasoned deterrently, and that the combination produced an unconditional assurance whose consequences neither had properly weighed. ⚠️ Saying that in one sentence is a stronger conclusion than picking one reading, and it is faithful to the record.

### 4. The operational and contingency schools

✅ **Fact:** Ritter believed the plan was extremely risky and inflexible and deserved to be seen as the start of disaster for Germany and Europe, giving the impression that Germany was ruled by a band of unscrupulous militarists. ✅ Taylor argued that the railway-timetable plans accelerated the tempo of events and reduced negotiating time almost to nil. ✅ Zuber's 2002 study argued that the plan was one of at least five alternatives, that Schlieffen never committed to a single plan, and that the rigid version was constructed after the war to explain defeat. ✅ Taylor later also questioned rigid blueprint claims.

✅ **Fact:** Turner, an Australian historian, suggested that the Germans may not have deliberately provoked war, that war was not inevitable, and that it should have been possible to reach agreement peacefully; the war was caused by a "tragedy of miscalculation" in which most of the leading rulers and politicians seemed incompetent and made bad mistakes. ✅ Turner's four specific miscalculations are recorded: the Austrians thought Russia would not support Serbia; Germany promised support with no conditions attached, so that Germans and Austrians alike were guilty of risking a major war; politicians in Russia and Germany assumed that mobilisation would not necessarily mean war; and, if Ritter and Taylor are right, the generals - especially Moltke - miscalculated by sticking rigidly to their plans in the belief that this would bring a quick and decisive victory.

✅ **Fact:** Bethmann, asked how it all began, raised his arms to heaven and replied: "Oh - if I only knew!"

✅ **Fact:** the 2007 collection edited by Afflerbach and Stevenson, *An Improbable War*, focused on the degree of probability and inevitability in the outbreak; no consensus was reached, but there was a clear leaning towards the view that in the circumstances that existed in 1914 war was certainly not inevitable, though it was possible.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Bethmann remark is the most quotable item in the topic and should be used once, with care. ⚠️ It is evidence of bewilderment at the highest level of a belligerent government, which supports the contingency reading; it is not evidence of innocence, and an answer that deploys it as though it were has misused it.

### 5. The refinements that redistribute weight

✅ **Fact:** Hamilton and Herwig, in *The Origins of World War I* (2002), examine and reject most of the suggested causes - alliance systems, mobilisation plans, the threat of socialism - and conclude that ultimate responsibility for the catastrophe probably rests with Germany. ✅ The Kaiser and his leading advisers and generals believed that time was running out for them as Russia's vast armament plans neared completion; it was a war to ensure survival rather than a war to secure world domination, and it had to take place before Germany's position among the Great Powers deteriorated too far for the war to be won. ✅ Herwig argues that the German leaders gambled on a victorious war even though they knew it was likely to last several years, and that world domination might well come later; in Moltke's words, the Germans took the gamble in 1914 in order to fulfil "Germany's preordained role in civilization", which could "only be done by way of war".

✅ **Fact:** Samuel Williamson, a leading expert on the Habsburg Empire, believes that the government in Vienna had not taken a decision to attack Serbia before the assassinations at Sarajevo, because they had other political priorities; the murders therefore really did provide the decisive moment, and without them there would have been no decision for war in Vienna and therefore no general conflict. ✅ Nor does he believe that German pressure and promises of support were important - the Austrian leaders made their own decisions.

✅ **Fact:** John Rohl argues that the German leaders deliberately started the war and that Wilhelm II bears the main responsibility because of his duplicity and his recklessness.

✅ **Fact:** William Mulligan, in *The Origins of the First World War* (2010), argues that Russia should bear at least equal responsibility. ✅ Russia's defeat by Japan in 1905 had fatal consequences for the peace of Europe: it sparked a revolution that severely weakened the government and forced Russian foreign policy towards the Balkans instead of the Far East. ✅ That policy had two aims - the desire for peace and the necessity of winning back lost prestige - and until 1911 the desire for peace was paramount; but in that year Stolypin, who favoured peace, was assassinated, and the government began to succumb to growing jingoistic public opinion. ✅ Consequently, after the Balkan Wars, in February 1914 the Tsar promised to help the Serbs in the event of an Austro-Hungarian attack and signed a naval agreement with Britain to safeguard access to the Mediterranean if the Germans and Turks should ever try to block the Dardanelles. ✅ On Mulligan's reading these new policies struck German foreign policy with devastating force and produced an important shift in German thinking about the international system: the naval agreement outraged the Germans, who saw it as a betrayal by the British, and the promise of backing for Serbia convinced them that it was vital to support Austria-Hungary; together with the vast Russian military expansion, this was enough to galvanize the Germans into risking a war for survival before Russia became any stronger.

✅ **Fact:** the source's own closing formulation is that perhaps the most sensible conclusion is that Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share the responsibility for the outbreak of war in 1914.

✅ **Fact:** the same positions are recorded in compressed form elsewhere in the evidence base, whose own synthesis is that the crisis began in the Balkans but became a world war because Austria-Hungary, Germany and Russia all treated prestige and security more urgently than restraint. ✅ Special weight is given there to Fischer's argument, and later scholars are noted as leaning towards ultimate German responsibility, while the debate is not closed absolutely and space is left for shared responsibility involving Austria-Hungary and Russia.

### 6. Comparison: the positions sorted by what they are really about

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the debate looks confusing because the positions answer different questions. Sorted by question, it becomes manageable.

| Question the scholar is answering | Positions | ⚠️ How to use it |
|---|---|---|
| Did Germany intend a general war? | Fischer and Rohl say yes; the deterrence reading says no; Hamilton and Herwig say it was a gamble for survival rather than for domination | The core of a "to what extent was Germany responsible" answer |
| Was the plan decisive? | Ritter and Taylor say the plans drove events; Zuber says the plan as described did not exist | The escalation paragraph and the factual-risk control |
| Was anyone in control? | Turner says the war came from miscalculation; Bethmann's remark supports bewilderment at the top | The contingency paragraph |
| Was it inevitable? | Kennan says the fate of Europe was sealed in 1894; Afflerbach and Stevenson's contributors lean towards not inevitable though possible | The verdict of an inevitability question |
| Whose autonomy? | Williamson says Vienna decided for itself and German pressure was not important | The counter to a Berlin-centred answer |
| Does Russia share it? | Mulligan says at least equally, through the post-1905 reorientation and the February 1914 policies | The redistribution paragraph at 20 marks |

### 7. How to write historiography without name-dropping

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** naming historians is optional enrichment, and the source says so explicitly: naming historians is not a requirement for full marks. ⚠️ What is required is that the *positions* be handled. Three rules follow.

1. **Name a scholar only when the name carries a distinct claim you are about to use.** ⚠️ Fischer, Zuber, Turner, Williamson and Mulligan each carry one; a list of eleven names carries none.
2. **State the claim, not the reputation.** ⚠️ "Fischer argued that Germany risked a major war by giving an unconditional assurance" is usable; "Fischer's influential thesis" is not.
3. **Never quote a historian verbatim.** ✅ The recorded factual-risk controls prohibit it, and paraphrase is sufficient at every mark level. ⚠️ Every quoted phrase this session carries is the recorded speech of a participant rather than of a historian: Lloyd George at the Mansion House in 1911, Churchill on the capital-ship balance in 1914, Moltke at the December 1912 meeting and again on the role he claimed for Germany, Bethmann-Hollweg's reply on how it all began, and an unnamed leading German industrialist's remark of 1913. ⚠️ Even those should be used sparingly, and no historian's words are reproduced anywhere in this session.

### 8. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is a "shared responsibility" conclusion not simply a refusal to decide?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It would be if it were undifferentiated. ✅ The recorded conclusion is not that everyone was equally to blame but that three named states must share responsibility - which excludes Britain, France, Serbia and Italy from the primary tier and is therefore a substantive judgement. ⚠️ A graded conclusion of that kind, which names who is in and who is out and says why, is a verdict; "all the powers were to blame" is not.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ If a majority of historians accept Fischer, should a candidate not simply say Germany was responsible?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, for two reasons recorded in the evidence base itself. ✅ First, the same account that records majority acceptance of Fischer also records Williamson's rejection of German pressure as important, Mulligan's case for Russian responsibility, Koch's objection to Fischer's key evidence and the Afflerbach-Stevenson leaning against inevitability - so the majority is not a consensus. ⚠️ Second, an examination answer is assessed on reasoning rather than on alignment with a majority; a script that states the majority position, gives the strongest dissent and then grades is worth more than one that reports a headcount.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The central question posed by the evidence base - why German policy changed between 1913 and 1914 - is recorded as unresolved, and this session does not resolve it.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source supplies a framework for "Who was to blame for the First World War?" in five steps: open with Sarajevo as trigger and not full explanation; explain the Austro-Serb rivalry; add Germany's blank cheque and preventive-war thinking; add Russian mobilisation and military timetables; and conclude with qualified shared responsibility, while noting the source's tilt towards heavy German responsibility. ✅ Its trap records that a "who was responsible" question must never be answered with one sentence naming one state, and must use at least two levels - immediate decision-makers and wider structural setting.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ To what extent was Germany responsible for the outbreak of the First World War?
> ⚠️ "The First World War was a tragedy of miscalculation rather than a crime." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ How far had war become likely, but not inevitable, by 1914? Discuss in the light of competing interpretations.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The five-step framework above is the safest structure for any responsibility question and should be internalised as a sequence. ⚠️ At 15 marks, use three named positions at most. ⚠️ At 20 marks, add the redistribution - Williamson on Vienna's autonomy and Mulligan on Russia - and close on the graded three-state conclusion.

### Traps

- Presenting the German-guilt thesis as an Allied invention. ✅ Fischer was a German historian writing in 1967 against his own national consensus.
- Presenting Fischer's claim as merely that Germany was responsible. ✅ His claim is deliberate planning and provocation for world-power objectives.
- Omitting Koch's objection when using the December 1912 meeting.
- Treating Hamilton and Herwig as agreeing with Fischer in full. ✅ Their conclusion is a war for survival rather than for world domination.
- Using Bethmann's remark as evidence of innocence rather than of bewilderment.
- Omitting Williamson when arguing that Berlin drove Vienna.
- Omitting Mulligan when asked whether responsibility should be redistributed.
- Quoting a historian verbatim. ✅ Prohibited by the source's factual-risk controls.
- Writing "all powers were equally responsible". ✅ The recorded conclusion names three states, not all.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Four blame positions: Austria-Hungary as first aggressor; Russia as first to order full mobilisation; Germany for supporting Austria; Britain for insufficient clarity about supporting France.
2. ✅ Beyond dispute: the Austro-Serb quarrel sparked off the outbreak; it had grown explosive since 1908; the Austrians seized on the assassination as the excuse for a preventive war with Serbia.
3. ✅ Post-war trajectory: Versailles-era sole blame on Germany, then a move away from it, then Fischer in 1967.
4. ✅ Fischer: Germany deliberately planned for and provoked war to become the dominant world power and to manage domestic tensions; evidence from von Muller's diary and the "war council" of 8 December 1912 where Moltke said war was unavoidable and the sooner the better.
5. ✅ Koch: nothing came of the war council. East German historians supported Fischer; West German historians largely did not.
6. ✅ Preventive timing: the net closing in; British naval superiority; massive Russian expansion; Reichstag refusal of army taxes; French loans; von Jagow reports Moltke's conclusion that preventive war must come before the end of 1914.
7. ✅ Miscalculated deterrence: Wilhelm II and Bethmann-Hollweg believed a strong line would frighten Russia into neutrality - a tragic miscalculation, if true.
8. ✅ Ritter: the plan was extremely risky and inflexible. Taylor: timetables accelerated events. Zuber, 2002: at least five alternatives; the rigid plan was a post-war construction.
9. ✅ Turner: a tragedy of miscalculation, with four specific errors by Austria, Germany, the Russian and German politicians, and the generals. Bethmann: "Oh - if I only knew!"
10. ✅ Hamilton and Herwig, 2002: reject alliance systems, mobilisation plans and the socialist threat, and conclude that ultimate responsibility probably rests with Germany - a war for survival rather than for world domination; Herwig: a gamble on victory knowing the war would likely last years.
11. ✅ Afflerbach and Stevenson, 2007: no consensus, but a clear leaning that war was certainly not inevitable though possible.
12. ✅ Williamson: Vienna had taken no decision before the assassinations; the murders provided the decisive moment; German pressure and promises were not important.
13. ✅ Rohl: the German leaders deliberately started the war; Wilhelm II bears the main responsibility for duplicity and recklessness.
14. ✅ Mulligan, 2010: Russia bears at least equal responsibility through the post-1905 reorientation, Stolypin's assassination in 1911, jingoistic opinion, and the February 1914 promise to Serbia and naval agreement with Britain, which shifted German thinking.
15. ✅ Recorded final conclusion: Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share the responsibility.
16. ⚠️ Writing rule: name a scholar only when the name carries a claim you will use; state the claim, not the reputation; never quote a historian.

### Concept check

**Question:** A 15-mark question asks to what extent Germany was responsible for the outbreak of the First World War. Construct the argument, including the strongest recorded case against a Germany-centred verdict, and state the graded conclusion you would give.

**Model answer:** The case for heavy German responsibility rests on three dated findings. First, a reversal: Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War of 1913, and in July 1914 the Kaiser sent a telegram urging Vienna to attack Serbia and promising help without any conditions attached, an unconditional assurance described as a blank cheque. The alliance and the quarrel were identical in both years; the German decision was not. Second, a timing logic: constrained by the Reichstag's refusal to sanction the tax increases army expansion required, and watching a Russian rearmament financed by French loans, the German general staff concluded, as von Jagow reported of Moltke in early 1914, that there was no alternative to a preventive war which must take place before the end of 1914 because Russia would afterwards be too strong. Third, an execution: when Russia mobilised, Moltke refused Wilhelm's proposal of a partial mobilisation against Russia alone on the ground that the railway timetables could not be changed, and Germany declared war on France on 3 August although it had no specific quarrel with France, and entered Belgium. Fischer's stronger claim - deliberate planning and provocation to secure world dominance and to manage domestic tensions, evidenced by von Muller's diary of a meeting on 8 December 1912 - is recorded as accepted by probably a majority of historians, though Koch objected that nothing came of that meeting, and Hamilton and Herwig, while concluding that ultimate responsibility probably rests with Germany, characterise it as a war for survival rather than for world domination. The strongest recorded case against a Germany-centred verdict is Williamson's: Vienna had taken no decision to attack Serbia before the assassinations, the murders supplied the decisive moment, and German pressure and promises were not important because the Austrian leaders made their own decisions; to which Mulligan adds that Russia should bear at least equal responsibility, since its post-1905 turn to the Balkans, the rise of jingoistic opinion after Stolypin's assassination in 1911, the February 1914 promise to Serbia and the naval agreement with Britain shifted German thinking decisively. The graded conclusion is therefore that German responsibility is primary but not exclusive: primary because the assurance of early July was a free decision that reversed a demonstrated capacity for restraint and because the operational execution converted a Balkan war into a western one, and not exclusive because Vienna chose its own course and Russia's commitments and rearmament had already narrowed the German calculation. That is close to the recorded conclusion that Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share responsibility - shared, but not equally, and with Britain, France and Serbia outside the primary tier.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the existence of a scholarly majority as settling the question. The same evidence base that records majority acceptance of Fischer's thesis also records Koch's objection to its key evidence, Williamson's rejection of German pressure as decisive, Mulligan's case for Russian responsibility and a scholarly leaning against inevitability. An answer earns marks by grading these positions against dated evidence, not by counting them.

*The next lesson takes the one question the historiography keeps circling without settling. If the structures did not compel and the decisions were choices, then the war was avoidable in principle - and the interesting work is to say exactly what would have had to be different, and how confident the evidence allows anyone to be about that.*

## Lesson 18 - Agency, misperception and contingency: was it inevitable, and what would have had to change?

Progress: 18/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: likelihood distinguished from inevitability; the five containments as the empirical case; the recorded misperceptions at each capital; Turner's four miscalculations and what unites them; the structure of a permitted counterfactual and the limits of the method; the counterfactuals this evidence base supports and those it does not; how to write about contingency without lapsing into a list of might-have-beens
