━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the inevitability question, resolved by counting

```text
   THE CLAIM UNDER TEST: "BY 1914 WAR WAS INEVITABLE"

   THE STRUCTURES WERE IN PLACE FROM 1907 AT THE LATEST.
   HOW MANY GREAT-POWER CRISES OCCURRED UNDER THOSE STRUCTURES,
   AND HOW MANY BECAME GENERAL WARS?

   1905-06  MOROCCAN CRISIS          -> CONTAINED (Algeciras)
   1908     BOSNIA CRISIS            -> CONTAINED (Russia retreats)
   1911     AGADIR CRISIS            -> CONTAINED (Congo exchange)
   1912     FIRST BALKAN WAR         -> CONTAINED (London conference)
   1913     SECOND BALKAN WAR        -> CONTAINED (Germany restrains
                                       Austria-Hungary)
   1914     JULY CRISIS              -> GENERAL WAR

   FIVE CONTAINMENTS, ONE WAR, SAME STRUCTURES.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   A THEORY THAT PREDICTS WAR FROM THE STRUCTURES ALONE PREDICTS
   SIX WARS AND GETS ONE RIGHT.
   THE VARIABLE IS THEREFORE NOT THE STRUCTURE.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------

   BUT NOTE THE OPPOSITE TREND INSIDE THE SAME RECORD:
   each containment left the next crisis harder to contain
   1906 -> Entente hardens, military conversations begin
   1908 -> Serbia permanently hostile; Russia rearms and resolves
   1911 -> German opinion turns intensely anti-British
   1913 -> Serbia enlarged; Vienna twice frustrated; Berlin
           misreads Britain as detachable
                                |
                                v
   THE DEFENSIBLE VERDICT: BY 1914 WAR HAD BECOME HIGHLY LIKELY
   BUT NOT INEVITABLE. LIKELIHOOD IS A STATEMENT ABOUT THE SYSTEM;
   INEVITABILITY WOULD BE A STATEMENT ABOUT THE FUTURE, AND THE
   RECORD OF FIVE CONTAINMENTS REFUSES IT.
```

*The diagram converts a philosophical question into an empirical one, which is the only way to handle it in an examination. ⚠️ The count is the argument: a structural theory that cannot distinguish the sixth case from the first five is not yet an explanation of the sixth. ⚠️ And the second half of the diagram prevents the opposite error, which is to conclude from five containments that the system was healthy.*

### 1. Likelihood and inevitability: the distinction that carries the lesson

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two words are used interchangeably in weak scripts and they are not interchangeable.

| Term | What it asserts | What would prove it | What would refute it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Likely** | The probability of the outcome was high given the system's condition | Repeated near-misses, accumulating grievances, a shrinking margin | Nothing in the record; the claim is compatible with the outcome not occurring |
| **Inevitable** | No available sequence of decisions could have avoided the outcome | A demonstration that every decision-maker's alternatives were foreclosed | ✅ Any instance of the same configuration not producing the outcome - and the record supplies five |

✅ **Fact:** the source records the rejection of simple inevitability in exactly this form: many historians think the alliance explanation is not convincing, because there had been many crises since 1904 and none of them had led to a major war. ✅ The 2007 collection edited by Afflerbach and Stevenson reached no consensus but leaned clearly towards the view that in the circumstances that existed in 1914 war was certainly not inevitable, though it was possible.

✅ **Fact:** the source supplies the thesis template: by 1914 war had become highly likely but not inevitable; earlier crises had been contained, and it took specific July decisions to break the pattern. ✅ Its factual-risk controls prohibit writing that "alliances made war certain". ✅ The source's line is that alliances created a dangerous framework but did not mechanically predetermine war; political decisions in July 1914 still mattered.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase to carry into an answer is the source's own - **highly likely but not inevitable** - because it is short, graded and defensible on the record. ⚠️ It also has a second advantage: it commits the writer to explaining the July decisions, which is where the marks are.

### 2. The misperceptions, capital by capital

✅ **Fact:** Turner's four miscalculations are recorded, and they are the spine of the contingency argument.

| Actor | ✅ The miscalculation | ⚠️ Why the belief was held |
|---|---|---|
| Vienna | Thought Russia would not support Serbia | ⚠️ Reasoning from 1908, when Russia had backed down; ⚠️ and from the effect of German backing in that year |
| Berlin | Promised support with no conditions attached, thereby risking a major war | ⚠️ On the deterrence reading, believing that firmness would frighten Russia into neutrality; ⚠️ reinforced by the 1912-13 belief that Britain might be detached |
| St Petersburg and Berlin | Assumed that mobilisation would not necessarily mean war | ✅ True in itself - troops could be halted at the frontiers - but false of German planning |
| The generals, especially Moltke | Stuck rigidly to their plans in the belief that this would bring a quick and decisive victory | ⚠️ The six-week promise embedded in the deployment; ⚠️ the fear of losing the race to mobilise |

✅ **Fact:** Bethmann's recorded answer to how it all began was to raise his arms to heaven and say: "Oh - if I only knew!"

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** what unites the four is worth stating explicitly, because it converts a list into an argument. ⚠️ Every one of them is an *inference from the previous crisis*. Vienna extrapolated from 1908; Berlin extrapolated from 1912-13; both capitals extrapolated the meaning of mobilisation from a period in which mobilisations had been reversible; the generals extrapolated from exercises. ⚠️ The July Crisis was misread because every capital was reading it against a case that had been overtaken - which is a general proposition about crisis behaviour and a strong closing line.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice also the asymmetry of the third row. ✅ The belief that mobilisation need not mean war was correct as a general proposition and false as applied to Germany, because German planning could not treat a Russian mobilisation as a pause. ⚠️ So the misperception was not a failure of intelligence but a failure to notice that one participant's machinery had changed the meaning of a shared convention.

### 3. Counterfactuals: the method and its limits

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a counterfactual is a disciplined instrument, not a speculation, and it is admissible in an answer when three conditions hold: the alternative was actually available to the decision-maker at the time; the change is minimal rather than a rewriting of the world; and the consequence follows from evidence rather than from preference.

✅ **Fact:** the evidence base supports a small number of counterfactuals and refuses others, and the distinction is recorded rather than invented.

| Counterfactual | Status on this evidence | ✅ Basis |
|---|---|---|
| If Vienna had not received an unconditional assurance, the Austro-Serb war might not have occurred | **Supported but contested** | ✅ Germany's refusal in 1913 produced no war; ✅ but Williamson holds that Vienna decided for itself and that German promises were not important |
| If the assassination had not occurred, there would have been no decision for war in Vienna and therefore no general conflict | **Recorded as a scholarly position** | ✅ Williamson's stated view, on the ground that Vienna had other political priorities and had taken no decision before the murders |
| If Britain had declared on 31 July that it would support France, Germany would not have attacked France and the fighting could have been restricted to eastern Europe | **Recorded as a blame position and refuted within the same account** | ✅ The recorded position exists; ✅ the operational evidence is that it would have made no difference, because it was the full plan or nothing |
| If Germany had mobilised only against Russia, Britain might have stayed out | **Recorded as Wilhelm's own hope, and refused operationally** | ✅ Wilhelm proposed exactly this on 30 July; ✅ Moltke refused on timetable grounds |
| If the alliance system had not existed, there would have been no war | **Not supported** | ✅ The alliances did not oblige anyone to fight and produced containment five times |
| If the naval race had not occurred, there would have been no war | **Not supported** | ✅ Naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction by spring 1914 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table's value is that two of the six are refuted by the evidence base itself, which is unusual and useful. ⚠️ An answer that says "a clearer British warning would have prevented the war" has adopted a position that the same source records and then answers; an answer that gives both halves is visibly in command of the material.

**Limit of the method.** ⚠️ No counterfactual in this topic can be carried beyond one step. ⚠️ It is defensible to say that without the unconditional assurance the Austro-Serb war might not have occurred in July 1914; it is not defensible to say what Europe would have looked like in 1920, because the Balkan quarrel, the Russian rearmament, the German deadline and the Habsburg nationality problem would all still have existed. ⚠️ This session therefore permits one-step counterfactuals about the crisis and refuses all speculation about the longer future.

### 4. Agency, restated precisely

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "agency" is a word that can hide as much as it reveals. In this topic it means something specific and demonstrable: at six identifiable moments, a named actor had an alternative and chose.

```text
   SIX POINTS AT WHICH A DIFFERENT CHOICE WAS AVAILABLE
   ----------------------------------------------------
   1. EARLY JULY   Berlin could have attached conditions to its
                   assurance, or refused, as it had in 1913
   2. 23 JULY      Vienna could have demanded an investigation
                   without a 48-hour ultimatum
   3. 25-28 JULY   Vienna could have treated the acceptance of most
                   demands as sufficient
   4. 29-30 JULY   St Petersburg could have confined mobilisation to
                   the Austrian frontier
   5. 30-31 JULY   Berlin could have accepted Wilhelm's partial
                   option - and was told by Moltke that it could not
   6. 31 JULY-     Berlin could have declined to declare war on a
      3 AUGUST     state with which it had no specific quarrel
   ----------------------------------------------------
   POINT 5 IS THE ONLY ONE WHERE THE ALTERNATIVE WAS TESTED AND
   REFUSED ON TECHNICAL GROUNDS. THE OTHER FIVE WERE POLITICAL
   CHOICES, FREELY MADE.
```

⚠️ The diagram is the answer to any question that asks whether individuals mattered. ⚠️ Five of the six points are political; only one is mechanical. ⚠️ That ratio is the case against the machine-driven reading of 1914, and it is drawn entirely from recorded decisions.

### 5. Comparison: three positions on contingency

| Position | Claim | ✅ Support | ⚠️ Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Strong determinism** | The structures made war certain | Kennan: the fate of Europe was sealed by 1894; the accumulating crises | ✅ Five containments under the same structures; ✅ nothing binding about the alliances |
| **Strong contingency** | The war was an accident of bad decisions | Turner's four miscalculations; Bethmann's bewilderment; Afflerbach and Stevenson's leaning | ⚠️ Cannot explain why the accidents all pointed the same way, or why the German staff had a deadline |
| **Graded** | War was highly likely and not inevitable; structures loaded the dice and decisions threw them | ✅ The source's own thesis template; ✅ the recorded shared-responsibility conclusion | ⚠️ Requires the candidate to say which decisions mattered most, which is the harder task |

⚠️ The graded position is the one this session teaches, and its cost is honest: it obliges the writer to identify the decisive decisions rather than to shelter behind either the system or the accident.

### 6. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is the five-containment argument not just survivorship reasoning - the crises that were contained are remembered as containable precisely because they were contained?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The objection would bite if the containments had been narrow escapes with no institutional content. ⚠️ They were not. ✅ Algeciras was a conference that produced a settlement; ✅ the Bosnian crisis ended in a completed diplomatic outcome; ✅ Agadir ended in an exchange of territory; ✅ the First Balkan War ended at a conference convened jointly by Britain and Germany; ✅ the Second Balkan War was contained by a German veto on its own ally. ⚠️ These are five instances of working machinery, not five coin-flips, and their existence is exactly what a claim of inevitability has to explain away.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ If each containment made the next crisis harder, is that not simply inevitability arriving on a longer timescale?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It is a trend, not a law, and the difference matters. ⚠️ A trend of declining margin tells you that the probability was rising; it does not tell you that the next crisis had to break. ⚠️ The evidence for that is that the margin was still sufficient in 1913, when a German veto held Vienna back - twelve months before the same relationship produced the opposite result. ⚠️ A system that could still contain a crisis in 1913 was not a system in which war had become impossible to avoid in 1914.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Whether the July decisions were the last point at which the sequence could have been stopped cannot be settled here. ✅ The evidence base records an attempt on 30 July and its refusal on 31 July, and it records no later attempt. ⚠️ What it does not record is whether any of the six alternative choices would have succeeded, and this session does not assert that any would.

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's directive map records that a question asking whether the war was inevitable is testing the ability to distinguish likelihood from inevitability, and that "alliances made war certain" is the prohibited answer. ✅ Its verdict scaffold for the inevitability demand is: highly likely, not inevitable - the same alliance structure had produced containment in 1905-06, 1908 and 1911. ⚠️ That scaffold names three instances rather than counting them; the complete series taught here adds the two Balkan Wars of 1912 and 1913, giving five containments. ✅ Its mark-scaled structure reserves the inevitability question for the 20-mark version.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Was the First World War inevitable? Discuss.
> ⚠️ How far had war become likely, but not inevitable, by 1914? Discuss in the light of competing interpretations.
> ⚠️ "The First World War was a tragedy of miscalculation rather than a crime." Critically examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ On any inevitability question, lead with the count - five containments under identical structures - and then reverse into the trend, that each containment lowered the margin. ⚠️ Close with the graded verdict in the source's own words. ⚠️ Do not open with philosophy; the empirical route is shorter and stronger.

### Traps

- Using "inevitable" as a synonym for "likely".
- Writing that alliances made war certain. ✅ Prohibited by the source's controls.
- Treating the containments as luck rather than as machinery that worked.
- Concluding from contingency that nobody was responsible. Contingency increases responsibility, because it means the decisions mattered.
- Running counterfactuals more than one step, or into the post-war world.
- Using the British-clarity counterfactual without the recorded operational reply.
- Omitting the trend of declining margin and therefore implying that the system was healthy.
- Presenting Turner's miscalculations as a list without noting that each is an extrapolation from a superseded precedent.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Likely and inevitable are different claims: the first is about probability, the second about foreclosure.
2. ✅ Five containments under the same structures: Morocco 1905-06, Bosnia 1908, Agadir 1911, First Balkan War 1912, Second Balkan War 1913.
3. ✅ Recorded rejection of simple inevitability: many crises since 1904 and none led to a major war.
4. ✅ Afflerbach and Stevenson, 2007: no consensus, but a clear leaning that war was certainly not inevitable though possible.
5. ✅ Recorded thesis template: by 1914 war had become highly likely but not inevitable; it took specific July decisions to break the pattern.
6. ✅ Turner's four miscalculations: Vienna on Russian abstention; Berlin's unconditional promise; the shared assumption that mobilisation need not mean war; the generals' faith in a quick victory.
7. ⚠️ What unites them: each is an extrapolation from a precedent that had been overtaken.
8. ✅ Bethmann, asked how it began: "Oh - if I only knew!"
9. ⚠️ Six points of available choice; only one - Moltke's refusal on 30-31 July - was refused on technical rather than political grounds.
10. ⚠️ Permitted counterfactuals: the assurance, the assassination and the partial-mobilisation option, each one step only.
11. ✅ Refuted counterfactuals: British clarity on 31 July, which the operational evidence says would have made no difference; and the removal of the alliances or the naval race, neither of which was necessary to the July sequence.
12. ⚠️ Contingency increases rather than reduces responsibility.

### Concept check

**Question:** Answer the question "was the First World War inevitable?" in a way that uses evidence rather than philosophy, and state what the evidence does not allow you to claim.

**Model answer:** Test the claim by counting. The structures usually said to have made war inevitable - two alliance blocs, arms competition, Balkan instability and imperial insecurity - were all in place by 1907 at the latest. Under those structures the great powers passed through five crises: Morocco in 1905-06, resolved at the Algeciras conference in January 1906; Bosnia in 1908, resolved by a Russian retreat; Agadir in 1911, resolved by Germany's recognition of the French protectorate in exchange for two strips of the French Congo; the First Balkan War of 1912, brought to a conference in London arranged jointly by Sir Edward Grey and the German government; and the Second Balkan War of 1913, in which Germany restrained an Austria-Hungary that was itching to attack Serbia. Five containments and one war under identical structures means that a theory predicting war from structure alone predicts six wars and gets one right, so the variable is not the structure. The same evidence records the rejection of simple inevitability on exactly this ground - many crises since 1904, none producing a major war - and a 2007 collection of essays devoted to the question reached no consensus but leaned clearly towards the view that war in 1914 was certainly not inevitable, though possible. The opposite error must also be avoided: the containments were not free. Each one hardened the Entente, confirmed German encirclement fears, left Serbia larger, left Vienna more frustrated and left Berlin with the belief that Britain might be detached. The margin was falling even as the system kept working. The defensible verdict is therefore that by 1914 war had become highly likely but not inevitable, and that it took specific July decisions to break a pattern that had held five times. What the evidence does not allow is a claim about what would have happened had any of those decisions been different. It supports one-step counterfactuals - that a conditional rather than unconditional German assurance might have produced the restraint of 1913 - but it cannot show that the Balkan quarrel, the German deadline, the Russian rearmament or the Habsburg nationality problem would have gone away, and no statement about Europe after 1914 in a world without this war is defensible from it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that rejecting inevitability means describing the war as an accident. The five containments show that the system could produce peace; the falling margin shows that producing peace was getting harder; and the six identifiable decision points show that the outcome was chosen rather than suffered. Contingency of that kind sharpens the responsibility question rather than dissolving it.

*The next lesson closes the historical argument by answering the third of the questions set out in Lesson 1. The escalation to a European war has been explained. What remains is why a European war became a world war - and the answer is partly in the alliance system, partly in the empires, and partly in the fact that by 1914 no great power's resources stopped at its own frontier.*

### Visual: two separate expansions, and the channels that carried each

```text
   EXPANSION ONE: LOCAL -> GENERAL (28 July to 6 August 1914)
   ----------------------------------------------------------
   CHANNEL A  ALIGNMENT
              Vienna is backed unconditionally by Berlin;
              Belgrade is backed by St Petersburg after a promise
              given in February 1914
   CHANNEL B  PRESTIGE AND STANDING
              a second Russian retreat after 1908 is politically
              unavailable; Vienna's imperial survival is at stake
   CHANNEL C  OPERATIONAL PLANNING
              German deployment requires attacking France first and
              passing through Belgium; there is no limited option
   CHANNEL D  LEGAL OBLIGATION
              the 1839 guarantee of Belgian neutrality gives Britain
              a ground for entry independent of any alliance
   ==================================================================
   EXPANSION TWO: EUROPEAN -> WORLD
   ----------------------------------------------------------
   CHANNEL E  THE BELLIGERENTS WERE EMPIRES
              Britain, France, Germany, Russia and the Ottoman state
              held territory, populations and resources far outside
              Europe; a war between them is automatically a war in
              several continents
   CHANNEL F  AN EXTRA-EUROPEAN ALLIANCE ALREADY EXISTED
              Britain and Japan had signed an alliance in 1902,
              BEFORE the crisis - a non-European great power was
              already inside the European alliance structure
   CHANNEL G  A GLOBAL PATTERN OF JOINT COERCION
              European powers, the USA and Japan had all forced
              concessions on China; the powers were already used to
              acting outside Europe together and against each other
   CHANNEL H  A MARITIME WORLD ECONOMY
              Britain still owned about half the world's tonnage of
              merchant ships in 1914; Russia's main trade route ran
              through the Dardanelles and could be strangled
   ==================================================================
   AND THE RECORDED RESULT
   "Germany was soon to be displaced, for a time at least, from her
   mastery of Europe, and EUROPE NEVER QUITE REGAINED ITS DOMINANT
   POSITION IN THE WORLD."
```

*The diagram separates two expansions that scripts routinely merge. ⚠️ The move from a Balkan war to a European war is a story about decisions and plans in five capitals. The move from a European war to a world war required no further decision at all: it followed from what the belligerents already were.*

### 1. The first expansion, restated as a mechanism

✅ **Fact:** the source poses the question in its own words: the Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak of the war, but not why it became a world war; and it then lists the reasons that have been suggested for the escalation, which Lessons 4 to 6 and 16 have examined.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four transmission channels of the visual are the compressed form of the whole escalation argument, and they can be written in four clauses under examination pressure.

| Channel | What it transmitted | ✅ Anchor |
|---|---|---|
| Alignment | The Austro-Serb dispute to Berlin and St Petersburg | The unconditional assurance; the February 1914 promise to Serbia |
| Prestige and standing | The impossibility of a second retreat, and the survival stake | Russia determined to avoid further humiliation after 1908; Serbian success threatening Habsburg internal survival |
| Operational planning | A war against Russia into a war against France and Belgium | Germany had no specific quarrel with France; the plan required passage through Belgium and the rapid capture of Liege |
| Legal obligation | A continental war into a British war | The 1839 promise to defend Belgian neutrality; the demand for withdrawal; entry on 4 August |

⚠️ Note the progression: each channel transmits the conflict to a wider circle, and each requires a different kind of explanation - political, psychological, technical and legal. ⚠️ An answer that names the four kinds has explained escalation; an answer that lists dates has narrated it.

### 2. The second expansion: what the belligerents already were

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the crucial point about the globalisation of the war is that it required no separate decision. Four of the six original belligerents held extensive territory, populations and trade outside Europe: Britain, France and Germany overseas, and Russia contiguously across northern Asia to the Pacific. Austria-Hungary's territory was entirely European and Serbia had none at all, so the imperial route runs through four of the six and not through all of them. ⚠️ The moment those four were at war with each other, their possessions, their shipping and the populations they governed were at war too - not by anyone's choice in August 1914 but by the structure of what those states were.

✅ **Fact:** the evidence base records the imperial fact directly. ✅ The European powers had taken part in a great burst of imperialist expansion after 1880; most of Africa was taken over by the European states; ✅ Germany had taken possession of some islands in the Pacific and some territory in Africa; ✅ and the European powers, the USA and Japan had all at different times forced the helpless Chinese to grant trading concessions.

✅ **Fact:** the alliance route outward is recorded and dated: Japan and Britain had signed an alliance in 1902.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1902 alliance deserves more prominence than it usually gets in this topic, for two reasons. ⚠️ First, it means the alliance system of 1914 was never purely European: a non-European great power was inside it from the beginning, twelve years before the crisis. ⚠️ Second, it is the clearest demonstration available that the pre-war order was already global in its operation even though its decisions were taken in European capitals - which closes the loop with the mismatch identified in Lesson 2.

✅ **Fact:** the maritime and economic dimension is recorded in two places: Britain still owned about half the world's tonnage of merchant ships in 1914, and the Dardanelles were the outlet from the Black Sea and the main Russian trade route, which could be strangled - and, the account records, this happened to some extent during the war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Straits point is the single most economical way to show that the war's global character was not merely a matter of colonies. ⚠️ A blockade of one strait in south-eastern Europe altered the economic life of the largest state in Europe. ⚠️ In a system that integrated, a great-power war could not be geographically contained even in principle.

### 3. India and the wider empire: one bounded statement

✅ **Fact:** the source sets the boundary for this topic explicitly. Its chapter is about Europe, and for examination purposes it is useful to connect the outbreak of war to imperial politics rather than repeating Indian detail here. ✅ It records the instruction that one sentence is enough in an answer: the war globalised European conflict and pulled colonies into a crisis whose political after-effects were felt far beyond Europe. ✅ It routes Indian wartime politics to the Modern Indian History owners.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this session follows that instruction exactly. ⚠️ The permitted statement is that the outbreak of a war between empires drew their colonial territories and populations into a European quarrel, and that the political consequences of that involvement were felt outside Europe. ⚠️ Nothing about Indian wartime expectations, recruitment, the Home Rule agitation, the Lucknow Pact or post-war disappointment appears in this session, because all of it belongs to other owners and none of it is evidence about the outbreak.

### 4. The recorded consequence for the world order

✅ **Fact:** the source closes its narrative of the outbreak with the consequence, and it is the sentence that justifies treating this topic as a hinge in world history. The war was to have profound effects on the future of the world; Germany was soon to be displaced, for a time at least, from her mastery of Europe, and Europe never quite regained its dominant position in the world.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read that against Lesson 2's opening, which recorded that Europe still dominated the rest of the world in 1914 and that most decisions shaping the fate of the world were taken in European capitals. ⚠️ The two sentences bracket the topic: it opens with European primacy asserted and closes with European primacy ended. ⚠️ Using them as a pair - first line and last line of an answer - is one of the most effective framing devices available on this topic, and both halves are sourced.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ How that displacement happened, what the war did to the four empires that collapsed, and what the settlements of 1919-23 built in their place belong entirely to the First World War and aftermath owner. ⚠️ This session asserts the consequence and narrates none of it.

### 5. Comparison: three ways a war becomes a world war

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is worth distinguishing the routes, because they carry different evidence and a question may target one of them.

| Route | Mechanism | ✅ Evidence in 1914 | ⚠️ Weight |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Extension by alliance** | A non-European power is already bound into the European structure | The Anglo-Japanese alliance of 1902 | ⚠️ Decisive as a route, because it operates without any new decision by the European belligerents |
| **Extension by possession** | The belligerents govern territory and populations outside Europe | The post-1880 partition; German Pacific islands and African territory | ⚠️ Automatic and immediate; the war exists wherever the empires do |
| **Extension by economy** | Global shipping, trade routes and choke points are inside the war zone | Britain owning about half the world's merchant tonnage; the Dardanelles as Russia's main trade route, strangled to some extent during the war | ⚠️ Slower but pervasive; it converts neutrals into participants in the war's economics |

⚠️ The first route is the one that most cleanly answers the question, because it requires no further explanation: the world war was latent in the alliance system from 1902.

### 6. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is calling it a "world war" in August 1914 not an anachronism, since the fighting at that point was European?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Partly, and the honest answer distinguishes the fighting from the conflict. ⚠️ In August 1914 the combat was European and the belligerent *system* was already global: an extra-European alliance existed, the belligerents' possessions spanned several continents, and the economic effects ran through world shipping. ✅ The account's own formulation is that other countries joined later, which concedes that the geographical extension was progressive. ⚠️ The precise statement is that the war was global in its participants' reach from the first week and became global in its theatres progressively - and the progressive part belongs to the next owner.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the imperial route not simply reintroduce the colonial-rivalry thesis that Lesson 3 rejected?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, and the distinction is exact. ⚠️ Lesson 3 rejected colonial rivalry as a *cause* of the war. This lesson uses imperial possession as an explanation of the war's *extent*. ⚠️ The powers did not go to war over colonies; but because they held colonies, the war they went to for other reasons was fought on several continents. ⚠️ Cause and extent are different questions, and an answer that keeps them apart avoids the topic's most seductive confusion.

**The residual.** ⚠️ This evidence base records nothing about colonial recruitment, mobilisation of imperial resources or the war's conduct outside Europe, and this session asserts nothing about them.

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the source's directive map records that a question asking why a Balkan crisis became a world war is testing the escalation mechanism rather than the origin, and that repeating the causes of Austro-Serb hostility is the wrong response. ✅ Its 20-mark structure requires the addition of the non-European and imperial dimension. ✅ The syllabus clause names "world wars ... their forms and effect on the society", so the global character of the conflict is directly examinable.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war in 1914?
> ⚠️ "The First World War was a world war before the first shot was fired." Comment.
> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the outbreak of the First World War for the global order.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Answer the escalation question with the four transmission channels, in four clauses, with the decision-maker named in each. ⚠️ Answer the globalisation question with the three routes, giving the 1902 alliance first because it requires no further argument. ⚠️ Close with the bracketing pair from Lesson 2 and this lesson: a Europe that still took the world's decisions in 1914 and never quite regained its dominant position afterwards.

### Traps

- Answering the escalation question with the causes of Austro-Serb hostility. ✅ Explicitly the wrong response.
- Omitting the 1902 Anglo-Japanese alliance, which is the clearest route outward.
- Reintroducing colonial rivalry as a cause when using empire to explain extent.
- Narrating the war's non-European theatres. They belong to the next owner.
- Importing Indian wartime politics beyond the single permitted sentence.
- Omitting the recorded consequence for European primacy, which is the topic's closing line.
- Claiming that the war was global in its theatres from the first week. ✅ Other countries joined later.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak but not why it became a world war.
2. ⚠️ Four transmission channels from local to general: alignment, prestige and standing, operational planning, legal obligation.
3. ✅ Alignment anchors: the unconditional German assurance; the Russian promise to Serbia of February 1914.
4. ✅ Operational anchor: Germany had no specific quarrel with France, yet declared war on 3 August because the plan ran through Belgium to France.
5. ✅ Legal anchor: the 1839 promise to defend Belgian neutrality; the demand for withdrawal; British entry on 4 August.
6. ⚠️ Three routes from European to world war: extension by alliance, by possession and by economy.
7. ✅ Britain and Japan had signed an alliance in 1902 - a non-European great power inside the European structure before the crisis.
8. ✅ The post-1880 partition; German Pacific islands and African territory; joint coercion of China by European powers, the USA and Japan.
9. ✅ Britain still owned about half the world's tonnage of merchant ships in 1914; the Dardanelles were Russia's main trade route and were strangled to some extent during the war.
10. ✅ Other countries joined later - the geographical extension was progressive.
11. ✅ Permitted single sentence on empire: the war globalised European conflict and pulled colonies into a crisis whose political after-effects were felt far beyond Europe.
12. ✅ Recorded consequence: Germany was soon displaced from her mastery of Europe, and Europe never quite regained its dominant position in the world.
13. ⚠️ Cause and extent are different questions; imperial possession explains extent, not cause.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the First World War became a world war, distinguishing carefully between the reasons a Balkan quarrel became a European war and the reasons a European war extended beyond Europe.

**Model answer:** The two expansions have different explanations, and merging them is the standard error. The Balkan quarrel became a European war through four transmissions, each requiring a decision. Alignment carried it outward: Berlin gave Vienna an unconditional assurance, and St Petersburg was committed to Belgrade by a promise the Tsar had given in February 1914. Prestige and survival made retreat unavailable: Russia had resolved after the humiliation of 1908 never to retreat again, and Vienna believed that South Slav success would destroy the Habsburg Empire from within. Operational planning converted a war against Russia into a war against France and Belgium, since German deployment required attacking France first and passing through Belgium to take Liege quickly - which is why Germany declared war on 3 August on a state with which, as the record notes, it had no specific quarrel. And legal obligation brought in a power that was not treaty-bound to fight: Britain had promised in 1839 to defend Belgian neutrality, demanded withdrawal, and entered on 4 August when the demand was ignored. The extension beyond Europe required no further decision, because it followed from what the belligerents already were. An extra-European great power was already inside the alliance structure, Britain and Japan having allied in 1902. The belligerents were empires whose territory and populations lay across several continents after the great burst of expansion that followed 1880, so a war between them existed wherever they did. And the world economy was maritime and integrated: Britain still owned about half the world's merchant tonnage in 1914, and Russia's main trade route ran through the Dardanelles and was strangled to some extent during the war. The war was therefore global in the reach of its participants from the first week, while becoming global in its theatres progressively, since other countries joined later. The recorded consequence closes the argument: Germany was soon displaced from her mastery of Europe, and Europe never quite regained its dominant position in the world.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the empires to explain why the war happened. The colonial disputes of the great powers had always been settled without war, and Anglo-German colonial relations were good in early July 1914. Imperial possession explains the war's extent, not its cause, and keeping the two questions apart is what allows an answer to use empire confidently without contradicting its own earlier reasoning.

*The final lesson converts everything into examination technique. The history is finished; what remains is the set of decisions a candidate has to make in the first ninety seconds of an answer - which question is being asked, which layers to use, which verdict to give, and which factual risks to avoid.*

### Visual: the ninety-second decision procedure

```text
   READ THE STEM. ANSWER FOUR QUESTIONS BEFORE WRITING ANYTHING.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   Q1  WHICH OF THE THREE QUESTIONS IS IT?
       outbreak of the Austro-Serb war | escalation to general war |
       extension to world war
       -> decides which evidence is relevant

   Q2  WHAT DOES THE DIRECTIVE DEMAND?
       "How far / to what extent"  -> GRADED VERDICT
       "Account for"               -> LAYERED CAUSATION
       "Was it inevitable"         -> LIKELIHOOD vs FORECLOSURE
       "Who was responsible"       -> TWO LEVELS: deciders + system
       "Role of X"                 -> ISOLATE X, SHOW INTERACTION
       "Why did a Balkan crisis
        become a world war"        -> MECHANISM, NOT ORIGIN

   Q3  WHICH LAYERS DOES IT NEED?
       10 marks -> 2 layers + escalation in one paragraph + verdict
       15 marks -> concession + 3 gaps + escalation chain + graded
                   verdict
       20 marks -> all of the above + war aims once war began +
                   the non-European dimension + the inevitability
                   question

   Q4  WHAT IS THE VERDICT SENTENCE?
       write it FIRST, in the margin, before the introduction
       -> everything else is then built to support one sentence
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   NINETY SECONDS SPENT HERE SAVES THE ANSWER.
```

*The procedure exists because the commonest failure on this topic is not ignorance but misdirection: a candidate who knows the material writes a causes essay in answer to an escalation question, or a balanced survey in answer to a "how far" question. ⚠️ Both lose heavily, and both are prevented by four questions asked before the pen moves.*

### 1. The directive and demand map

✅ **Fact:** the source records six demand types with what each is really testing and what must not be written.

| ✅ If the question says | ✅ It is really testing | ✅ Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| How far / to what extent | A graded verdict: partly correct, and here is what it misses | A yes/no answer, or a balanced-sounding answer with no verdict |
| Account for the outbreak | Layered causation: immediate, structural, regional, escalatory, operational | A July 1914 diary |
| Was it inevitable? | Distinguishing likelihood from inevitability | "Alliances made war certain" |
| Who was responsible? | Two levels - decision-makers and system | Naming one state in one sentence |
| Role of nationalism, imperialism or militarism | Isolating one factor while showing its interaction | Reproducing the whole causes essay |
| Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war? | The escalation mechanism, not the origin | Repeating the causes of Austro-Serb hostility only |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third column is the more valuable one. ⚠️ Each entry names a specific failure mode, and each failure mode is the default behaviour of a well-prepared candidate who has not decoded the directive. ⚠️ Reading the third column once before an examination is worth more than re-reading the chronology.

### 2. Qualified thesis templates

✅ **Fact:** the source supplies three thesis templates, which this session reproduces in substance.

| Demand | ⚠️ Thesis |
|---|---|
| The exact "how far / balance of power" demand | Balance-of-power calculation was the organising logic of the alliance system that turned a Balkan quarrel into a continental war, but it was not the purpose for which the war was fought: Austria-Hungary fought for imperial survival, Serbia and the South Slavs for national unification, Germany against encirclement and for a preventive advantage, Russia for prestige and Balkan position, and Britain on a concrete legal-strategic trigger. Balance of power explains the structure, not the motives or the timing |
| Causation generally | The war originated in a regional nationality conflict, but it became general because the European system had converted every regional dispute into a test of great-power standing |
| Inevitability | By 1914 war had become highly likely but not inevitable; earlier crises had been contained, and it took specific July decisions to break the pattern |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each of these is a *thesis*, meaning it can be disagreed with. ⚠️ That is the property an examiner is looking for. ⚠️ A sentence that no one could dispute - "the causes of the First World War were complex" - is not a thesis and earns nothing.

### 3. Mark-scaled structures

✅ **Fact:** the source's mark-scaled structures are recorded as follows.

| ✅ Marks | ✅ Structure |
|---|---|
| 10 | Thesis, then two causal layers such as alliance structure and Balkan nationalism, then escalation in one paragraph, then verdict |
| 15 | Thesis, then concede what balance of power explains, then three things it does not - nationalism, imperial insecurity, mobilisation rigidity - then the July escalation chain, then a graded verdict |
| 20 | All of the above, plus war aims once war began, the non-European and imperial dimension, and the inevitability question, with a verdict that answers the exact wording |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that the 20-mark version does not add more causes; it adds three *different kinds* of content - what the belligerents fought for once fighting began, the global dimension, and the modal question of inevitability. ⚠️ Length is not the difference between 15 and 20 marks; range of analytical operation is.

**Word-ceiling discipline.** ⚠️ Answers are written at 150 words for 10 marks and 250 words for 15 and 20 marks, which is the established convention used throughout this session's Mains practice. ⚠️ At 250 words, the concession-and-limit structure fits in five short paragraphs and nothing else does. ⚠️ A candidate who has not practised compressing it will overrun.

### 4. Evidence bank A: what balance of power does explain

✅ **Fact:** the source's first evidence bank concedes, with named evidence.

| ✅ Claim | ✅ Named evidence | ⚠️ Why it supports the reading |
|---|---|---|
| Europe had organised itself into two counterweighted blocs | Triple Alliance of Germany, Austria-Hungary and Italy, 1882; Franco-Russian alliance 1894; Entente Cordiale 1904; Anglo-Russian agreement 1907 | Alliance formation was about not letting any bloc become preponderant |
| Germany read the Entente as encirclement | The recorded German fear of being encircled by Britain, France and Russia | Encirclement is a balance-of-power fear by definition |
| Naval competition was a power-ratio contest | Anglo-German naval rivalry; eight Dreadnought-type ships to four at the end of 1911 | Britain's concern was the relative naval balance, not territory |
| Britain's entry followed a strategic-legal trigger | Germany entered Belgium; Britain entered on 4 August 1914 | A hostile great power on the Channel coast is the classic balance-of-power threat |
| Balkan outcomes were treated as power transfers | Bosnia Crisis 1908; the Balkan Wars of 1912-13 strengthening Serbia and alarming Austria-Hungary; Russia resolved not to suffer humiliation again | Each side scored regional change as a shift in the great-power ledger |
| Earlier crises were managed by the same logic | Moroccan Crisis 1905-06; Agadir 1911 | Shows a functioning balance system - and therefore that it did not require war |

⚠️ The last row is the one that looks like a concession and works as an argument. ⚠️ If the balance system functioned, then it did not need war; so a war fought "for the preservation of the balance of power" has to explain why preservation required war in 1914 when it had not in 1906, 1908 or 1911.

### 5. Evidence bank B: what balance of power fails to explain

✅ **Fact:** the source's second evidence bank is the core of the "how far" answer.

| ✅ Missing explanation | ✅ Named evidence | ⚠️ Why balance of power cannot absorb it |
|---|---|---|
| Imperial survival, not equilibrium | Serbian success threatened not just Austria's prestige but the internal survival of the Habsburg Empire | A multi-national empire's fear of dissolution is an internal nationality problem, not a ratio-of-power calculation |
| Nationalism as a positive aim | Serbian nationalism and the ambition of a larger South Slav or Yugoslav state | South Slav unification sought to create a state, not to preserve an equilibrium |
| Revanchism | French resentment at the loss of Alsace-Lorraine in 1871 | A territorial-national grievance, not a balancing objective |
| Preventive-war timing | German backing for Austria and mobilisation once Russia mobilised; the reasoning that action must come before Russia grew stronger | Preventive war seeks to change the future balance, which is the opposite of preserving one |
| Operational rigidity | Mobilisation plans, especially the move through Belgium | Timetables, not diplomats, compressed the decision window - a mechanical cause, not a strategic purpose |
| Contingent July decisions | The Austrian ultimatum; Russian mobilisation for Serbia; German declarations of 1 and 3 August; British entry on 4 August | Identical structural conditions had existed in 1908 and 1911 without general war |
| Domestic and regime pressures | Autocratic Russia and Austria-Hungary and semi-constitutional Germany faced internal strain that made external firmness attractive | Balance-of-power theory treats states as unitary; it cannot see this |
| What the war became | The conflict pulled in the wider world, imperial manpower and territory | Once fought, the war's aims went far beyond restoring the 1914 equilibrium |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second column of this bank is a complete answer plan by itself. ⚠️ Three of its rows, with their named evidence and their "why it cannot absorb it" reasons, will fill the central 150 words of a 250-word answer.

### 6. The escalation chain, written out

✅ **Fact:** the source supplies the chain in this form, which this session has taught in Lessons 14 and 15.

```text
   Sarajevo, 28 June 1914          [immediate trigger - not the cause]
      -> Austria-Hungary's ultimatum and declaration on Serbia,
         28 July                    [imperial survival motive]
      -> Russian mobilisation for Serbia
                                    [prestige and Balkan position,
                                     after the 1908 humiliation]
      -> German backing for Austria and counter-mobilisation
                                    [alliance choice + preventive
                                     timing]
      -> German attack on France through Belgium
                                    [operational plan overrides
                                     diplomacy]
      -> Britain enters, 4 August    [Belgian neutrality + Channel
                                     and naval balance]
      => a Balkan nationality quarrel becomes a general European war
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source supplies the reading instruction with the chain, and it is the single most valuable sentence in the whole answer architecture: read the chain back against the question, because only the first and last links are cleanly balance-of-power and the middle links are imperial, national and operational. ⚠️ That asymmetry is the answer to "how far".

### 7. Verdict scaffolds

✅ **Fact:** the source records three verdicts.

| ✅ Demand | ⚠️ Verdict |
|---|---|
| "How far ... essentially for the preservation of balance of power?" | Correct as a description of the system that made general war possible and of Britain's entry; substantially incomplete as a statement of why the belligerents chose war in July 1914. Balance of power was the medium through which other motives - Habsburg survival, South Slav nationalism, German preventive calculation, Russian prestige - were transmitted and amplified. It is a necessary part of the explanation and not the essential one |
| "Who was responsible?" | Immediate responsibility lies with Vienna's decision for war and Berlin's unconditional backing; systemic responsibility lies with a militarised alliance order in which every state believed restraint would be read as weakness |
| "Was it inevitable?" | Highly likely, not inevitable - the same alliance structure had produced containment in 1905-06, 1908 and 1911, and, on this session's fuller series, in 1912 and 1913 as well |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each verdict has the same internal shape - a concession, a limit and a grading word. ⚠️ "A necessary part of the explanation and not the essential one" is the model: it concedes necessity, denies essentiality and answers the exact adverb in the question.

### 8. The complete factual-risk control list

✅ **Fact:** the source's controls, reproduced in full because every one of them is a real examination risk.

- ✅ Do not write that alliance treaties obliged every signatory to fight in 1914. Italy was in the Triple Alliance and entered on the Allied side in 1915.
- ✅ Do not say Britain entered because of the alliance with France. Britain had no binding alliance obligation of that kind; Belgian neutrality was the immediate legal-political issue.
- ✅ Do not present the Schlieffen Plan as an uncontested fixed blueprint; the dispute is recorded. Say that German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium.
- ✅ Do not give casualty, army-size, dreadnought or arms-spending figures beyond those sourced here. The only capital-ship counts available are eight to four at the end of 1911. This control governs quantitative evidence about capacity, loss and wealth; dates, intervals and sequence numbers are unaffected.
- ✅ Do not name a historian's position unless it is recorded, and do not quote any historian verbatim.
- ✅ Do not date the Balkan Wars, the Bosnia Crisis or Agadir loosely: 1912-13, 1908 and 1911.
- ✅ Do not claim that Germany planned the war for years. That is recorded as a contested historiographical position, not as an established fact.

✅ **Fact:** the source's misconception list adds four more corrections: Italy did not fight from the start with Germany and Austria-Hungary; naval rivalry mattered more directly than colonial rivalry in Anglo-German antagonism; Britain did not enter only because of France; and the Balkans were not a side-show but the immediate ignition zone.

⚠️ **This session adds three controls of its own**, each derived from material taught above: do not assert a single certain date for the Russian general mobilisation where the records differ between 29 and 30 July; do not assert a single certain date for the German entry into Belgium where the evidence base itself carries both 3 and 4 August; and do not attribute the Sarajevo plot to the Serbian government, which is recorded only as an Austrian allegation.

### 9. The seven boundary handovers

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each handover is one clause. Using them prevents both trespass and omission, because naming a boundary shows the examiner that the omission is deliberate.

| Material | Handover clause |
|---|---|
| The Concert of Europe and the congress system | "The nineteenth-century congress system, which had managed earlier crises, belongs to the Vienna settlement" |
| Unification and Alsace-Lorraine | "The province had been transferred at the end of the Franco-Prussian War of 1871, and the resulting French grievance is taken here as given" |
| The partition of Africa | "The partition after 1880 is assumed here; its mechanics belong to the New Imperialism question" |
| The war's military course | "The campaign that opened at Liege on 5 August belongs to the history of the war itself" |
| The 1919-23 settlements | "The peace settlements, and the war-guilt clause that shaped the first phase of this historiography, lie beyond the outbreak" |
| The Russian Revolution | "Durnovo's warning that a long war could bring down the regime was made before the war; its fulfilment belongs elsewhere" |
| India and the empire in the war | "The war globalised a European conflict and drew colonies into a crisis whose political after-effects were felt far beyond Europe" |

### 10. Opening and closing architecture

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two sentences at each end do most of the work.

```text
   OPENING (two sentences)
   S1  Name the question and the trigger-cause distinction.
       "The assassination at Sarajevo on 28 June 1914 was the
        immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on
        Serbia; the general war requires a separate explanation."
   S2  State the thesis with its grading word.
       "Balance-of-power calculation explains the structure within
        which that explanation must be found, but not the motives
        or the timing of the decisions that produced it."

   CLOSING (two sentences)
   S1  Deliver the graded verdict in the question's own words.
   S2  Add the qualification that shows the verdict is
       question-sensitive.
       "If the question were confined to Britain's entry, the
        balance-of-power reading would be substantially stronger."
```

⚠️ The closing qualification is the highest-value sentence available on this topic, because it demonstrates that the candidate has understood what the verdict depends on rather than having memorised it.

### 11. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is a prepared architecture not simply a template, and will examiners not penalise templated answers?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Examiners penalise answers that ignore the question, and templates cause that when the template is a content template. ⚠️ What this lesson supplies is a *decision procedure* and a set of verdicts, not a pre-written answer: the four questions of the visual produce different structures for different stems, and the verdict scaffolds are conditional on the demand. ⚠️ The discipline that prevents templating is Q4 - write the verdict sentence first, from the stem's own wording.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the emphasis on grading not risk fence-sitting?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Grading and fence-sitting are opposites. ⚠️ A graded verdict says which part of a proposition is correct, which is not, and why; a fence-sitting answer says that there is much to be said on both sides. ✅ The source's directive map names the second as the failure mode of "how far" questions - a balanced-sounding answer with no verdict.

**The residual.** ⚠️ No answer architecture can substitute for the content of Lessons 1 to 19. ⚠️ This lesson is a set of instructions for deploying evidence; without the evidence it produces an empty shape.

### 12. Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Four questions before writing: which of the three topics, what the directive demands, which layers, and what the verdict sentence is.
2. ✅ Six demand types with their real tests and their prohibited answers.
3. ✅ Three thesis templates: balance of power as structure not purpose; regional origin and systemic generalisation; highly likely but not inevitable.
4. ✅ Mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20, with the 20-mark version adding war aims, the non-European dimension and inevitability.
5. ✅ Evidence bank A: two blocs, encirclement, the naval ratio, Britain's entry, Balkan outcomes as power transfers, and the record of earlier management.
6. ✅ Evidence bank B: imperial survival, positive nationalism, revanchism, preventive timing, operational rigidity, contingent July decisions, domestic pressures, and what the war became.
7. ✅ The escalation chain with the reading instruction: only the first and last links are cleanly balance-of-power.
8. ✅ Three verdict scaffolds, each with concession, limit and a grading word.
9. ✅ Seven factual-risk controls plus four misconception corrections, and three added by this session.
10. ⚠️ Seven boundary handovers, one clause each.
11. ⚠️ Opening and closing architecture, two sentences at each end, with a question-sensitive qualification at the close.
12. ⚠️ 150 words at 10 marks and 250 at 15 and 20; the concession-and-limit structure fits 250 words in five short paragraphs.

### Concept check

**Question:** You have ninety seconds before writing an answer to the exact demand: "How far is it correct to say that the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of balance of power?" Set out the decisions you would take and the verdict sentence you would commit to.

**Model answer:** The first decision is which question is being asked: this is a question about the purposes of the belligerents, so it spans the outbreak and escalation questions and does not require the global-extension material except as a closing qualification. The second is the directive: "how far ... essentially" is a graded-verdict demand, so a yes, a no, or a balanced survey without a verdict will all fail. The third is the structure: at 15 marks and 250 words the concession-and-limit shape is the only one that fits, so the plan is to concede what balance of power genuinely explains, identify three things it cannot absorb with named evidence and a stated reason for each, run the escalation chain once, and grade. The concession names the bloc formation of 1882, 1894, 1904 and 1907, the German encirclement complaint, the naval competition as a contest over a ratio, and Britain's entry over Belgium and the Channel; it should also concede that the system had functioned, since Algeciras in 1906 and the Agadir settlement of 1911 were balance-of-power outcomes reached without war. The three limits are Habsburg imperial survival, which is an internal nationality problem and not a ratio calculation; South Slav nationalism, which sought to create a state rather than preserve an equilibrium; and German preventive timing, which sought to change a future balance and is therefore the opposite of preserving one - to which operational rigidity can be added if space allows, since a railway timetable is not a strategic purpose. The escalation chain is then read back against the stem: only the first link, the bloc structure, and the last, Britain's entry, are cleanly balance-of-power, while the middle links are imperial, national and operational. The verdict sentence, written before the introduction, is: balance-of-power calculation was the organising logic of the system that made a general war possible and it explains Britain's entry, but it was not the purpose for which the belligerents chose war in July 1914 - a necessary part of the explanation and not the essential one. The closing qualification is that if the question were confined to Britain's entry, the balance-of-power reading would be substantially stronger.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "essentially" as a decorative adverb. It is the whole question. The stem does not ask whether balance of power was a cause; it asks whether it was the essential one, and an answer that proves it was a cause has proved something the question already concedes. Every "how far" stem contains such a word, and locating it is the first act of reading the question.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

## Direct ownership

✅ **Fact:** this topic owns exactly one verified Mains question in the audited spans. The routing ledger for 2024-2025 records it as **2024, General Studies Paper I, Question 12**, themed on the First World War fought for the preservation of the balance of power, with the metadata **15 marks and 250 words**, and routes it to this topic.

✅ **Fact:** the exact wording is held in the canonical owners, which record it as verified from the locally held official paper: **"How far is it correct to say that the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of balance of power? (Answer in 250 words)"**. ✅ The subject's folder README records the same wording and routes it to the source's answer-architecture section.

⚠️ **A recorded discrepancy, displayed rather than resolved.** The routing ledger's directive column reads **Comment**, while the wording held in the owners is a **"How far is it correct to say"** formulation. ⚠️ These are not the same directive: one invites a substantiated observation, the other requires a graded verdict on a stated proposition. ⚠️ The ledger's own scope note records that its theme column names the subject matter in neutral words and that it is a pointer rather than an answer key, which is consistent with the directive column being the ledger's classification rather than the paper's word. ⚠️ **Preparation consequence:** prepare the question as a graded-verdict demand, because that treatment also satisfies a "comment" directive, whereas the reverse is not true. ⚠️ Do not reproduce a directive word as though it were verified when the two held records differ.

### The demand, decoded

| Element | Reading |
|---|---|
| "How far is it correct to say" | ⚠️ A graded verdict is compulsory. A yes, a no, or a balanced survey with no verdict all fail |
| "essentially" | ⚠️ The operative word. The question does not ask whether balance of power was *a* cause; it asks whether it was *the* essential one |
| "was fought" | ⚠️ A question about **purposes**, not about origins alone. It therefore requires the belligerents' motives, and at 20 marks it would also admit what the war became once fighting began |
| "preservation of balance of power" | ⚠️ Preservation is a conservative aim. Any motive that sought to *change* a distribution - preventive advantage, national unification, territorial recovery - is outside it by definition |
| 250 words | ⚠️ Five short paragraphs. Only the concession-and-limit structure fits |

### The answer route, mapped to this session

| Step | Content | Taught in |
|---|---|---|
| Thesis | Balance of power explains the structure, not the motives or the timing | Lesson 16, Lesson 20 |
| Concession, with dates | Triple Alliance 1882; Franco-Russian alliance 1894; Entente Cordiale 1904; Anglo-Russian agreement 1907; the German encirclement complaint; the naval ratio contest; Britain's entry on 4 August; and the fact that the system had produced settlements at Algeciras in 1906 and over Agadir in 1911 | Lessons 4, 5, 10, 15 |
| Limit one: imperial survival | Serbian success threatened the internal survival of the Habsburg Empire; an internal nationality problem is not a ratio calculation | Lesson 7 |
| Limit two: positive nationalism | The South Slav programme sought to create a state, not to preserve an equilibrium | Lesson 7 |
| Limit three: preventive timing | Moltke's reported conclusion that preventive war must come before the end of 1914; preventive war seeks to change a future balance | Lessons 5, 9, 17 |
| Limit four, if space allows: operational rigidity | The refusal to alter the deployment on 30-31 July; the declaration on France without a quarrel | Lessons 6, 15 |
| The chain, read back | Only the first and last links are cleanly balance-of-power | Lessons 15, 20 |
| Graded verdict | A necessary part of the explanation and not the essential one | Lesson 20 |
| Question-sensitive qualification | Confined to Britain's entry, the balance-of-power reading is substantially stronger | Lessons 15, 20 |
