## What to rehearse, given one owned question

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with a single owned question is prepared differently from one with a bank of past papers, and differently again from one with none.

1. **Rehearse the owned demand until it is automatic.** The concession-and-limit structure, at 250 words, in twelve minutes. It is the only verified target this topic has.
2. **Rehearse the six directive rows, not six answers.** ✅ The source's directive map exhausts what can realistically be asked. Practise producing the correct structure from a cold stem in under ninety seconds.
3. **Rehearse the controls, because they are what a first attempt gets wrong.** Ten items in total - seven from the source, three added by this session - of which the alliance-obligation control and the Schlieffen-blueprint control are the most frequently breached.
4. **Rehearse the escalation chain separately.** It is the paragraph that appears in almost every framing, and under time pressure it is the first thing to become vague.
5. **Rehearse the handovers.** Seven boundaries, one clause each. A script that trespasses into the war's course or the peace settlement loses time and gains nothing.
6. **Use the owners' probable framings as the practice bank.** ✅ The source lists three - the Balkan origin with structural escalation; Balkan nationalism converting a regional crisis into a world war; and accounting for the outbreak with reference to alliance politics and mobilisation. ✅ The source lists three more - the extent of German responsibility; the role of Balkan nationalism; and how far war had become likely but not inevitable. ⚠️ The seven original questions below are built to cover all six framings and the owned demand.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - The standing system, Lessons 1 to 6

### Cumulative check A1 - from a description of a system to an explanation of an event

**Question:** Assemble the 1914 international system from its components and then state precisely why a complete description of it still does not explain the war.

**Model answer:** The components are five. First, a distribution of power in which Europe still took most of the decisions shaping the world while the largest industrial economy, the United States, stood outside the system, Germany led the continent militarily and economically having overtaken Britain in pig-iron and steel though not quite in coal, Russia expanded rapidly from a backward base, and Japan had been a power to be reckoned with since defeating Russia in 1904-05. Second, a distribution of regime types in which the United States, Britain and France had parliaments with an important say, Germany, Italy and Japan had elected chambers with power held elsewhere, and Russia and Austria-Hungary were autocracies whose parliaments could only advise - which determines how few people could decide for war. Third, an alliance structure assembled between 1882 and 1907, with an extra-European member from 1902. Fourth, an arms competition running on two clocks: a naval race from the Navy Law of 1897 and the Dreadnought of 1906, which Britain had effectively won by 1914, and a land and railway competition after 1908 in which Russia was subsidised by French loans while German army expansion was blocked by the Reichstag's refusal of taxes. Fifth, a set of operational plans in which German deployment required an attack through Belgium and, on the recorded account, could not be altered at short notice. A complete description of all five still does not explain the war, for the reason the record itself supplies: these conditions were in place from 1907 at the latest, and under them the powers passed through Morocco in 1905-06, Bosnia in 1908, Agadir in 1911 and two Balkan wars in 1912-13 without a general war. A system explains a propensity; it cannot explain an event that the same system declined to produce five times.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a longer description of the system is a better explanation of the war. Beyond a certain point additional structural detail reduces explanatory power, because every extra structural cause makes it harder to explain the five containments. The explanatory work is done by identifying what was different in July 1914, and that is a question about decisions.

### Cumulative check A2 - the two clocks

**Question:** Explain why an answer on militarism that concentrates on the Anglo-German naval race is chronologically misdirected, and identify what the correctly directed answer says.

**Model answer:** It is misdirected because the naval race had effectively ended before the crisis began. The programme ran from Tirpitz's Navy Law of 1897 and was transformed by the British Dreadnought of 1906, which made all other battleships obsolete and allowed Germany to build in the new class on closer terms; but at the end of 1911 Britain held eight Dreadnought-type battleships to Germany's four, Germany reduced naval spending early in 1913 in order to concentrate on the army, and Churchill's assessment for the spring and summer of 1914 was that naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction because Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships. The correctly directed answer moves to the land and railway competition, which was accelerating precisely when the naval race was decelerating. After the Bosnian humiliation of 1908 Russia undertook a massive military build-up, modernised the army and improved its railway system specifically to mobilise faster, financed by huge loans from the French government, while German army expansion was hampered because the Reichstag refused to sanction the necessary tax increases. The consequence was a conclusion with a date: von Jagow reported that Moltke saw no alternative to a preventive war to defeat Germany's enemies before they became too powerful, that such a war was necessary, and that it must take place before the end of 1914 because Russia would afterwards be too strong. Militarism in 1914 therefore supplied a deadline rather than an appetite, and the naval race explains not the timing of the war but the fact that Britain had come to regard Germany as its principal rival.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the 1913-14 qualification as a reason to drop the naval race from the answer. The distinction to hold is between the stock of mistrust, which was high, and the flow of new provocation, which had stopped. Both findings are recorded, and stating them together shows command of a tension inside the evidence rather than a choice between halves of it.

## Block B - The quarrel and the rehearsals, Lessons 7 to 12

### Cumulative check B1 - why the Balkans, and why not before

**Question:** Explain why the crisis that produced a general war arose in south-eastern Europe, and why the same region had produced four contained crises in the preceding six years.

**Model answer:** It arose there because three things intersected in one small space. The Ottoman retreat had left open title to territory: the Balkan League states had all once been part of the Ottoman Empire, Turkey was regarded as the Sick Man of Europe, and in 1912 the League captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe. The claims that filled the space were mutually exclusive and, in one case, non-negotiable: after the dynastic change of 1903, when King Alexander was murdered by army officers who installed Peter Karageorgevic and Serbian policy switched to a pro-Russian line, Serbia openly sought to unite all Serbs and Croats into a large South Slav kingdom, and many of them lived inside a Habsburg Empire that also contained Germans, Hungarians, Magyars, Czechs, Slovaks, Italians, Poles, Romanians, Ruthenians and Slovenes, so that conceding the principle once would have invited dissolution. And a great power had a material stake at the region's exit, since the Dardanelles were the outlet from the Black Sea and Russia's main trade route, which German influence over Bulgaria and Turkey might have allowed Germany and Austria to close. The same region nonetheless produced three of the five containments because in each case a great power chose restraint or retreat. In 1908 Germany made clear it would support Austria, France drew back unwilling to enter a Balkan war, Britain protested only, and Russia, still smarting from its defeat by Japan, dared not risk war without allies. In 1912 and 1913 Grey and the German government jointly convened a conference in London, Austria-Hungary was given an independent Albania instead of a Serbian outlet to the sea, and in the second war Germany restrained an Austria-Hungary that was itching to attack Serbia. The region supplied the quarrel in every year; what varied was whether a great power converted it into a war.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the location by the character of the region - a powder keg, a zone of ancient hatreds. Those are descriptions masquerading as causes. The analytical content is a configuration: open title created by an imperial retreat, overlapping and partly non-negotiable claims, and a great-power economic lifeline running through the exit.

### Cumulative check B2 - the cost of winning

**Question:** Show how two crises that ended without a general war - Bosnia in 1908 and the Second Balkan War in 1913 - made the general war of 1914 possible, and state what general proposition about crisis management follows.

**Model answer:** Bosnia in 1908 was a complete diplomatic victory whose costs were delayed. Austria-Hungary pre-empted a Young Turk attempt to reassert Turkish control by annexing a province it had administered since 1878, striking directly at a Serbia that had hoped to take a territory containing about three million Serbs in a mixed population; Serbia appealed to Russia, Russia called for a conference, Germany made clear it would back Austria, France drew back, Britain merely protested, and Russia retreated. Two consequences followed. Serbia remained bitterly hostile, and that quarrel is the one which sparked off the outbreak of war. And Russia resolved never to be humiliated again, rebuilding the army and improving the railways specifically for faster mobilisation, which simultaneously produced the German general staff's deadline, undermined the assumption of slow Russian mobilisation on which German planning rested, and made a second Russian retreat politically unavailable. The Second Balkan War of 1913 was contained by German restraint of an Austria-Hungary itching to attack Serbia, and its three recorded consequences were a strengthened Serbia determined to agitate among Serbs and Croats inside the empire, an Austria-Hungary equally determined to end Serbian ambitions, and a German belief, drawn from Grey's cooperation over the London conference, that Britain might be detached from France and Russia. Each of those six outputs appears in the July decisions. The general proposition is that containment is not free: a crisis resolved without war still redistributes grievance, resolve and expectation, and a system that keeps producing settlements while lowering its own margin is solvent rather than healthy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the contained crises as evidence that the system was working well. They show the system working, which is the argument against inevitability, and they simultaneously show the margin falling, which is the argument against complacency. Using only one half produces either a determinist or a complacent answer, and both are refutable from the same record.

## Block C - The crisis, Lessons 13 to 15

### Cumulative check C1 - the chain of choices

**Question:** Reconstruct the July Crisis as a sequence of decisions with the decision-maker and the alternative named at each step, and identify which single step, if reversed, most clearly changes the outcome.

**Model answer:** The chain runs as follows. Berlin, in early July, gave Vienna a telegram urging an attack on Serbia and promising help without any conditions attached; the available alternative was the response Germany had actually given twelve months earlier, when it restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War, or an assurance with conditions attached. Vienna then blamed the Serbian government and delivered a harsh ultimatum with a forty-eight-hour limit; the alternative was a demand for a joint investigation without an ultimatum. Serbia accepted most but not all of the demands on 25 July and Vienna broke off relations the same day; the alternative was to treat substantial acceptance as sufficient. Vienna declared war on 28 July, expecting a localised conflict like the Balkan Wars. St Petersburg then mobilised, anxious not to let the Serbs down again and bound by a promise given in February 1914; the alternative was to confine mobilisation to the Austrian frontier, and the records differ on whether it did so on 29 July before a general order on 30 July or ordered general mobilisation on 29 July. On 30 July the Kaiser and Bethmann-Hollweg urged Vienna to negotiate and Wilhelm proposed a partial German mobilisation against Russia alone, and Moltke refused on the ground that there was no time to change the railway timetables. Berlin demanded cancellation on 31 July, declared war on Russia on 1 August and on France on 3 August although it had no specific quarrel with France, and entered Belgium; London, which had promised in 1839 to defend Belgian neutrality, demanded withdrawal and entered on 4 August; Vienna declared war on Russia on 6 August. The step whose reversal most clearly changes the outcome is the first, because it is the only one with a controlled comparison attached: the same alliance, partner and quarrel produced no war in 1913 when Germany refused.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the sequence as a cascade in which each step follows automatically from the last. Every step was an order given by a government that had an alternative, and the only step refused on technical rather than political grounds was Moltke's rejection of partial mobilisation on 30-31 July. Even there, the constraint was the product of earlier political choices about how the army would be built and deployed.

### Cumulative check C2 - what each belligerent was actually fighting about

**Question:** State what each of the six original belligerents entered the war for, and show why the set of answers is itself an argument about causation.

**Model answer:** Austria-Hungary fought for imperial survival: it believed that South Slav success would cause the collapse of a state containing a dozen nationalities, and it used the assassination as the excuse for a preventive war against Serbia that it expected to localise. Serbia fought to defend itself after an ultimatum most of which it had accepted, within a national programme aimed at uniting Serbs and Croats in a South Slav kingdom. Russia fought for prestige as leader of the Slavs, for its Balkan position after the humiliation of 1908, for a trade route through the Dardanelles that Germany and Austria might otherwise close, and in fulfilment of a promise given in February 1914. Germany fought because it had guaranteed Vienna unconditionally, because its general staff believed a preventive war had to come before the end of 1914, and because its deployment required attacking France first - which is why it declared war on a state with which it had no specific quarrel. France fought because it was attacked, with a standing grievance over Alsace-Lorraine dating from 1871 in the background. Britain fought on the violation of Belgian neutrality that it had promised in 1839 to defend, reinforced by the strategic threat of a hostile great power on the Channel coast. The set is an argument because only two of the six motives - the German encirclement complaint and the British Channel interest - are balance-of-power motives in the strict sense of preserving a distribution. Imperial survival, national unification, prestige, preventive advantage and a treaty obligation are not. That distribution of motives is precisely what a "how far ... essentially for the preservation of balance of power" question is asking the candidate to notice.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that states in the same coalition fought for the same reason. Austria-Hungary sought to preserve a state form and Germany to alter a future balance of strength; Serbia sought to create a state and Russia to defend a position and a trade route. Coalitions coordinate means, not ends, and treating them as unitary is exactly what makes a balance-of-power explanation look more complete than it is.

## Block D - Judgement, Lessons 16 to 20

### Cumulative check D1 - responsibility on two levels

**Question:** Answer the question "who was responsible for the outbreak of the First World War?" on both required levels, and explain why an answer that uses only one level cannot be complete.

**Model answer:** At the level of decision, immediate responsibility lies with Vienna's choice to use the assassination as the occasion for a war against Serbia, after an ultimatum with a forty-eight-hour limit most of whose demands were accepted, and with Berlin's unconditional backing, which reversed the restraint Germany had applied to the same partner in the same quarrel in 1913 and which the source describes as a blank cheque. Russia's mobilisation and Moltke's refusal to alter the deployment belong to the same level, as does Britain's decision of 4 August. At the level of system, responsibility lies with a militarised alliance order in which every state believed that restraint would be read as weakness, in which a retreating empire had left open title in a region where a national principle operated inside a dynastic state, in which a general staff had built a single indivisible instrument, and in which each contained crisis lowered the margin for the next. An answer using only the decision level cannot explain why competent men in five capitals all chose risk simultaneously, nor why the same men had chosen restraint repeatedly before. An answer using only the system level cannot explain why the system produced containment at Algeciras in 1906, over Bosnia in 1908, at Agadir in 1911 and in the Second Balkan War of 1913 and war in 1914. The recorded scholarly distribution matches this two-level shape: probably a majority accept Fischer's thesis of deliberate German provocation, Hamilton and Herwig conclude that ultimate responsibility probably rests with Germany while characterising it as a war for survival rather than for world domination, Rohl places the main responsibility on Wilhelm II, Williamson insists that Vienna decided for itself and that German promises were not important, Mulligan argues that Russia bears at least equal responsibility, and the source's own conclusion is that Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting a two-level answer into an evasion by saying that everyone was responsible. The recorded conclusion names three states and therefore excludes Britain, France, Serbia and Italy from the primary tier; and within the three, the German assurance carries more weight than the others because it is the one decision with a controlled comparison showing that the alternative was available and had been taken twelve months earlier.

### Cumulative check D2 - the owned question, assembled

**Question:** Produce the complete plan for the owned 2024 demand on the balance of power, showing the concession, the limits, the mechanism and the verdict, and state the one sentence you would add if the question carried 20 marks rather than 15.

**Model answer:** Concede first, with dates: the pre-war order was organised as two counterweighted blocs formed in 1882, 1894, 1904 and 1907; the German complaint of encirclement by Britain, France and Russia is a balance-of-power fear by definition; the Anglo-German naval competition was a contest over a ratio rather than over territory, standing at eight Dreadnought-type ships to four at the end of 1911; Balkan outcomes were scored by both sides as transfers of power, which is why Bosnia in 1908 and the Balkan Wars of 1912-13 mattered so much; and Britain's entry on 4 August answered the classic balance-of-power threat of a hostile great power on the Channel coast, reinforced by the 1839 guarantee. Concede further that the system had worked as a balance system, at Algeciras in January 1906 and in the Agadir settlement of 1911 - which establishes that preserving the balance had not previously required war. Then state the limits with their reasons: Austria-Hungary fought for the internal survival of a multi-national empire, which is a nationality problem and not a ratio calculation; Serbia and the South Slav movement sought to create a state rather than preserve an equilibrium; German reasoning was preventive, and preventive war seeks to change a future balance, which is the opposite of preserving one; and the operational plan that carried the war to France and Belgium was a timetable, not a strategic purpose. Add the mechanism by reading the escalation chain back against the stem: Sarajevo, the Austrian ultimatum and declaration, Russian mobilisation, German backing and counter-mobilisation, the attack on France through Belgium, and British entry - of which only the first link, the bloc structure, and the last, Britain's entry, are cleanly balance-of-power, while the middle links are imperial, national and operational. Verdict: balance of power explains the structure that made a general war possible and explains Britain's entry, but not the motives or the timing of the belligerents' choices in July 1914; it is a necessary part of the explanation and not the essential one. At 20 marks the sentence to add is that once fighting began the belligerents' aims - territory, colonies, regime change and national self-determination - travelled far beyond any restoration of the 1914 equilibrium, so that the proposition becomes weaker still when applied to the war as fought rather than to the crisis as entered.

**Misconception to avoid:** Spending the concession paragraph proving that balance of power was a cause. The stem concedes that already by asking how far it was the essential one. The concession's function is to establish command of the structural evidence quickly so that the limits, which are where the marks are, can be given the space they need.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These seven questions are original to this session and are not verified questions; the single verified question owned by this topic is treated above as a demand, a decoding and an answer route, without a model answer, because the learner maintains a separate solved workbook for verified items. Attempt each of these under timed conditions before reading the model. Each model sits at or below its stated word ceiling - 150 words at 10 marks and 250 words at 15 and 20 marks, the established convention in this repository - and each carries a scoring note identifying the single element that most often separates a good script from an average one on that demand. The seven together cover all six probable framings recorded by the two canonical owners.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Examine the role of Balkan nationalism in the outbreak of the First World War. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

Balkan nationalism supplied the one quarrel in Europe that could not be settled by compensation. After the dynastic change of 1903, Serbia openly sought to unite Serbs and Croats in a South Slav kingdom. Many lived inside a Habsburg Empire containing a dozen nationalities, so conceding the principle once invited general dissolution; some Austrians therefore favoured a preventive war to destroy Serbia before she grew strong enough to provoke the break-up.

Its role was nevertheless regional, not general. The same nationalism existed in 1908, 1912 and 1913 without producing a European war, because Russia retreated, Germany restrained Vienna, and the powers conferenced in London. It became generalising only when great-power sponsorship hardened: Russia had resolved after 1908 never to retreat again and promised Serbia help in February 1914.

Nationalism therefore explains why Vienna would not compromise and where the crisis arose; the decisions of July 1914 explain why it spread.

*Scoring note: the separator is the second paragraph. Scripts that establish only how dangerous Serbian nationalism was have explained an Austro-Serb war; the mark comes from showing that the same factor was present in three earlier crises that stayed local.*

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war in 1914? (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

Through four transmissions, then one pre-existing condition.

Alignment carried it outward: Berlin assured Vienna unconditionally in early July, and St Petersburg was bound to Belgrade by a promise of February 1914. Prestige removed the exit: Russia had resolved after the 1908 humiliation never to retreat again, and Vienna believed Serbian success threatened Habsburg survival. Operational planning widened it: German deployment required attacking France first through Belgium, so Germany declared war on 3 August despite having no quarrel with France. Legal obligation completed it: Britain, bound since 1839 to defend Belgian neutrality, demanded withdrawal and entered on 4 August.

Extension beyond Europe required no further decision. Britain and Japan had been allied since 1902, the belligerents were empires holding territory across continents, and Britain owned about half the world's merchant tonnage while Russia's trade ran through the Dardanelles.

The war was global in reach before it was global in theatre.

*Scoring note: the separator is the refusal to answer this question with the causes of Austro-Serb hostility. The source's directive map records that mistake explicitly; the demand is for a mechanism, and naming four named transmissions with dates is what supplies it.*

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> "The alliance system made the First World War inevitable." Critically examine. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The proposition contains a strong argument and a false inference.

The argument is Kennan's: once France and Russia allied in 1894, Europe divided into camps in which every retreat became a public humiliation, so that Russia, Austria-Hungary and Germany could not withdraw without loss of face. The structure grew accordingly - the Triple Alliance of 1882, the Franco-Russian alliance of 1894, the Entente Cordiale of 1904, the Anglo-Russian agreement of 1907 - and friction between the armed camps brought Europe to the verge of war several times after 1900. The alliances also shaped the war's form: the belligerent lineup of August 1914 follows the 1907 map almost exactly.

The inference from likelihood to inevitability fails on recorded evidence. Nothing was binding about these alliances. France sent no help when Russia fought Japan in 1904-05 and gave none when Russia protested the annexation of Bosnia; Austria-Hungary took no interest in Germany's Moroccan defeats; Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia in the Second Balkan War; and Italy, a Triple Alliance member, entered the war against Germany in 1915. No power declared war in 1914 because of one of these treaties.

What the alliances actually did was fourfold: they signalled who mattered, emboldened clients, restrained partners when their patrons chose, and raised the political cost of retreat.

The verdict is graded. The alliances explain the shape of the war far better than its occurrence; the same structure produced containment five times, and what changed in 1914 was the decision taken inside it.

*Scoring note: the separator is the Italian clause. A member of one camp fighting in the other is a single-fact refutation of the automatic-trigger model, and a script that deploys it has closed the question with evidence rather than with assertion.*

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> To what extent was Germany responsible for the outbreak of the First World War? (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Germany's responsibility is primary but not exclusive, and the evidence for each half is specific.

Primary, first, because of a reversal. Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War of 1913; in July 1914 the Kaiser urged Vienna to attack Serbia and promised help without conditions - a blank cheque. The alliance, partner and quarrel were identical; only the German decision changed, which removes the defence of treaty compulsion.

Primary, second, because of timing. Constrained by a Reichstag that refused the taxes army expansion needed, and watching a French-financed Russian rearmament, the general staff concluded, as von Jagow reported of Moltke, that preventive war must come before the end of 1914.

Primary, third, because of execution. When Wilhelm proposed partial mobilisation against Russia alone on 30 July, Moltke refused for want of time to change railway timetables; Germany then declared war on France on 3 August despite having no specific quarrel with France, and entered Belgium.

Not exclusive, because Vienna chose its own course: Williamson holds that the Austrian leaders decided for themselves and that German promises were unimportant. Not exclusive, because Mulligan argues Russia bears at least equal responsibility, its post-1905 Balkan turn, the February 1914 promise to Serbia and the naval agreement with Britain having reshaped German calculation.

Fischer's stronger claim of deliberate provocation is widely accepted but Koch contested it, and Hamilton and Herwig call it a war for survival, not domination.

Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary share responsibility - unequally, with Berlin first.

*Scoring note: the separator is the word "unequally". Scripts either name one state and stop or distribute blame evenly; the mark comes from a graded distribution that states an order and gives a reason for the ordering.*

## Question 5 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Account for the outbreak of the First World War with special reference to alliance politics and mobilisation. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The outbreak requires five layers, of which the question names two.

The structural layer is the alliance order built between 1882 and 1907, together with nationalism, imperial insecurity and arms competition. It made every regional dispute a test of great-power standing but cannot explain timing, since the same structures produced containment five times between 1905 and 1913.

The regional layer is the Austro-Serb quarrel. After 1903 Serbia sought a South Slav kingdom drawing on populations inside a multi-national empire, and the annexation of Bosnia in 1908 left permanent hostility.

The proximate layer is the assassination of 28 June 1914, the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration on Serbia of 28 July and of nothing beyond it.

The great-power layer is alliance politics in action: an unconditional German assurance, and Russian mobilisation for Serbia after a February 1914 promise and a resolve never to repeat 1908.

The operational layer is mobilisation. German planning assumed France would join Russia, required passage through Belgium and the rapid capture of Liege, and promised victory in six weeks. Taylor's point is that railway timetables reduced negotiating time almost to nil; Moltke's refusal of partial mobilisation on 30 July shows the constraint operating. Zuber's challenge to the plan's documentary status does not disturb the finding that German planning required an attack through Belgium.

Alliances therefore explain who fought; mobilisation explains why the local war became general, why France was attacked without a quarrel, and why Britain had a trigger.

*Scoring note: the separator is keeping the two named factors in different jobs. Alliance politics belongs to the escalation of commitment and mobilisation to the escalation of geography; a script that blends them produces a single vague paragraph about "militarism and alliances" and loses both.*

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> "The First World War began in the Balkans, but the structure of European politics made escalation increasingly likely." Examine. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Both halves are correct, and the interest lies in how they connect.

It began in the Balkans because an Ottoman retreat had left open title where claims were mutually exclusive. Serbia's South Slav programme after 1903 targeted populations inside a Habsburg Empire of a dozen nationalities, so that conceding once invited dissolution; the annexation of Bosnia in 1908 left permanent hostility; and the Balkan Wars of 1912-13 left Serbia stronger and Vienna determined to end her ambitions.

Escalation became increasingly likely because each containment was purchased with a structural cost. Algeciras in 1906 hardened the Entente and produced Anglo-French military conversations. Bosnia in 1908 humiliated Russia, which then rebuilt its army and railways for faster mobilisation - undermining the assumption of slow Russian mobilisation on which German planning rested and producing the staff conclusion that preventive war must come before the end of 1914. Agadir in 1911 turned German opinion intensely anti-British. The London conference of 1912-13 left Berlin believing Britain might be detached from France and Russia.

The connection is that the region supplied a non-negotiable quarrel while the system supplied sponsors, deadlines and an indivisible military instrument.

Two qualifications complete it. Likelihood is not inevitability: five crises under these structures ended without general war. And once fighting began, the belligerents' aims - territory, colonies, regime change, self-determination - exceeded anything the pre-war structure had contained, while the extension beyond Europe followed automatically from the 1902 Anglo-Japanese alliance and from empire.

Structure loaded the dice; July 1914 threw them.

*Scoring note: the separator at 20 marks is the penultimate paragraph. Adding war aims, the global dimension and the inevitability qualification is what distinguishes a 20-mark answer from a longer 15-mark one; adding more causes does not.*

## Question 7 - 20 marks, 250 words

> How far had war become likely, but not inevitable, by 1914? Discuss in the light of competing interpretations. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Highly likely; not inevitable. The distinction is empirical, not philosophical.

Likely, because the margin was falling. The alliance order was complete by 1907 and every subsequent crisis left the next harder to manage: military conversations after 1906, a humiliated and rearming Russia after 1908, an intensely anti-British German opinion after 1911, a strengthened Serbia and a twice-frustrated Vienna after 1913, and a general staff reasoning by 1914 that preventive war must come before the year ended.

Not inevitable, because the same structures produced five containments - Morocco 1905-06, Bosnia 1908, Agadir 1911, and the two Balkan Wars, the second contained by Germany restraining its own ally. A theory predicting war from structure alone predicts six wars and explains one.

The interpretations divide accordingly. Kennan held that the Franco-Russian alliance of 1894 sealed Europe's fate. Fischer argued deliberate German provocation, evidenced by the December 1912 meeting; Koch replied that nothing came of it. Ritter and Taylor made the plans decisive; Zuber argued that the single rigid plan was largely a post-war construction. Turner described a tragedy of miscalculation - Vienna expecting Russian abstention, Berlin promising unconditionally, both capitals assuming mobilisation need not mean war, the generals trusting a quick victory. Afflerbach and Stevenson's contributors reached no consensus but leaned towards war being possible rather than inevitable.

The synthesis is that structure raised probability while decisions determined outcome. Contingency does not diminish responsibility; it establishes it, since an avoidable war is one that identifiable people chose.

*Scoring note: the separator is the final sentence. Most scripts treat contingency as a way of softening blame; the strongest version reverses that, since a war that could have been avoided is a war for which decisions, not forces, are answerable.*

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it costs marks | The correction | Taught in |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Writing that the assassination caused the First World War | Conflates the trigger with the cause and abandons two of the three questions | ✅ It was the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia | Lessons 1, 13 |
| 2 | Writing that the alliances made war inevitable | ✅ Contradicted by the record of five containments and by the absence of any binding obligation | The alliances raised the cost of retreat; they compelled nothing | Lessons 4, 18 |
| 3 | Saying "Serbia assassinated the Archduke" | Converts an Austrian allegation into a finding and collapses five attribution levels | ✅ Gavrilo Princip shot the Archduke; ✅ the Austrians blamed the Serb government | Lesson 13 |
| 4 | Writing about "the Serbs" or "the Germans" as collective actors | An error of fact: peoples do not form intentions | Name governments, cabinets, general staffs and individuals | Lessons 7, 13, 17 |
| 5 | Forgetting Italy's entry against Germany in 1915 | Loses the single-fact refutation of the automatic-alliance model | ✅ Italy was in the Triple Alliance and entered on the Allied side in 1915 | Lesson 4 |
| 6 | Treating the Triple Entente as a military alliance | Makes Britain's entry look automatic and removes the need to explain it | ✅ The 1907 instrument was not a military alliance | Lessons 4, 15 |
| 7 | Saying Britain entered because of France | ✅ Explicitly prohibited by the source's controls | ✅ Belgian neutrality, guaranteed in 1839, was the immediate legal-political issue | Lesson 15 |
| 8 | Presenting the Schlieffen Plan as an uncontested fixed blueprint | ✅ The dispute is recorded and the source prohibits the uncontested version | Say German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium | Lesson 6 |
| 9 | Writing that mobilisation meant war | Removes the misperception that Lesson 18 identifies as central | ✅ Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war; troops could be halted at the frontiers | Lessons 6, 15, 18 |
| 10 | Treating the naval race as a live cause in 1914 | ✅ Contradicted by the 1913 spending reduction and Churchill's assessment | It fixed British perceptions earlier; the live competition in 1914 was on land | Lesson 5 |
| 11 | Treating colonial rivalry as a direct cause | ✅ Contradicted by the settlement record and by good Anglo-German colonial relations in early July 1914 | Concede the habit and the mood; deny the causal inference | Lesson 3 |
| 12 | Omitting the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 | Loses the origin of Serbian hostility and of Russian rearmament | ✅ Both recorded consequences belong in any causation answer | Lesson 11 |
| 13 | Omitting the Balkan Wars of 1912-13 | Loses the last rehearsal and the German misreading of Britain | ✅ Three recorded consequences | Lesson 12 |
| 14 | Omitting German restraint of Austria-Hungary in 1913 | Loses the controlled comparison that closes the alliance-obligation defence | ✅ Germany restrained Vienna in 1913 and encouraged it in 1914 | Lessons 12, 14 |
| 15 | Saying Serbia rejected the ultimatum | Inverts the recorded position and destroys the argument about the "excuse" | ✅ Serbia accepted most of the demands | Lesson 14 |
| 16 | Saying Serbia accepted the ultimatum in full | Overstates and invites correction | ✅ Most, not all | Lesson 14 |
| 17 | Treating the blank cheque as an alliance obligation | Removes the German decision from the answer | ✅ A telegram urging attack and promising help without conditions | Lesson 14 |
| 18 | Asserting a single certain date for the Russian general mobilisation | The evidence base itself differs between 29 and 30 July | Write 29-30 July and name the difference if space allows | Lesson 15 |
| 19 | Asserting a single certain date for the German entry into Belgium | The source's narrative and its own prologue differ | Write 3-4 August, with the violation complete by 4 August | Lesson 15 |
| 20 | Dating Britain's entry to 3 August | Factual error | ✅ 4 August 1914 | Lesson 15 |
| 21 | Dating the Austro-Hungarian declaration on Russia to 1 August | Factual error | ✅ 6 August 1914 | Lesson 15 |
| 22 | Dating Algeciras to 1905 | Factual error | ✅ The conference was held in January 1906 | Lesson 10 |
| 23 | Confusing the two Moroccan crises | Loses both the Entente-testing argument and the settlement evidence | ✅ 1905-06 produced Algeciras; 1911 produced the Panther and the Congo exchange | Lesson 10 |
| 24 | Attributing the Mansion House warning to the Foreign Secretary | Factual error | ✅ Lloyd George, Chancellor of the Exchequer | Lesson 10 |
| 25 | Confusing the 1878 arrangement with the 1908 annexation | Loses the ambiguity that made 1908 possible | ✅ 1878: officially Turkish, administered by Austria-Hungary. 1908: formal annexation | Lessons 7, 11 |
| 26 | Omitting the Young Turks | Loses the reason for the timing of the annexation | ✅ Their determination to assert Turkish control gave Vienna the occasion | Lessons 8, 11 |
| 27 | Treating Bosnia as ethnically Serbian | Implies a national settlement was available | ✅ About three million Serbs in a mixed population of Serbs, Croats and Muslims | Lesson 11 |
| 28 | Reducing Russian Balkan interest to Slav sentiment | Loses the material stake that makes the interest intelligible | ✅ The Dardanelles were the outlet from the Black Sea and the main Russian trade route | Lesson 8 |
| 29 | Omitting the February 1914 Russian promise to Serbia | Makes Russian mobilisation look improvised and removes Mulligan's basis | ✅ The Tsar promised help against an Austro-Hungarian attack | Lessons 8, 17 |
| 30 | Presenting Fischer's thesis as an Allied position | Reverses the historiography | ✅ Fischer was a German historian writing in 1967 against his own national consensus | Lesson 17 |
| 31 | Reporting Fischer's claim as merely "Germany was responsible" | Understates a specific and contested claim | ✅ Deliberate planning and provocation for world-power objectives and domestic management | Lesson 17 |
| 32 | Using the December 1912 meeting without Koch's objection | Presents contested evidence as settled | ✅ Koch: nothing came of the war council | Lessons 9, 17 |
| 33 | Treating Hamilton and Herwig as endorsing Fischer in full | Misstates their conclusion | ✅ A war for survival rather than for world domination | Lesson 17 |
| 34 | Omitting Williamson when arguing that Berlin drove Vienna | Leaves the strongest counter-position unanswered | ✅ Vienna decided for itself; German promises were not important | Lessons 14, 17 |
| 35 | Omitting Mulligan when redistributing responsibility | Loses the strongest case for Russian responsibility | ✅ Russia bears at least equal responsibility on his account | Lessons 8, 17 |
| 36 | Quoting a historian verbatim | ✅ Prohibited by the source's controls | Paraphrase the claim | Lesson 17 |
| 37 | Writing that all the powers were equally responsible | Matches no recorded position and dissolves the question | ✅ Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share it - which excludes three other belligerents | Lesson 17 |
| 38 | Using Bethmann's remark as evidence of innocence | Misuses evidence of bewilderment | It supports contingency, not exculpation | Lessons 17, 18 |
| 39 | Concluding from contingency that nobody was responsible | Inverts the logic | An avoidable war is one that identifiable people chose | Lesson 18 |
| 40 | Running counterfactuals more than one step | Produces speculation the evidence cannot support | One step only; no claims about the post-war world | Lesson 18 |
| 41 | Using the British-clarity counterfactual without its recorded reply | Presents a refuted position as open | ✅ A British announcement on 31 July would have made no difference | Lessons 15, 18 |
| 42 | Answering an escalation question with the causes of Austro-Serb hostility | ✅ Named in the source as the wrong response | Give the mechanism: alignment, prestige, planning, legal obligation | Lessons 16, 19 |
| 43 | Giving a balanced answer with no verdict on a "how far" question | ✅ Named in the source as the failure mode | Grade: necessary but not essential | Lessons 16, 20 |
| 44 | Supplying casualty, army-size or spending figures | None is sourced here | ✅ The sourced quantitative evidence is limited to the eight-to-four capital-ship count, about three million Serbs in Bosnia, roughly half the world's merchant tonnage held by Britain, the German Socialist Party's share of over a third of Reichstag seats, and the 12 000-foot shell trajectory at Liege | Lessons 5, 9, 11, 15, 19 |
| 45 | Using empire to explain why the war happened | Confuses cause with extent | Empire explains the war's extent; the colonial disputes were settled | Lessons 3, 19 |
| 46 | Carrying the answer into the Marne, Versailles or 1917 | Trespasses on other owners and loses time | ✅ this topic ends when general war begins | Lessons 1, 15, 19 |
| 47 | Opening a 1914 answer at 1871 or at the Congress of Vienna | Delays the verdict and answers a different question | Open with the trigger-cause distinction | Lessons 1, 20 |
| 48 | Calling Franz Ferdinand the Emperor | Factual error | ✅ Nephew and heir to Emperor Franz Josef | Lesson 13 |
| 49 | Placing Sarajevo in Serbia | Factual error that also loses the 1908 connection | ✅ Sarajevo was the capital of Bosnia | Lesson 13 |
| 50 | Asserting Franz Ferdinand's own political views | Not recorded in this evidence base | Say nothing about them | Lesson 13 |

## Drill set B - one-line corrections

⚠️ Each line states an error and its correction in the form a script should carry.

1. Not "alliances obliged them to fight" but "no power declared war because of one of these treaties".
2. Not "the Entente was an alliance" but "the 1907 agreement was not a military alliance".
3. Not "Britain fought for France" but "Britain fought on the 1839 guarantee of Belgian neutrality".
4. Not "Germany had a quarrel with France" but "Germany had no specific quarrel with France and attacked because the plan required it".
5. Not "the Schlieffen Plan was a fixed blueprint" but "German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium".
6. Not "mobilisation meant war" but "mobilisation did not necessarily mean war, but German planning could not treat it as a pause".
7. Not "Serbia rejected the ultimatum" but "Serbia accepted most of the demands and was attacked anyway".
8. Not "Germany was obliged to back Vienna" but "Germany restrained Vienna in 1913 and encouraged it in 1914".
9. Not "the war was inevitable" but "war had become highly likely but not inevitable".
10. Not "everyone was to blame" but "Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary share responsibility, unequally".
11. Not "the Balkans were a side-show" but "the Balkan question was the immediate ignition zone".
12. Not "colonial rivalry caused the war" but "colonial disputes had always been settled without war, and Anglo-German colonial relations were good in early July 1914".

## Drill set C - the three questions, sorted by stem

⚠️ Given a stem, name which of the three questions it asks. This is the ninety-second decision of Lesson 20 in drill form.

| Stem fragment | ✅ Question |
|---|---|
| "Account for the outbreak" | All three, weighted to one and two |
| "Why did a Balkan crisis become a world war" | Two, then three |
| "Who was responsible" | One and two |
| "Was it inevitable" | Two, with one as evidence |
| "Role of Balkan nationalism" | One, with a bridge to two |
| "Preservation of balance of power" | One and two, with three as a closing qualification |
| "Sarajevo was the occasion, not the cause" | One, then two |
| "Effects on the global order" | Three, handing over to the next owner |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 - The complete causal architecture, end to end

```text
   BACKGROUND CONDITIONS (permanent features; explain possibility)
   --------------------------------------------------------------
   European primacy in world decision-making | a multi-national
   Habsburg state held by dynastic allegiance | Ottoman retreat
   from south-eastern Europe leaving open title | a maritime,
   integrated world economy | empires with extra-European territory
                                |
   STRUCTURAL CAUSES (raise probability; shape form; cannot fix timing)
   -------------------------------------------------------------------
   ALLIANCES 1882 -> 1894 -> 1902 -> 1904 -> 1907
   ARMS      naval 1897/1906, decided by 1911-13
             land and railways from 1908, accelerating to 1914
   PLANS     German deployment through Belgium; six-week promise;
             assumption of slow Russian mobilisation
   NATION    Serbian South Slav programme from 1903; Habsburg
             nationalities; French Alsace-Lorraine grievance
   DOMESTIC  German Reichstag clash and army finance; Russian
             post-1905 weakness and post-1911 jingoism
                                |
   REGIONAL CAUSE (supplies the specific non-negotiable quarrel)
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   1878 ambiguity -> 1903 Serbian switch -> 1908 annexation ->
   permanent Serbian hostility + Russian resolve -> 1912-13 wars ->
   Serbia enlarged, Vienna determined, Berlin misreading Britain
                                |
   PROXIMATE CAUSE (supplies the occasion, not the policy)
   -------------------------------------------------------
   28 June 1914: the heir and his wife killed at Sarajevo
   -> the immediate cause of ONE declaration of war, on 28 July
                                |
   DECISIONS (convert occasion into war; each had an alternative)
   -------------------------------------------------------------
   Berlin: unconditional assurance, early July (cf. refusal, 1913)
   Vienna: 48-hour ultimatum, 23 July; rupture on partial
           acceptance, 25 July; declaration, 28 July
   St Petersburg: mobilisation, 29-30 July
   Berlin: refusal of the partial option, 30-31 July; demand,
           31 July; declarations, 1 and 3 August
   London: demand for withdrawal, then entry, 4 August
                                |
   MECHANISMS (transmit and widen)
   -------------------------------
   ALIGNMENT -> PRESTIGE -> OPERATIONAL PLANNING -> LEGAL OBLIGATION
                                |
   OUTCOME
   -------
   a general European war by 6 August; global in the reach of its
   participants from the first week; global in theatre progressively
   => HANDOVER to the First World War and aftermath owner
```

## Map 2 - The five containments and the sixth case

⚠️ **Count discipline:** the canonical series is **five** great-power crises contained between 1905 and 1913 - Morocco 1905-06, Bosnia 1908, Agadir 1911, the First Balkan War 1912 and the Second Balkan War 1913 - with the July Crisis of 1914 as the sixth case and the only one that became a general war. The Anglo-Russian agreement of 1907 is listed below for alliance context because it changed the German reading of the system, but it was a settlement of colonial disputes and not a contained crisis; it is **outside the count**.

| Entry | ✅ Object | ✅ How it ended | ⚠️ Structural cost it left |
|---|---|---|---|
| Containment 1 - Moroccan Crisis 1905-06 | German test of the Entente | Algeciras conference, January 1906; Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain support France; German diplomatic defeat | Entente demonstrated; Anglo-French military conversations begin |
| *Alliance context, outside the count* - Anglo-Russian agreement 1907 | Persian, Afghan and Tibetan disputes | Settled by negotiation; not a crisis and not a military alliance | German encirclement perception confirmed |
| Containment 2 - Bosnia Crisis 1908 | Annexation of an administered province | Russia retreats; Austria keeps Bosnia | Permanent Serbian hostility; Russian army and railway rebuilding; Russian resolve never to retreat again |
| Containment 3 - Agadir Crisis 1911 | German compensation claim in Morocco | Panther withdrawn; French protectorate recognised for two strips of the French Congo | German opinion intensely anti-British; Britain ahead eight to four in capital ships |
| Containment 4 - First Balkan War 1912 | Partition of remaining Ottoman Europe | London conference convened by Grey with the German government; independent Albania denies Serbia an outlet | Serbia enlarged and frustrated; Austrian containment policy visible |
| Containment 5 - Second Balkan War 1913 | Macedonia and the spoils | Bulgaria defeated; Treaty of Bucharest; Germany restrains Austria-Hungary | Serbia stronger; Vienna determined; Berlin believes Britain may be detached |
| **Sixth case - July Crisis 1914** | The assassination and the Serbian question | **General war** | — |

⚠️ **Reading instruction:** the right-hand column is the argument. Five containments, each purchased with an adverse structural output, and a sixth case in which the accumulated outputs - a permanent quarrel, a rearmed and committed Russia, a determined Vienna and a misinformed Berlin - all operated at once.

## Map 3 - The responsibility matrix

| Actor | ✅ Decision attributed | Level of responsibility | ✅ Recorded position for | ✅ Recorded position against |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Vienna | Used the assassination as the excuse for war; 48-hour ultimatum; rupture on partial acceptance; declaration of 28 July | Immediate, for the local war | Blamed as first aggressor; the recorded characterisation of "excuse" | Expected localisation; Williamson holds no prior decision existed |
| Berlin | Unconditional assurance; refusal of the partial option; declarations of 1 and 3 August; entry into Belgium | Immediate, for the general war | Fischer; Hamilton and Herwig; Rohl on Wilhelm II; the 1913 comparison | Koch on the December 1912 evidence; the recorded deterrence reading; Williamson on Vienna's autonomy |
| St Petersburg | Mobilisation of 29-30 July; the February 1914 promise | Immediate, for the escalation trigger | Blamed as first to order full mobilisation; Mulligan's case for at least equal responsibility | Mobilisation did not necessarily mean war; Vienna ought to have foreseen Russian involvement |
| London | Ambiguity before 31 July; entry on 4 August | Contributory, by omission | The recorded position that clearer signalling might have restricted the war | The operational reply that a 31 July announcement would have made no difference |
| Paris | Alignment with Russia | Not in the primary tier | — | Attacked on 3 August without a specific German quarrel |
| Belgrade | The South Slav programme after 1903 | Cause of the local quarrel, not of the general war | Russian backing probably made Serbian policy more reckless | Accepted most of the ultimatum's demands |
| The system | Two armed camps; arms competition; indivisible plans; falling margin | Systemic, shared | Kennan; the record of rising crisis frequency | Five containments; nothing binding in the alliances |

✅ **Recorded conclusion:** Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share the responsibility for the outbreak of war in 1914.

## Map 5 - Balance of power, scored by layer

| Layer | Does "preservation of balance of power" explain it? | ✅ Decisive evidence |
|---|---|---|
| The bloc structure | **Yes** | 1882, 1894, 1904, 1907; the encirclement complaint |
| The naval competition | **Yes** | A contest over a ratio; eight to four at the end of 1911 |
| Britain's entry | **Yes** | A hostile great power on the Channel coast; the 1839 guarantee |
| Austrian motive | **No** | Internal survival of a multi-national empire |
| Serbian motive | **No** | Creation of a South Slav state |
| French grievance | **No** | Recovery of Alsace-Lorraine, lost in 1871 |
| German timing | **No** | Preventive war seeks to change a future balance |
| The operational plan | **No** | A railway timetable is not a strategic purpose |
| July contingency | **No** | Identical structures produced containment in 1908 and 1911 |
| Domestic pressures | **No** | Balance-of-power theory treats states as unitary |
| The war as fought | **No** | Aims exceeded any restoration of the 1914 equilibrium |

⚠️ **Verdict shape:** three yes, eight no; the three yes rows are the system and the British case, and the eight no rows are the motives and the mechanism. A necessary part of the explanation and not the essential one.

## Map 6 - The two escalations, side by side

| | Local to general | European to world |
|---|---|---|
| Dates | 28 July to 6 August 1914 | Latent from 1902; progressive after August 1914 |
| Required a new decision? | ⚠️ Yes, at six identifiable points | ⚠️ No; it followed from what the belligerents already were |
| Channels | Alignment; prestige and standing; operational planning; legal obligation | Extra-European alliance; imperial possession; the maritime economy |
| ✅ Key anchors | The unconditional assurance; the February 1914 promise; no German quarrel with France; the 1839 guarantee | The Anglo-Japanese alliance of 1902; the post-1880 partition; about half the world's merchant tonnage; the Dardanelles |
| Owner | This topic | This topic establishes it; the theatres belong to the next owner |

## Map 7 - Comparison of the four great-power positions in July 1914

| | Austria-Hungary | Germany | Russia | Britain |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Threat perceived | Internal dissolution of a dynastic state | Encirclement and a closing military window | A second humiliation; closure of the Straits | A hostile great power on the Channel coast |
| Time horizon | Indefinite | Short and dated: before the end of 1914 | Medium; rebuilding since 1908 | Immediate, once Belgium was entered |
| Objective in the crisis | Curb Serbia; localise the war | Support Vienna; deter Russia or defeat it early | Support Serbia; preserve standing and the trade route | Uphold the 1839 guarantee; prevent a Channel threat |
| Constitutional decider | An autocratic emperor and his ministers | Kaiser and Chancellor, with a general staff holding technical authority | An autocratic Tsar and his ministers | A cabinet answerable to a parliament with an important say |
| Was a treaty obligation engaged? | Alliance relationship, but ⚠️ no automatic obligation | ⚠️ None; the assurance was freely given | ⚠️ None; a political promise of February 1914 | ✅ The 1839 guarantee of Belgian neutrality |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## The three questions and the trigger discipline

- ✅ The topic contains three questions: why Austria-Hungary went to war with Serbia; why that became a European war; why a European war became a world war.
- ✅ The assassination is the immediate cause of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia, not of the First World War.
- ✅ The Austro-Serb quarrel explains the outbreak of the war but not why it became a world war.
- ✅ The quarrel had become increasingly explosive since 1908; the Austrians seized on the assassination as the excuse for a preventive war with Serbia; they probably hoped the war would remain localised, like the Balkan Wars.
- ⚠️ Five kinds of cause: background condition, structural cause, precipitant, decision, mechanism. They are not items of one list.
- ✅ Syllabus: General Studies Paper I, the "world wars" sub-clause; no separate Prelims clause; this topic ends when general war begins.

## The world of 1914

- ✅ Europe still dominated the rest of the world; most decisions shaping the world were taken in European capitals.
- ✅ Germany was the leading European power militarily and economically, having overtaken Britain in pig-iron and steel though not quite in coal; France, Belgium, Italy and Austria-Hungary were well behind.
- ✅ Russian industry expanded rapidly from so backward a base that it could not seriously challenge Germany or Britain.
- ✅ In 1914 the USA produced more coal, pig-iron and steel than either Germany or Britain and ranked as a world power.
- ✅ Japan had modernised rapidly and was a power to be reckoned with after defeating Russia in 1904-05.
- ✅ Regime types: the USA, Britain and France had parliaments with an important say; Germany had a Reichstag but real power lay with the Chancellor and Kaiser; Italy's franchise was limited to the wealthy; Japan's franchise was restricted with power in the emperor and privy council; Russia and Austria-Hungary were autocracies whose parliaments could only advise.
- ⚠️ The regime table explains how few people could decide, supplies the domestic-pressure mechanism, and disciplines the language of blame.
- ✅ Closing consequence: Germany was soon displaced from her mastery of Europe, and Europe never quite regained its dominant position in the world.

## Empire after 1880, and its limited causal weight

- ✅ Imperialism: the building up of an empire by seizing territory overseas; a great burst after 1880.
- ✅ Most of Africa was taken over in the Scramble, mainly for new markets and raw materials; European powers, the USA and Japan forced concessions on China; the Chinese overthrew the Manchu dynasty and set up a republic in 1911.
- ✅ German Weltpolitik produced some Pacific islands and African territory - small in comparison with others and not very rewarding economically - and a complaint of encirclement by Britain, France and Russia.
- ✅ Colonial rivalry as a direct cause is recorded as not convincing: disputes had always been settled without war, and in early July 1914 Anglo-German relations were good, an agreement favourable to Germany having just been reached over a possible partition of Portuguese colonies in Africa.
- ✅ The exception: one side effect of colonial rivalry did cause dangerous friction - naval rivalry.
- ✅ The economic interpretation exists, favoured by Marxist historians, with Britain still owning about half the world's merchant tonnage; the recorded rebuttal is a leading German industrialist's remark of 1913 that three or four more years of peace would make Germany the unchallenged economic master of Europe.

## The alliance order and its real functions

- ✅ 1882 Triple Alliance; 1894 Franco-Russian alliance; 1902 Anglo-Japanese alliance; 1904 Entente Cordiale; 1907 Anglo-Russian agreement.
- ✅ Triple Alliance: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy. Triple Entente: Britain, France, Russia.
- ✅ Friction between the armed camps brought Europe to the verge of war several times since 1900.
- ✅ The 1907 instrument settled Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet and was not a military alliance.
- ✅ Kennan: once the 1894 alliance was signed the fate of Europe was sealed; states could not withdraw without humiliation and war seemed the only way to save face.
- ✅ Counter-evidence: no French help to Russia in 1904-05; no French support over Bosnia; Austrian indifference to Germany's Moroccan defeats; German restraint of Austria-Hungary in the Second Balkan War; Italy's entry against Germany in 1915. No power declared war because of one of these treaties.
- ⚠️ Four real functions: signalling, emboldening, restraining, raising the cost of retreat.
- ⚠️ Answer line: the alliances explain the shape of the war better than its occurrence.

## Arms competition on two clocks

- ✅ Mahan's doctrine influenced the German government; Tirpitz's Navy Law of 1897 began the programme.
- ✅ The British Dreadnought of 1906 made all other battleships obsolete, allowing Germany to build on equal terms in the new class.
- ✅ End of 1911: Britain 8 Dreadnought-type battleships to Germany's 4.
- ✅ The naval race was the main bone of contention right up to 1914; but early in 1913 Germany reduced naval spending to strengthen the army, and Churchill's assessment for spring and summer 1914 was that naval rivalry had ceased to be a cause of friction because Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships.
- ✅ After 1908 Russia undertook a massive military build-up, army modernisation and railway improvement for faster mobilisation, funded by huge French government loans, while German army expansion was hampered by the Reichstag's refusal to sanction the necessary tax increases.
- ✅ Von Jagow reported Moltke's early-1914 conclusion: no alternative but preventive war, before the end of 1914, because afterwards Russia would be too strong.
- ⚠️ Formulation: arms competition supplied a deadline, not an appetite; stock of mistrust high, flow of provocation stopped.

## War plans and the operational constraint

- ✅ Mobilisation is not war: Russian mobilisation did not necessarily mean war, since troops could be halted at the frontiers.
- ✅ The plan known as the Schlieffen Plan was drawn up by Count von Schlieffen in 1905-06; Ritter called it extremely risky and inflexible and the start of disaster for Germany and Europe.
- ✅ Its assumptions: France would automatically join Russia; the bulk of German forces would go by train to the Belgian frontier and through Belgium; France would be knocked out in six weeks; forces would then switch east against a slow Russian mobilisation; success depended on the rapid capture of Liege.
- ✅ Taylor: precise railway timetables accelerated events and reduced negotiating time almost to nil.
- ✅ 30 July: the Kaiser and Bethmann urged Vienna to negotiate; Wilhelm proposed partial mobilisation against Russia only; Moltke refused - no time to change the railway timetables. It looks as though the generals had taken control from the politicians, and a British announcement on 31 July would have made no difference.
- ✅ Zuber, 2002: at least five alternatives after 1900; an eastern-transfer option exercised in 1905; Schlieffen never committed to one plan; the rigid version was constructed after the war to explain defeat.
- ✅ Required formulation: German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium.

## Nationalism and the Balkan state system

- ✅ Nationalism: the desire to free your nation from control by people of another nationality; Serbian nationalism was probably the most dangerous cause of friction.
- ✅ Treaty of Berlin, 1878: Austria-Hungary was allowed to occupy Bosnia, which Serb nationalists thought should be part of a Greater Serbia; Bosnia remained officially part of Turkey while Austria-Hungary administered it.
- ✅ Serbian policy was pro-Austrian under Milan from 1882 and Alexander from 1893; in 1903 Alexander was murdered by army officers who installed Peter Karageorgevic, and policy switched to a pro-Russian line with an open ambition to unite all Serbs and Croats in a large South Slav kingdom.
- ✅ Habsburg nationalities: Germans, Hungarians, Magyars, Czechs, Slovaks, Italians, Poles, Romanians, Ruthenians, Slovenes, Serbs and Croats. If the Serbs and Croats left the fold, many others would demand independence and the empire would break up.
- ✅ Some Austrians favoured a preventive war to destroy Serbia before she became strong enough to provoke that break-up; the Austrians also resented Russian support for Serbia.
- ✅ The Russians were suspicious of Austrian ambitions in the Balkans and worried about German strength.
- ⚠️ Graded formulation: nationalism was dangerous in proportion to how far its object lay inside another great power's frontiers.

## The Eastern Question

- ✅ The Balkan League states - Serbia, Greece, Montenegro, Bulgaria - had all once been part of the Ottoman Empire; Turkey was regarded as the Sick Man of Europe.
- ✅ In 1908 a Young Turk government of army officers resented the Austrian presence in Bosnia and was determined to assert Turkish control, which gave the Austrians the chance to get in first.
- ✅ In 1912 the League captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe.
- ✅ Russia feared that Bulgaria and Turkey under German influence would let Germany and Austria control the Dardanelles, the outlet from the Black Sea and the main Russian trade route, which could be strangled - and this happened to some extent during the war.
- ✅ Russia also feared losing prestige as leader of the Slavs, and saw the matter as a struggle for survival once Austria declared war on Serbia.
- ✅ February 1914: the Tsar promised to help the Serbs against an Austro-Hungarian attack and signed a naval agreement with Britain to safeguard Mediterranean access.

## Domestic politics and the economic setting

- ✅ 1912 elections: the SPD won over a third of Reichstag seats and became the largest single party.
- ✅ January 1914: the Reichstag passed a vote of no confidence in Bethmann-Hollweg; he remained in office because the Kaiser had the final say; a major clash was coming.
- ✅ Fischer's domestic reading: a victorious war would keep minds off political problems, allow suppression of the SPD and keep power with the Kaiser and aristocracy; evidence from von Muller's diary on the "war council" of 8 December 1912, where Moltke said war was unavoidable and the sooner the better; Koch objected that nothing came of it.
- ✅ Russia: the 1905 defeat sparked a revolution that severely weakened the government; until 1911 the desire for peace was paramount; Stolypin, who favoured peace, was assassinated in 1911 and the government began to succumb to growing jingoistic opinion demanding prestige.
- ✅ Durnovo warned shortly before the war that a long war would strain the country severely and could lead to the collapse of the tsarist regime.
- ⚠️ Germany's two clocks ran opposite ways: the economic clock said wait, the military clock said act before the end of 1914.

## The crises in sequence

- ✅ **1905-06 Morocco:** a German test of the Entente; a conference at Algeciras in January 1906; Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain supported the French demand to control the Moroccan bank and police; a serious German diplomatic defeat, soon followed by Anglo-French military conversations.
- ✅ **1907 Anglo-Russian agreement:** made possible by Russia's defeat by Japan, the January 1905 revolution, Russian desire for British investment and the Tsar's October 1905 concessions; settled Persia, Afghanistan and Tibet; read by Germany as confirmation of encirclement.
- ✅ **1908 Bosnia:** Young Turk pressure; formal annexation; a deliberate blow at Serbia; about three million Serbs in a mixed population of Serbs, Croats and Muslims; Serbia appealed to Russia; Russia called for a conference; Germany backed Austria; France drew back; Britain protested only; Russia dared not risk war. Austria kept Bosnia - a triumph for the Austro-German alliance, with two costs: permanent Serbian hostility, and Russian rearmament and railway improvement.
- ✅ **1911 Agadir:** French troops occupied Fez; Germany sent the gunboat Panther to Agadir seeking compensation, perhaps the French Congo; Britain feared a naval base threatening its trade routes; Lloyd George warned Germany at the Mansion House; the settlement gave Germany two strips of the French Congo for recognition of the French protectorate; German opinion turned intensely anti-British.
- ✅ **1912 First Balkan War:** the League attacked Turkey and captured most of the remaining Turkish territory in Europe; Grey, with the German government, arranged a peace conference in London; Serbia wanted Albania for an outlet to the sea, and Austria-Hungary, with German and British support, insisted on an independent Albania to prevent Serbia becoming more powerful.
- ✅ **1913 Second Balkan War:** Bulgaria, dissatisfied over Macedonia, attacked Serbia; Greece, Romania and Turkey rallied to Serbia; Bulgaria was defeated and forfeited most of its gains by the Treaty of Bucharest. Three consequences: Serbia strengthened and determined to agitate among Serbs and Croats inside Austria-Hungary; the Austrians determined to end Serbia's ambitions; and the Germans reading Grey's cooperation as a sign that Britain might be detached from France and Russia.
- ✅ Germany restrained Austria-Hungary from attacking Serbia during the Second Balkan War.

## Sarajevo and attribution

- ✅ 28 June 1914 at Sarajevo, the capital of Bosnia: Archduke Franz Ferdinand, nephew and heir to Emperor Franz Josef, and his wife were shot dead on an official visit by Gavrilo Princip.
- ✅ **Fact:** an earlier bomb attempt that morning failed; the conspirators were a locally organised group of young Bosnians associated with the Young Bosnia movement; weapons and assistance came from the secret Serbian organisation Union or Death, known as the Black Hand, whose leader held a senior post in Serbian military intelligence.
- ✅ The Austrians blamed the Serb government and sent a harsh ultimatum.
- ⚠️ Five attribution levels: the individual, the conspiracy and the organisation are secure; the Serbian government's responsibility is recorded only as an Austrian allegation; collective attribution to a people is prohibited.
- 📰 Single current linkage: the Museum of Sarajevo's exhibition "The Sarajevo Assassination - 112 Years Later", opening 27 June 2026 as part of the official commemoration, prepared with the International Burch University and the Sarajevo VRX Museum; the institution describes the event as the immediate catalyst for the outbreak of the war.

## The July Crisis

- ✅ Germany restrained Austria-Hungary in 1913 and in 1914 encouraged it; the Kaiser's telegram urged an attack on Serbia and promised help without any conditions attached - a blank cheque. The important question is why German policy changed.
- ✅ **Fact:** the assurance was given in Berlin on 5-6 July 1914; the ultimatum was delivered on 23 July with a 48-hour limit; Serbia replied on 25 July; Austria-Hungary broke off relations the same day.
- ✅ Serbia accepted most of the demands; the Austrians, with a promise of German support, were determined to use the incident as an excuse for war.
- ✅ 28 July: Austria-Hungary declared war on Serbia, probably hoping the war would remain localised.
- ✅ 29 July: Russia ordered mobilisation, anxious not to let the Serbs down again. ✅ **Fact:** partial on 29 July, general on 30 July; the source dates general mobilisation to 29 July. The difference is recorded, not resolved.
- ✅ 30 July: the Kaiser and Bethmann urged Vienna to negotiate; Wilhelm proposed partial mobilisation; Moltke refused.
- ✅ 31 July: Germany demanded cancellation; Russia did not comply.
- ✅ 1 August: Germany declared war on Russia. ✅ 3 August: Germany declared war on France, having no specific quarrel with France.
- ✅ German troops entered Belgium; the narrative gives 4 August and the prologue implies 3 August; the violation was complete by 4 August.
- ✅ Britain, which in 1839 had promised to defend Belgian neutrality, demanded withdrawal and entered on 4 August. ✅ 6 August: Austria-Hungary declared war on Russia; other countries joined later.
- ✅ First major engagement: German columns at Liege from the night of 5 August; forts resisted; heavy howitzers fired shells from 12 000 feet; the first fort surrendered on 13 August and Liege fell three days later.

## Causation, historiography and contingency

- ✅ Five layers: immediate, structural, regional, great-power, operational, each with a stated exam use.
- ⚠️ Necessity test: naval and colonial rivalry fail; the blocs, the quarrel, the assurance, the mobilisation and the plan pass as necessary but not sufficient; the assassination's necessity is contested.
- ✅ Four blame positions: Austria-Hungary as first aggressor; Russia as first to order full mobilisation; Germany for supporting Austria; Britain for insufficient clarity.
- ✅ Three answers to the German question: Fischer's deliberate provocation; preventive timing; miscalculated deterrence.
- ✅ Ritter, Taylor and Zuber on the plans; Turner on the tragedy of miscalculation; Bethmann's "Oh - if I only knew!"; Hamilton and Herwig on ultimate German responsibility for a war of survival; Afflerbach and Stevenson on a war not inevitable though possible; Williamson on Vienna's autonomy and the decisive moment; Rohl on Wilhelm II; Mulligan on Russian responsibility.
- ✅ Recorded conclusion: Germany, Russia and Austria-Hungary must share the responsibility.
- ✅ Five containments under identical structures - 1905-06, 1908, 1911, 1912, 1913 - against one war in 1914.
- ⚠️ Verdict: highly likely but not inevitable; contingency establishes responsibility rather than diminishing it.
- ⚠️ Six points at which a different choice was available; only one was refused on technical grounds.

## Globalisation and the handover

- ⚠️ Four transmissions from local to general: alignment, prestige, operational planning, legal obligation.
- ⚠️ Three routes from European to world war: the Anglo-Japanese alliance of 1902; imperial possession after the post-1880 partition; a maritime economy in which Britain owned about half the world's merchant tonnage and Russia's trade ran through the Dardanelles.
- ✅ Other countries joined later; the war was global in reach before it was global in theatre.
- ✅ One permitted sentence on empire: the war globalised European conflict and pulled colonies into a crisis whose political after-effects were felt far beyond Europe.
- ✅ Handover: the war's course, total-war character, imperial collapses and the settlements of 1919-23 belong to the First World War and aftermath owner.

## Answer architecture in one page

- ⚠️ Four questions before writing: which of the three topics; what the directive demands; which layers; what the verdict sentence is.
- ✅ Six demand types with their prohibited answers.
- ⚠️ Three theses: balance of power as structure not purpose; regional origin with systemic generalisation; highly likely but not inevitable.
- ✅ Mark scaling: 10 marks - thesis, two layers, escalation, verdict; 15 - concession, three limits, chain, graded verdict; 20 - plus war aims, the global dimension and inevitability.
- ✅ Three verdict scaffolds, each with concession, limit and a grading word.
- ✅ Ten factual-risk controls: alliance obligation; Britain and France; the Schlieffen blueprint; figures; historians' positions and quotation; crisis dates; the "planned for years" claim; the Russian mobilisation staging; the Belgian entry date; the Sarajevo attribution.
- ⚠️ Seven boundary handovers, one clause each.
- ⚠️ Closing qualification: confined to Britain's entry, the balance-of-power reading is substantially stronger.
