## Book evidence and its controls

1. `books\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH  ##.pdf`, read through the locally held full-text extraction at `attempts\_gs_source_text\WORLD_HISTORY_NORMAN_LOWE_ENGLISH_pdf.txt` - the folder's designated world-history spine and the **one core OCR history source** used for this topic under the bounded History research rule. Its opening chapter on the world in 1914 and the outbreak of the First World War is the chapter both canonical owners cite, and it was read in full for this session. It supplied: the prologue on the Liege operation; the account of European dominance and the comparative industrial position of Germany, Britain, France, Belgium, Italy, Austria-Hungary, Russia, the United States and Japan; the three-way classification of political systems with its exact constitutional descriptions; the definition of imperialism and the record of the post-1880 expansion, the Scramble for Africa, the Chinese concessions and the republic of 1911; the two alliance systems with their membership, the 1902 Anglo-Japanese alliance and the armed-camps formulation; the six recorded causes of friction, including the naval rivalry, Alsace-Lorraine, the encirclement complaint and Weltpolitik, Russian suspicion of Austrian ambitions, and the full treatment of Serbian nationalism with the 1878 Treaty of Berlin, the policies of Milan and Alexander, the 1903 murder and dynastic change, the South Slav programme, the Habsburg nationality list, the break-up logic and the preventive-war current; the complete time chart of main events; the Moroccan Crisis of 1905-06 with the Entente bargain, the Algeciras conference of January 1906 and its outcome; the British agreement with Russia of 1907 with its background and its non-military character; the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 with the Young Turks, the annexation, the population composition, the failed appeal and the two recorded consequences; the Agadir Crisis of 1911 with Fez, the Panther, the Mansion House warning, the Congo exchange and the Dreadnought counts; the First and Second Balkan Wars with the League, the London conference, the Albanian question, the Treaty of Bucharest and the three recorded consequences; the assassination of 28 June 1914 with its victims, its perpetrator, the ultimatum, the Serbian acceptance of most demands and the escalation to 6 August; and the whole assessment section on what caused the war and who was to blame, including the four blame positions, the alliance explanation with Kennan and the five counter-instances, colonial rivalry with the early-July 1914 agreement, the naval race with Mahan, Tirpitz, the Dreadnought, the 1913 reduction and Churchill's assessment, economic rivalry with the Marxist reading and the 1913 industrialist's remark, Russian backing for Serbia with the Dardanelles and Durnovo, German backing with the blank cheque and the three interpretations including Fischer, the Muller diary, the December 1912 meeting, Koch and the reception politics, the preventive-timing reading with von Jagow and Moltke, the deterrence reading, the mobilisation plans with Ritter, Taylor, the 30-31 July sequence and Zuber, the tragedy of miscalculation with Turner and Bethmann's remark, and the closing treatment of Hamilton and Herwig, Afflerbach and Stevenson, Williamson, Rohl and Mulligan with the final shared-responsibility conclusion.
2. **Period control on the book.** Only the opening chapter was used. Nothing from the book's later chapters on the war's course, the peace settlements, the interwar period or any later topic appears anywhere in this session, because all of it belongs to other owners.
3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook, monograph or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded below as open uncertainty rather than filled from outside.
4. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above or from the canonical owners. **Six distinct participant quotations** appear in the file, some of them repeated in revision and remediation, and each is reproduced exactly as the source records it: Lloyd George's Mansion House warning of 1911; Churchill's assessment that Britain could not be overtaken in capital ships; Moltke's remark at the meeting of 8 December 1912; Moltke's phrase on the role he claimed for Germany, reported by Herwig; Bethmann-Hollweg's reply on how it all began; and an unnamed leading German industrialist's remark of 1913 on three or four more years of peace. Three further quoted phrases are the source's own words rather than any person's speech - its description of the 1878 arrangement as a rather confusing decision, the label "the Sick Man of Europe" applied to the Ottoman state, and the "preventive war" that some Austrians called for - and they are marked as recorded phrasing wherever they appear. **No historian is quoted verbatim anywhere**, in accordance with the recorded factual-risk control: Mulligan's assessment of the effect of Russian policy on German thinking, which the source renders as a quotation, is paraphrased in Lesson 17 precisely so that the learner-facing rule against quoting historians is not contradicted by this session's own practice. The two owners' analytical phrasings are likewise used in substance rather than as quotation.
5. **Internal-inconsistency control.** Two disagreements inside the evidence base are displayed rather than resolved: the date of the Russian general mobilisation, which the book's narrative and time chart both give as 29 July while the standard chronology distinguishes a partial order on 29 July from a general order on 30 July; and the date of the German entry into Belgium, which the book's causes section gives as 4 August while its own prologue implies 3 August. Both are flagged in Lesson 15, recorded in the factual-risk controls of Lesson 20 and listed in the remediation error map.

## The single bounded material-fact verification pass

⚠️ **Why the pass was necessary.** The two owners and the core book supply the crisis sequence, the alliance chronology, the arms competition, the Balkan structure, the historiography and the complete answer architecture. They do not supply the exact dates of three July instruments, the names of the Sarajevo conspirators or their organisations, or the instrument by which Belgian neutrality was guaranteed in 1839 - all of which are standard essentials without which the teaching would have to use vague formulations where the standard account has exact names and dates. The instruction governing this generation is that such essentials be supplied and labelled rather than suppressed. One bounded pass was therefore run, confined to those essentials, and every item it produced is labelled `Fact (material-fact pass)` in the lesson that uses it.

| Cluster verified | Items carried |
|---|---|
| The Sarajevo conspiracy | An earlier bomb attempt on the morning of 28 June 1914; a locally organised group of young Bosnian conspirators associated with the Young Bosnia movement; assistance, including weapons, supplied by the secret Serbian organisation formally called Union or Death and commonly known as the Black Hand, whose leader held a senior position in Serbian military intelligence |
| The German assurance | Given in Berlin on 5-6 July 1914, when an envoy sent from Vienna carried the question to the German capital and received the unconditional answer from the Kaiser and the Chancellor |
| The ultimatum sequence | Ultimatum delivered 23 July 1914 with a 48-hour limit expiring 25 July; Serbian reply 25 July accepting most but not all demands; Austro-Hungarian rupture of diplomatic relations the same day |
| The mobilisation staging | Russian partial mobilisation 29 July; Russian general mobilisation 30 July; German ultimatum to Russia 31 July; German ultimatum to Belgium 2 August; German entry into Luxembourg 2 August; invasion of Belgium 3-4 August; British declaration 4 August |
| The Belgian guarantee | The Treaty of London of 1839, to which the major European powers including Britain were parties, guaranteeing Belgian independence and neutrality |

⚠️ **Control on the pass.** No item from the pass overrides an owner or the core book. Where a held source and the pass address the same point, the held source's formulation is used and the pass supplies only the missing precision - for example, the book's "harsh ultimatum" is retained and the pass adds the date and the time limit. Where the pass and a held source conflict, as on the staging of the Russian mobilisation, both are displayed and neither is suppressed. The pass produced no quantity of any kind, and no number from it appears anywhere in this session. It was run once and is not reopened.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from the excluded derived or generated package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion; no learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning sessions and solved workbooks and the complete-topic-package file that duplicates this topic.
- No workbook operation of any kind was performed: no objective corpus was constructed, copied, relocated or migrated, and no solved-practice workbook was created. This session is in concept-check mode, with exactly one concept check, one model answer and one misconception note in each of its twenty lessons.
- No model answer is supplied anywhere for the verified 2024 question. Its demand, decoding, route and lesson mapping are given; the solved answer belongs to the learner's separate workbook.
- No casualty figure appears anywhere in this session - not for the Balkan Wars, the Liege operation or any other engagement. Every standard account records heavy mortality in the war that followed; that qualitative fact belongs to the next owner and no number is given, estimated or implied here.
- Quantitative historical evidence in this session is confined to five sourced items: the capital-ship counts of eight to four at the end of 1911; the recorded figure of about three million Serbs in Bosnia's mixed population; the German Socialist Party's recorded share of over a third of Reichstag seats after the 1912 elections; the recorded statement that Britain still owned about half the world's tonnage of merchant ships in 1914; and the recorded height of 12 000 feet from which howitzer shells fell at Liege. No army size, mobilised strength, casualty figure, military-spending series, industrial output figure, trade figure or population total appears anywhere. This control governs quantitative evidence about military and economic capacity, human loss and wealth; it does not extend to ordinary numerals of date, interval, sequence, enumeration or examination format - years, the forty-eight-hour ultimatum limit, the six-week planning assumption, the count of scholarly alternatives recorded in a documentary challenge, marks, word ceilings and lesson numbers - all of which are used freely and are not historical quantities.
- Attribution is disciplined throughout: responsibility is stated about governments, cabinets, general staffs, named organisations and named individuals, and never about nations or peoples. The core account's description of the assassin as a Serb terrorist is reported as the source's characterisation and is not adopted as this session's own classification; the factual statement made instead is that Gavrilo Princip shot and killed the Archduke and his wife.
- No claim is made anywhere about what the Serbian government knew, authorised or did in relation to the Sarajevo plot. The Austrian allegation is recorded as an allegation, and the internal politics of Serbia between 1903 and 1914 are recorded as unknown to this evidence base.
- No claim is made anywhere about Archduke Franz Ferdinand's own political views, which are not recorded in this evidence base.
- Contested points are displayed rather than resolved: the status of the Schlieffen Plan as a single document; whether German backing was decisive for the Austrian choice; whether the assassination was a necessary occasion; whether clearer British signalling would have altered the outcome; the staging of the Russian mobilisation; and the date of the German entry into Belgium.
- The Concert of Europe, German and Italian unification, the Franco-Prussian War, the partition of Africa, the military course of the war, the settlements of 1919-23, the Russian Revolution and India's wartime politics are all routed to their owners; each is named where it arises and none is narrated here.
- Remaining open uncertainty, recorded rather than filled: the extent and commitments of the Anglo-French military conversations after 1906; the extent and instruments of German influence in Bulgaria and Turkey, recorded here only as a Russian perception; the internal Russian deliberations of 1908 and of July 1914; the relationship between the Serbian civil government, the dynasty and the officer networks; the content and outcome of the Austrian investigation after 28 June; whether the German political leadership shared the general staff's deadline; the technical feasibility of an alternative German deployment in the last days of July; the distribution of public opinion in any capital other than Russia after 1911; the unresolved tension inside A. J. P. Taylor's own position, since the evidence base records both his argument that railway timetables were decisive and his later questioning of rigid blueprint claims, without recording how he reconciled them; and whether any of the six identified alternative choices would in fact have prevented a general war.
